- **The Complete Investor** — `Charlie Munger - The complete investor.txt` — 166 pages — blocks: 1–100, 101–166

Entries for Talks Four and Five were already in the file from Damn Right! The .txt is garbled in places: the end of Talk Five (p. 342), the middle of Talk Seven (pp. 373–375) and the closing parable of Talk Eight (pp. 398–399) are lower-case run-ons with misread words; entries quote only the legible sentences, or quote the passage “as printed” and say so. The Almanack's own printed page numbers that appear in the .txt (“298,” “309,” “334,” “351,” “379”) are book pages, not `[PDF PAGE n]` markers.

## 1. DECISIONS

- **AUTHOR** — *Army branch.* Situation: enlisted a few days after his 19th birthday, after six years of ROTC. / Did: stayed out of the infantry, joined the Army Air Corps. / Reason (author's, not his): “Munger was bored with marching.” (Damn Right!, p. 61)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Not surgery, not mathematics as a calling.* Situation: Army aptitude tests; watching his Caltech thermodynamics professor Homer Joe Stewart. / Did: went into law. / Reason, HIM: “That confirmed what I already knew. My spatial talents were not up to my general level of talents.” and “If I'd have gone into surgery when I was young, I wouldn't have been an outstanding surgeon.” Reason, AUTHOR: “To go into a calling where he would not be exceptional was not in Charlie's thinking.” (Damn Right!, p. 64)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Getting into Harvard Law with no degree.* Situation: applied before his 1946 discharge; the dean objected. / Did: went to Roscoe Pound. HIM: “I was admitted over the objection of Dean Warren Abner Seavy through the intervention of family friend Roscoe Pound,” AUTHOR (p. 64): warned the Dean would say finish college first, “Munger replied “We'll see.”” (p. 64) Also in, HIM (p. 354, Appendix D): “I was admitted over the objection of Warren Abner Seavey through intervention of family friend Roscoe Pound.” (Damn Right!, pp. 63–64) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 341 (Talk Five): “I was admitted over the objection of Warren Abner Seavey through the intervention of family friend Roscoe Pound.”
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED** — *Los Angeles, not Omaha or Boston.* Situation: graduated Harvard Law 1948. / Did: went to California. AUTHOR (p. 67): “Al Munger advised against it.” (returning to Omaha). HIM (p. 67): “Boston in 1948 was terribly interbred-intermarried. It was a hard town in which to get ahead. In Los Angeles, I would go ahead faster.” HIM (p. 68), on whether his wife's Southern California home mattered: “I don't remember discussing it with her,” REPORTED (p. 67): his father, visiting Los Angeles after World War I, “declared “There's no future in this town.”” (Damn Right!, pp. 66–68)
- **AUTHOR** — *Early practice.* Situation: junior partner on a case; clients due the next day. / Did: “Charlie thought about the case and decided that there were only three reasonable ways to resolve the issue.” The clients chose one of the three; “Charlie told the group that if they brought in a stenographer, he could do the letter then and there and save the clients the trouble of returning the next day.” Result: the clients asked for him on later business. No quote from him. (Damn Right!, pp. 86–87)
- **AUTHOR** — *Admitted error, young lawyer.* Situation: he drafted legislation giving property-tax exemption to university buildings under construction. / What happened: “Munger was embarrassed to realize that it covered the buildings, but failed to mention the land under the buildings.” Another partner got it corrected. (Damn Right!, p. 87)
- **HIM** — *Standing up to Roy Garrett over billing.* Situation: Garrett, the leading partner, “chewed me out for running up $20,000 of billable time, with no collection” on an account he had assigned. / Did: answered at once. / Quote: “I replied, ‘Roy, you have no right to talk to me this way until the first time I fail you in billing and collection’ and we left it at that. A couple of weeks later I collected $50,000. This sort of being right got mixed reactions from Roy.” (Damn Right!, p. 88)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *The boron mine, wanted and not had.* Situation: AUTHOR: “Even things that were merely near at hand received close scrutiny, including an excellent mining property in California's Mojave Desert.” / Did: the text records no purchase. / Reason, HIM: “I would like to own that boron mine-boron is an element, the mine is in an open pit in a safe country. It has low costs and big reserves,” and “It would be a really nice mine to own, but it is already owned by someone who knows it's a very nice mine.” (Damn Right!, p. 88)
- **HIM** — *Which clients he wants to resemble.* Situation: he told his father, an Omaha lawyer, to take fewer bad clients. / Did: took Al's client Grant McFayden as the model. Quote: “I once complained that he [Al Munger] should have more clients like Grant McFayden and fewer like a certain other man,” “I can remember my father's mock horror when he explained how McFayden treated his customers right, his suppliers right, and his employees right.” “The lesson helped me prefer McFayden types as clients and McFayden behavior as the right example for myself.” (Damn Right!, pp. 88–89) Also in, HIM (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 273–274): the setting, as he tells it: “Omaha's Pioneer Ford dealer” and “a self-made Irishman who'd run away uneducated from a farm as a youth because his father beat him.” (p. 273) His father's answer, as he gives it (this version says “his problems” where the Damn Right! version says “his suppliers”): “Grant McFadyen treats his employees right, his customers right, and his problems right. And if he gets involved with a psychotic, he quickly walks over to where the psychotic is and works out an exit as fast as he can. Therefore, Grant McFadyen doesn't have enough remunerative law business to keep you in Coca-Cola. But Mr. X is a walking minefield of wonderful legal business.” (p. 274)
- **AUTHOR** — *Starting to invest.* “Munger gradually accumulated money from his legal practice and began investing in securities and joining friends and clients in business endeavors” (p. 89). Also REPORTED (Molly, p. 74): “He was always good at money. He invested in the stock market.” (Damn Right!, pp. 74, 89)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Transformer Engineers (Pasadena) — first ownership stake.* Situation: legal client, got along with them; hoped for more work. AUTHOR: “Charlie decided that he was being too shy. He shouldn't wait for the clients to call him.” He turned the car around, visited, got more work; “Eventually, he took an ownership position in the business, borrowing some of the necessary funds.” HIM, on the founder Ed Hoskins and his venture-capital backers: “We worked out a deal for him to buy them out, using large amounts of credit. It was an early leveraged buyout. It was a nonlegal solution to what looked like a legal problem.” (Damn Right!, p. 89)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Buying William Miller Instruments — admitted error.* HIM: “The worst trouble came from buying William Miller Instruments, Inc. That was not a good idea.” AUTHOR: the product was an oscillograph and “rapidly was made obsolete by more sophisticated magnetic tape technology.” (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Rescuing the transformer business, then leaving.* Situation: after the Korean War the business was bad. / Did: HIM: “we finally righted it by firing all the customers who wouldn't let it make money and downsizing to a much smaller company.” Result, HIM: “We damn near lost everything. We finally made it work out, but not fabulously. But we got a very respectable return on investment eventually.” AUTHOR: “Munger went into the partnership with Hoskins in the 1950s and got out of it in 1960 and 1961.” (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **HIM** — *Olive trees at Alhambra Village Green.* “We paid less than $100 each for them. Got them from an olive grove that was being torn out.” (Damn Right!, p. 93)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *1998 Berkshire meeting: his own real-estate record at Berkshire.* Quote: “Here's an area in which we have a virtually perfect record extending over many decades,” said Munger. “We've been demonstrably foolish in almost every operation having to do with real estate that we've ever touched. Every time that we had a surplus plant and didn't want to accept the bid of some developer that planned a development, we'd have been better off later if we'd accepted the bid and done something with the sales proceeds in a field where we had expertise.” AUTHOR then disputes the self-assessment for Munger personally: “it is misplaced modesty to say that Munger personally has a certified record of failure in real estate.” (Damn Right!, p. 95)
- **HIM** — *How the first real-estate deal began.* Quote: “I had a client-two, a father and son.” “The father had no use for the property-we were supposed to sell it off in probate.” (Otis Booth and his father; land across from Caltech.) (Damn Right!, p. 95)
- **HIM + REPORTED** — *Advising Otis Booth to keep the land, then putting his own money in.* HIM: “I said to Otis, build your own apartments.” “You should not allow those two houses forming the whole end of the block to go into other hands. You buy them. Tear them down, re-zone, build, and sell own-your-own apartment units.” REPORTED (Booth's words as Munger tells it): “if this is such a good idea, and you're so sure it will work, why don't you put up some of the money and join me. I won't do it without you.” HIM: “He shamed me into demonstrating the wisdom of my own advice,” (Damn Right!, p. 97)
- **HIM + REPORTED** — *Result of the Caltech condominiums.* HIM: “We gave our occupants more land and a better size and it turned out people really liked it. Moreover we had a good location,” “It worked out very well.” REPORTED (Booth): “It was slow, there was a recession we had to wait out,” and “very substantive profit - 400 percent. We put in $100,000, got back $500,000.” (Damn Right!, p. 98) Also in: AUTHOR, “The venture, building condominiums on land near Caltech, was a smashing success, and partners earned a handsome profit of $300,000 on a $100,000 investment.” (the figure differs from Booth's above) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 17)
- **AUTHOR** — *Next projects built one-story.* Situation: on the first project “the ground-floor apartments in the first project sold out quickly, but the upstairs units seemed to sell molasses slow.” / Did: “Munger stuck with the single-level floor plan on a third, fourth, and fifth project,” at a higher price. Also: “For years he took no money out, rather he invested in one project after another.” No quote from him. (Damn Right!, p. 98)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *The Alhambra deal he took without Booth.* AUTHOR: Booth stayed out “because he thought the conditions of the land ownership might cause problems. Charlie saw the risks, but decided he could make it work.” HIM, on how it began: “I had a client who came to me after I used to beat him at the poker table.” “He hired me to work on his legal problems, but I didn't like the way he was doing it. I said ‘I quit’ as his attorney. He turned the tables and said ‘put on paper what you would do.’ I said, ‘if you put up the ground lease, I'll do all the development work, financing, etc. and give you half the profit.’” AUTHOR: “Rather than share in the profits, the shopping center owner preferred to have cash up front, which turned out to be a mistake. Half the profits would have been a much better deal.” AUTHOR: the 442 units sold at about $20,000 each; “He and the builders did not cut corners on design or construction details.” (Damn Right!, p. 99)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED** — *Bringing Al Marshall into Alhambra.* AUTHOR: asked Marshall, unemployed petroleum engineer, to put up $15,000. HIM: “The business he was in was going to hell under him, and he had five children.” “I knew he was a great guy and he needed something else to do. I brought him in to do sales. I told him, ‘Build your own department.’ He'd never done it before, but he was naturally talented. He liked what he did. It was screamingly successful.” Earlier: AUTHOR, “Munger structured the deal in an ABC trust, which was a type of tax shelter that was legally correct at the time, but was so much abused that it has since been outlawed.” REPORTED (Marshall): “We only put up $1,000 each and we've each probably made a half a million out of it.” AUTHOR: “but Munger gave his to his children.” (Damn Right!, p. 100)
- **AUTHOR** — *Raising money for Buffett in California.* “Munger spread the word about Buffett's skills in California, bringing millions more dollars into his investment pool.” AUTHOR also: “Berkshire's early success was due to the acquisition of Blue Chip Stamps, See's Candy, and other California companies, most of which were discovered by Munger and his circle of West Coast investors.” (The reasoning is not given here; this records Munger's own actions, not Buffett's.) (Damn Right!, p. 25)
- **AUTHOR (eyewitness scene)** — *A phone call to Buffett at the lake.* The author hears only Munger's side; the subject is not named. Munger: “So it's the price that bothers you?” then “If you wait, I think you'll get your price.” (Damn Right!, p. 29)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Marshall)** — *Alhambra Village Green: lending on a 49-year ground lease.* Situation, AUTHOR: “Booth had opted out because the property was not owned, but rather came with a 49-year-lease from the city. Ordinarily lenders wouldn't issue loans for developments with less than a 50-year-lease, but that did not deter Munger.” / Reason, AUTHOR: “He knew that a local savings and loan, State Mutual, had recently raked in $60 million by increasing its passbook rate by one-half of 1 percent. The lender was racing to get the money out and earning interest in the form of loans.” Result, AUTHOR: “When Charlie and Al met with the loan officer to look at the property, Al said the lender all but asked if they wanted an even bigger loan than they had applied for.” (The text says only “Al”.) AUTHOR: the way Munger handled it convinced Marshall that Charlie “had the ability to think through a future event and come to a conclusion different from what others assumed it would be,” REPORTED (Marshall): “and I've hardly ever seen him wrong.” (Damn Right!, p. 101)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Stopping real estate after five projects.* Did: AUTHOR: “Charlie and Al Marshall developed one more project, but decided it would be their last.” Reason, AUTHOR: “Munger enjoyed development and construction, but it worried him that a successful builder was dependent on debt financing on an ever-increasing scale.” Reason, HIM: “I did a total of five projects, then stopped. I didn't like constantly borrowing more money. Also, it was an activity with many details, each crucial, difficult to handle as a full-time activity and extremely difficult as a part-time activity.” Result, HIM: “When it was over, I had $1.4 million as the result of my real estate involvement,” (in the .txt the AUTHOR's sentence “That was a lot of money at that time.” sits inside the same quotation marks; it is not his.) AUTHOR: “Munger walked away from the real estate phase of his career with enough money to finance his foray into the world of independent investing.” (Damn Right!, p. 102)
- **AUTHOR** — *His own houses.* “He had built his own home on Edgewood Drive shortly after he arrived in Los Angeles, then in 1960 he acquired a mansion on two large lots in Hancock Park. He demolished the large house and sold one lot at a substantial profit. On the remaining lot he built a house for his new family.” No reason or quote from him. (Damn Right!, p. 103)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Booth, Buffett)** — *Sea Meadow, Santa Barbara (22 acres Wesco's Mutual Savings got in a 1966 foreclosure).* Situation, HIM: “We have ‘given' a very large fraction of the value of our land to the County of Santa Barbara in exchange for permission to use it at all,” (price of permits, streets, sewage, archaeology). Did: developed it. Reason, HIM: “I developed it because I didn't want to let the zoning authorities rob me the way they wanted to. And now I know that if I had let them rob me, we would have had better financial results.” HIM, 1989 report to shareholders: “development of this property has been delayed over 14 years in the course of administration of land-use laws. - **REPORTED (Hills)** — *A tax-shelter offer from Hills.* Hills had a tax-shelter investment he liked. - **AUTHOR** — *Ending the partnership.* AUTHOR: “When dealing only with his own money, investment losses never bothered Munger much. (Damn Right!, p. 201)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Freddie Mac, 1988: act in scale.* Situation, AUTHOR (p. 209): “In 1988, Munger and Buffett moved Mutual Savings another step away from its traditional role as a thrift. After a three-hour discussion, the pair decided to beef up Mutual's small stake in the Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, commonly called Freddie Mac.” AUTHOR (p. 210): “The implicit federal backing for Freddie Mac gave Munger and Buffett the competitive edge that they like.” / Did, AUTHOR (p. 210): Wesco-through Mutual Savings “bought 28.8 million shares of Freddie Mac for $72 million at a time when Freddie Mac shares could be lawfully owned only by an S&L. It was the maximum investment in Freddie Mac then allowed by law.” / Result, AUTHOR: “It was an investment that built a castle wall around Wesco during the later collapse of the thrift industry.” Market value at the end of 1999 is printed as “$1.38 billion” (p. 210) and as “with a value of $1.9 billion” (p. 218). / HIM (p. 210): “Our experience in shifting from savings and loan operation to ownership of Freddie Mac shares tends to confirm a long-held notion that being prepared, on a few occasions in a lifetime, to act promptly in scale, in doing some simple and logical things, will often dramatically improve the financial results of that lifetime,” (Damn Right!, pp. 209–210, 218)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Buffett)** — *Fannie Mae, not bought.* AUTHOR: “When a similar opportunity arose to buy Fannie Mae shares, Buffett and Munger faltered. Buffett said they should have taken a large stake in Fannie Mae as well.” Buffett (the text ties it to both men; no Munger words): “My biggest lost opportunity was probably Fannie Mae. We owned a savings and loan, and that entitled us to buy 4 percent of Freddie Mac stock when it first came out. We did this and should have followed the same reasoning and bought more Fannie Mae stock. What was I doing? I was sucking my thumb.” (Damn Right!, p. 210)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Salomon preferred, 1987, and the board seat.* AUTHOR (p. 209): Wesco bought 100,000 newly issued shares of Series A cumulative convertible preferred of Salomon on October 1, 1987, at a cost of $100 million, “part of a $700 million transaction, in which Berkshire purchased $600 million and Wesco bought the remainder”; 9 percent dividend; each share convertible into 26.3 shares of common; profit on conversion “if the shares traded at or above $38.” (p. 257 prints Berkshire's own allocation as $700 million.) / Board seat, HIM (p. 258): “We had some pretty good foresight,” “When we bought big positions in ABC and in Salomon, Warren suggested I go on ABC's board. I said, ‘you never will need me (there).’ Salomon could get into enough trouble that it would need both of us.” (Damn Right!, pp. 209, 257–258)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Buffett)** — *Resigning Mutual Savings from the U.S. League, May 30, 1989.* Situation, AUTHOR (p. 211): Mutual belonged to the trade and lobbying organization; Munger sent “a flaming letter to the U.S. League”. REPORTED (Buffett): “Charlie and I really thought what was going on was awful. We wanted no part of it. We both had a certain fervor,” / Did: the letter, signed Charles T. Munger, is the formal resignation, and he released copies to the media. HIM (letter, p. 211): “we believe that the League's current lobbying operations are so flawed, indeed disgraceful, that we are not willing to maintain membership.” Reasons, HIM: the mess was made worse by “constant and successful inhibition over many years, through League lobbying, of proper regulatory response to operations of a minority of insured institutions dominated by crooks and fools,” by “Mickey Mouse accounting which made many insured institutions look sounder than they really were,” and by “inadequate levels of real equity capital”. HIM (p. 212): “It is not unfair to liken the situation now facing Congress to cancer and to liken the League to a significant carcinogenic agent.” / “The League responds to the savings and loan mess as Exxon would have responded to the oil spill from the Valdez if it had insisted thereafter on liberal use of whiskey by tanker captains.” / “a public apology is in order, not redoubled efforts to mislead further.” HIM (p. 213): “we are also, as one small measure of protest, releasing to the media, for such attention as may ensue, copies of this letter of resignation.” AUTHOR (p. 214): “Munger took some pride in the idea that Mutual Savings contributed to tough legislative action by his dramatic and widely printed resignation letter”; AUTHOR: “Just as he had on the issue of legalized abortions, Munger had taken off on a divergent path from his friends in the Republican party.” (Damn Right!, pp. 211–214)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Mutual Savings leaves the thrift business, 1987–1993.* AUTHOR (p. 209): “Munger and Buffett began to divert both Wesco and Mutual Savings away from the thrift business, preparing Wesco for what was to come.” AUTHOR (p. 214): in the 1989 annual report Munger said Mutual “expected to stay in the S&L business if all went well, but if not, it would get out of the business all together.” AUTHOR (p. 215): the new law forced Mutual to sell its portfolio of high quality preferred stocks yielding 10.8 percent and its Salomon convertible preferred; “though Munger felt that a profit would he realized on the sale of those securities as well, he preferred to play out the Salomon hand in his own way.” The law also required 70 percent of its $300 million in assets primarily in real estate loans and raised deposit-insurance premiums. HIM (p. 215): “by the mid-1990s the new premium rates will reduce Mutual Savings' annual earning power by about $200,000 from the level which would have occurred if it were still paying at the 0.083 percent-of-deposits rate which was in effect for years, instead of the new rate of 0.23 percent,” / Did, AUTHOR (p. 216): in 1992 Mutual gave up its S&L charter and liquidated many assets; in 1993 Wesco became a financial holding company; about $300 million of capital went to Wesco-Financial Insurance Co. (supercat), and Mutual sold its $92 million loan portfolio and $230 million of deposits to CenFed. Reason, AUTHOR: “Munger explained that the S&L took up a lot of his time in relation to the capital that was involved.” HIM: “So why shouldn't we do more of what works well for us and what's less complicated,” Munger asked. (Damn Right!, pp. 209, 214–216)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Moving Wesco's annual meeting.* AUTHOR (p. 207): the cafeteria building went vacant; Munger explained that “it would have cost more to clean the building up and move furniture into the vacant restaurant than it cost to rent a room fora few hours.” (The .txt prints “fora”.) HIM, to shareholders at McCormick & Schmick's: “I want to apologize for the elaborateness of this room,” (Damn Right!, p. 207)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *New American Electric: three choices, then a loss.* Situation, AUTHOR (p. 217): Glen Mitchel, a Caltech electrical engineer and friend “whom Munger believed had good business abilities,” found the business; Munger and Rick Guerin invested through the New America Fund; for years it “was a cash cow.” / Did, AUTHOR: when the Fund liquidated, Munger gave Mitchel three choices: (1) distribute the shares to Fund shareholders; (2) sell it entirely any way Mitchel wished; (3) “have Wesco buy 80 percent of New America Electric at a price approved by Buffett, 70 percent coming from New America Fund and 10 percent coming from Mitchel, leaving him with 20 percent.” Mitchel took the third. / Outcome, AUTHOR: choice (3) “appeared far from optimal for Mitchel, bad for Wesco, and good for New America Fund shareholders like Munger”; “the company lost about 30 percent of its value.” HIM: “It was the worst recession in Southern California since the Depression. New America Electric got clobbered,” “Wesco sold it at a moderate loss. It wasn't as if I knew it was going to result in a loss to Wesco. If so, I would have never done it. It was very embarrassing.” (Damn Right!, p. 217)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Wesco's dividend policy: the minority sets it.* Situation, AUTHOR: Berkshire owns most of Wesco; the Peters family holds about 1.3 percent. / Did, HIM: “Wesco's dividend policy is that which the minority shareholders prefer,” “At least the ones we know who invited us in. So, we are just deferring to the wishes of the very much minority shareholders. Now you can say, ‘that's eccentric,’ and you're right.” AUTHOR: “Though Munger does not approve of the practice, many investors pore over Wesco's Form 10-Q filing for insight into Buffett's investing style at Berkshire Hathaway.” (Damn Right!, pp. 206, 219)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Wesco: stating intrinsic value and saying when the stock is above it.* AUTHOR (p. 220): “Munger departs from the philosophy of Berkshire Hathaway and in the annual report he calculates Wesco's intrinsic value for shareholders.” End of 1998: $342 per share against a price of $354, “about 4 percent above intrinsic value.” AUTHOR: “Munger has no compunction about telling shareholders when the stock is overpriced.” HIM, 1993: “An orangutan could figure out that the stock is selling miles above the value of the company if it were liquidated. I keep telling people this, but they keep buying the stock.” Reason for doing it at Wesco and not at Berkshire, AUTHOR (p. 221): “because the companies are quite different.” HIM: “Wesco is so liquid and its operating businesses so moderate in size that it's rather easy to make a computation as to its asset value. You can figure out what would happen if you just closed the place and mailed checks back to all the shareholders.” (Damn Right!, pp. 220–221)
- **HIM** — *The bank account that would not balance.* “Wesco once moved its account from Security Pacific to Bank of America where we had the account get out of balance and nobody at the bank could get it back in balance. We closed our account and let it run out and made some accounting adjustments. In five years, nobody will remember....” AUTHOR's frame: “some corporate problems seem large at the moment, but in time, they will seem trivial. That is why long-term investing pays off.” (Damn Right!, p. 221)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Booth, Marshall)** — *Sending people to Buffett.* AUTHOR (p. 224): “Munger started steering California investors in Buffett's direction.” AUTHOR (p. 225): “In 1963, Munger suggested to Booth that he go see Buffett.” REPORTED (Booth): “After I'd been in Charlie's partnership a year or two, he told me about Warren and showed me the record from 1957 on,” Booth then spent a night in Omaha talking investments and wrote about $1 million across two years. REPORTED (Marshall): “Charlie recognized Warren's genius before anybody. If I had totally listened to Charlie about Warren I'd be a lot richer now,” (Damn Right!, pp. 224–225)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *GEICO and “pure opportunism”.* AUTHOR (pp. 226–227): Buffett bought into GEICO in 1976 when it was mismanaged and near bankruptcy; between 1976 and 1981 Berkshire invested $45 million; “Munger said there was no particular strategy involved, except to wait and watch for opportunities.” HIM: “Our rule is pure opportunism,” “We do not have a master plan. If there is a master plan somewhere in Berkshire, they're hiding it from me. Not only do we not have a master plan, we don't have a master planner.” (Damn Right!, p. 227)
- **AUTHOR** — *Gillette, 1989.* “In July 1989, Berkshire invested $600 million in Gillette's preferred stock, all of which later was converted into common shares.” “Buffett and Munger negotiated together with Gillette Chairman Coleman Mockler.” No reason from him on the page. (Damn Right!, p. 227)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Pushing for quality, See's onward.* AUTHOR (p. 228): “During this activity, Munger kept agitating to buy better quality companies, ones with strong earnings potential for the long-term and ones he believed would be less troublesome to own.” AUTHOR (p. 229): “Starting with See's Candy, Munger nudged Buffett in the direction of paying up for quality.” REPORTED (Ron Olson): “Charlie was very instrumental in pushing Warren toward Coca Cola type investments-a franchise that will have carrying value for generations,” REPORTED (Stan Lipsey): “Many times, Charlie elevates Warren's thinking-such as going for a stronger franchise.” (Damn Right!, pp. 228–230)
- **AUTHOR** — *Keeping Cap Cities when Buffett sold.* “When Buffett sold Berkshire's Capital Cities Communications stock in 1978 to 1980, he later regretted the sale. Munger, however, kept some personal holdings of Cap Cities, which performed exceptionally well” (Damn Right!, p. 230)
- **REPORTED (Graham) + AUTHOR** — *The New Yorker, not bought.* AUTHOR: “Buffett and Munger called on Katharine Graham and asked her to participate in purchasing the Neu' Yorker magazine.” (The .txt prints “Neu'”.) REPORTED (Graham): she did not want to be partners: “I thought it needed a new editor and I didn't know how to choose one.” No words of Munger's on the page. (Damn Right!, p. 230)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Selling and giving away Berkshire stock.* REPORTED (Buffett): “Charlie's family has 90 percent or more of its net worth in Berkshire shares,” AUTHOR (p. 236): “between 1993 and 1997 he sold $25 million worth of Berkshire”; he has given away hundreds of shares, including one to Robert Cialdini, author of Influence, “just because he likes him and his work.” HIM: “I've given away a fair amount of Berkshire (stock) in the last couple of years,” “I gave away a lot ... because I thought it was the correct way to behave. And I've sold some because I've had businesses of my own.” (Damn Right!, pp. 235–236)
- **AUTHOR** — *Keeping his own pay low.* “Munger and Buffett have remained steadfast in keeping their own compensation low. Each takes $100,000 in salary, plus directors fees from various corporations not controlled by Berkshire. Munger's 1998 directors fees came to about $81,300.” AUTHOR (p. 206): the Wesco chairmanship “pays Munger nothing”. (Damn Right!, pp. 206, 235)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Defending the terms Berkshire gets.* Situation, AUTHOR (p. 239): critics, “the Wall Street Journal especially,” objected that Buffett and Munger got better deals than other investors, as at Salomon and USAir where Berkshire was “summoned by management as a white knight”. / Did, AUTHOR: Munger defended the arrangements, saying that Berkshire “brings more to the party than just our money”; Buffett provides advice and expertise plus “patient” capital; “other shareholders also benefit when the stock in these companies rise.” (Damn Right!, pp. 239–240)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Buying whole companies, and paying in stock.* AUTHOR (p. 240): “Munger and Buffett were able to pursue their preferred strategy of swallowing companies whole whenever possible. When Berkshire owns a company entirely, the two are free to allocate the company's profits as they see fit.” At the 2000 meeting they explained that “by owning companies in their entirety, they could minimize the impact of a whimsical stock market or Berkshire's share price.” HIM (pp. 240–241): “It's not by accident that recent acquisitions at Berkshire Hathaway have been with stock. It's hard to make cash transactions in this market,” (Damn Right!, pp. 240–241)
- **AUTHOR** — *GEICO's second half, 1994–1995.* “Buffett said he wanted to talk to Munger about the deal. Later the same day, he offered $70 per share in cash to GEICO shareholders.” (After Morgan Stanley's range of $50.80 to $73.43 and its suggestion of $70.) Cost $2.3 billion. No words of Munger's. (Damn Right!, p. 242)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Booth)** — *General Re, 1998.* AUTHOR: Buffett acquired it in a stock swap “when in 1998” Berkshire's stock “was trading near an all-time high.” REPORTED (Booth): “He bought out Gen Re when Berkshire's stock was trading at around $80,000 in a stockfor-stock merger.” AUTHOR: “Munger points out, however, that Gen Re's stock, too was trading at a high price.” After an underwriting error found almost immediately: “Their experience told them that long-term, the re-insurer remained an excellent purchase.” (Damn Right!, pp. 243–244)
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED (Buffett)** — *USAir, 1990–1998.* Situation, AUTHOR (p. 244): in 1990 Buffett and Munger “bought a $358 million stake in USAirways preferred stock that could be converted into a 12 percent ownership of the airline. Both men joined the airline's board of directors.” REPORTED/HIM phrase (p. 244): Munger described an airline as “marginal cost with wings.” AUTHOR (pp. 244–245): he believed a bankruptcy judge should have shut Eastern down earlier; “once an airline files for bankruptcy, it is then operating debt free, thus it can be more competitive than a solvent one.” / Outcome, AUTHOR: dividends stopped in 1994; a $269.5 million pretax write-down; in 1995 the stake was valued at $86 million; “Munger and Buffett stepped down in 1995 after the airline failed to win cost-saving concessions from its unions.” USAir redeemed the $358 million of preferred on February 3, 1998. / HIM (p. 245), asked to explain the economics: “I'm glad you've given me the opportunity to display my small share of humility” and “We did not display our greatest brilliance.” HIM (pp. 245–246): “It was a humbling experience. To sit there and watch that net worth melt away-$150 million, $200 million-to watch a lot of lovely money that used to be yours and see it melt away.” and “All that said, it's a very leveraged business. So when the industry turned, it turned beautifully-for USAir included.” and “It worked out fine for Berkshire. But we're not looking for another experience like it.” REPORTED (Buffett): “When Charlie and I left the board, the fortunes of USAir went abruptly upward,” (Damn Right!, pp. 244–246)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Silver, July 1997 – January 1998.* AUTHOR (p. 248): Berkshire bought 129,710,000 ounces; the company press release says that “Mr. Buffett and Mr. Munger, vice chairman of Berkshire, concluded that equilibrium between supply and demand was only likely to be established by a somewhat higher price.” AUTHOR: a $650 million purchase of about 20 percent of the world's supply. HIM (pp. 248–249): “This whole episode will have about as much impact on Berkshire's future as Warren's bridge playing. It is close to a non-event.” Outcome, AUTHOR (p. 249): paid around $4.60 to $4.80 per ounce in summer 1997; $7 in February 1998; around $5 at year end. Munger would not give the position but said it was “perfectly obvious” that their expectations of the silver price “had not yet been realized.” (Damn Right!, pp. 248–249)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Sinegal, Buffett)** — *Costco: an outside board seat against his own rule; Price Enterprises.* HIM (p. 249): “I admired this place so much that I violated my rules [against sitting on outside boards,” (the .txt leaves the bracket unclosed). HIM: “It's hard to think of people who've done more in my lifetime to change the world of retailing for good, for added human happiness for the customer.” AUTHOR: “by selling quality merchandise very close to cost, the stores built such a loyal customer base that it qualifies as a franchise.” HIM: “If you get hooked on going to Costco with your family, you'll go for the rest of your life,” REPORTED (Sinegal, p. 250): “He truly believes in our business,” and the 76-year-old Munger “has never missed a board meeting.” Buffett (the text ties it to Munger's urging): “Charlie was constantly telling me about this terrific method of distribution, and after 10 years or so I started catching on to what he was saying, and we bought a little of Costco at Berkshire.” and “We should've owned more Costco, and probably if Charlie had been sitting in Omaha, we would've owned more Costco.” AUTHOR (pp. 250–251): “In February 1999, Munger made a related investment when he and several family members bought 8 to 9 percent of San Diego-based Price Enterprises Inc.”, a REIT holding the real estate left from Price Club, with about 2 million of its 23.7 million preferred shares. (Damn Right!, pp. 249–251) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 147–148 (REPORTED, Sinegal: “My purpose was to convince him to consider joining the Costco board of directors.” and “Later when he called to accept the position on the board, he referred to himself as a septuagenarian.”; HIM on Sinegal, p. 148, is in SIGNALS).
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED (Buffett, Olson)** — *Salomon, August 1991: get the whole truth out, then clean house.* AUTHOR (p. 255): “From the very first telephone call, Munger harbored suspicions that Salomon's official story was incomplete.” REPORTED (Buffett, p. 261): “Charlie insisted that they get the whole truth out,” REPORTED (Olson, p. 261): “the decision to waive attorney client privilege between the Salomon board and previous lawyers had a lot to do with why Salomon wasn't criminally indicted. Buffett and Munger made that decision together. We would give them everything we had as fast as we could get it.” HIM (p. 262), on political help: “We said to Moynihan, the New York Fed chairman (Gerald Corrigan) is a dominant man with his nose out of joint,” “He will resent political interference. We asked Moynihan to go back to his office and stay out of it, which is what he did.” AUTHOR (p. 263): “Buffett was the obvious candidate, but Munger told him he would be crazy to take the job, a warning that did not stick.” HIM: “When Warren realized the problem, he volunteered, which they took him up on,” “He had a good reputation. Nick Brady (Secretary of the Treasury) backed off a little, enough of a signal. Based on Brady's backing off, we know it gave us credibility.” AUTHOR: after testifying in Washington with Buffett, “Munger decided to go home and thereafter served as a long-distance advisor, except when he needed to be present as a Salomon board member.” REPORTED (Buffett): “It was sort of out of our hands. We sort of behaved and hoped they wouldn't kill us.” REPORTED (Denham): “Charlie was a very involved director. He had real ideas about the best way to approach things.” (Damn Right!, pp. 255, 261–263)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Rickershauser)** — *Buying the Daily Journal, 1977.* Situation, AUTHOR (p. 273): “When the small Los Angeles legal publication, the Daily Journal, came on the market, Munger saw a chance to own his own newspaper and expressed interest immediately.” AUTHOR (p. 274): he asked Stan Lipsey for an appraisal; Lipsey said the paper was “sadly outdated in its style and content and needed modernizing.” REPORTED (Rickershauser): “I asked him how to conduct the sale. He said, ‘I would like to be a bidder.’ We had some connection, so I got him another lawyer.” / Did, AUTHOR: Munger was the high bidder; “The Daily Journal was bought for about $2.5 million through the New America Fund.” HIM: “But since I had this legal-judicial background that he didn't, I was the logical one to be chairman,” “And we made him the vice chairman.” (“Him” is Guerin.) (Damn Right!, pp. 273–274)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Brill)** — *Steven Brill enters the Bay Area, 1986.* HIM (p. 277), when Brill asked if the Daily Journal was for sale: “We have no intention of selling at any price,” / Did, AUTHOR (p. 278): the Daily Journal bought smaller San Francisco-area legal papers (the San Francisco Banner and the Marin County Reporter, circulation 800 together) and redesigned features. HIM: “It's always hard to know what your motivations are when they are mixed. We had been thinking for a long time of doing a better job in San Francisco.” and “because we've coexisted with the Recorder with respectable profits for many decades. I don't expect the whole thing to escalate into insanity.” REPORTED (Brill, 1997): “If Charlie spent even 20 percent of his time running his legal papers, we probably would have been crushed.” HIM: “Why would I want to crush a gnat?” AUTHOR (p. 279): the Wall Street Journal reported in 1996 that the San Francisco paper had cost $2 million a year for ten years; “Munger disagrees with the numbers and maintains he will never close the paper down.” (Damn Right!, pp. 277–279)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Salzman as president, 1986.* AUTHOR (p. 280): when the editor Robert Work died, “Rather than promote Work's second in command, Munger gave the job of president to Gerald L. Salzman, the newspaper's accountant and chief financial officer.” HIM: “That's an accident of Salzman being so talented and so honest,” AUTHOR: “As he is prone to do, Munger decided to stick with a known, tested, and trusted person when choosing a new chief executive officer”; Salzman owns about 1 percent. (Damn Right!, p. 280)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Why he keeps the Daily Journal.* AUTHOR (p. 277): Munger “concedes that he is in the newspaper business more for personal satisfaction than for profit”. AUTHOR (p. 283): though buyers had shown interest, “Munger says the Daily Journal is a vehicle that allows him to be ‘socially constructive’ and has interesting financial prospects.” HIM (p. 276), on the daily profiles of judges: “The truth of the matter is I like judges. If judges don't do their work well, then civilization doesn't work well.” (Damn Right!, pp. 276–277, 283)
- **REPORTED (Guerin) + AUTHOR** — *The Ben Franklin head: changed his mind.* Situation, REPORTED (Guerin): a 3 percent-of-cost art requirement for the new building. “Charlie said, ‘let's commission a head of Ben Franklin, looking affable and wise, and engrave his great sayings on the pedestal.’ I said, ‘Charlie, that's horse manure. Our employees don't want to be preached to.. I said, ‘Let's do something cheerful.’ He thought about it for a while and said, ‘I think you're right.’ We commissioned an artist to do sea otters and a fountain.” Afterward, REPORTED (Guerin): “Charlie commissioned an artist to do about 20 copies,” of a Franklin bust, which he gave to himself, Guerin, the Marlborough School, the Harvard School and others. HIM (p. 284), to a Rotary Club: “Franklin's story can scarcely ever be told often enough,” (Damn Right!, pp. 284–285)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Taking the Good Samaritan board seat, and reversing the medical staff.* HIM: “I had enough sense to know it would be like tar baby. Once you got in, you would become stuck,” AUTHOR (p. 289): “it is Charlie's philosophy that a first-rate man should be willing to take at least some difficult jobs with a high chance of failure.” / Did, HIM (p. 292): “I proposed a resolution reversing a ruling of the organized medical staff on the ground that the staff was endangering the health and safety of our patients. With some doctors on the board voting yes, it passed 17 to 2,” AUTHOR: before taking that position he “studied, with the help of a physician friend, the mortality and morbidity rates for different systems of surgical care”; the chairman resigned and “Because Charlie had started the trouble by pushing for reform, he felt obligated to accept the job of chairman.” HIM on the old chairman: “He was an able man,” “and understandably was reluctant, as a layman, to reverse what purported to be medical decisions.” Reason, AUTHOR (p. 291): “the board always backed decisions of the medical staff and these decisions frequently protected the economic interests of certain doctors, rather than that of the patients or of quality medicine.” HIM, years later: “Ten years later, I hate having had to go through the heartache and tragedy, but I love many of the people I now work with,” (Damn Right!, pp. 289, 291–292)
- **REPORTED (Leeka) + AUTHOR** — *Hiring Andrew Leeka as president.* REPORTED (Leeka): Munger came into the interview, “went on for 35 to 40 minutes. Finally he asked me a question, but didn't give me a chance to answer,” and did not shake his hand. At the offer Leeka said he would normally discuss it with his wife; “Munger said nothing; he simply stared at Leeka.” Leeka: “In this situation, I don't think I need to.” “Good,” said Charlie. “That's why I'm hiring you.” AUTHOR (p. 289): they agreed Munger would hold executive committee meetings less than once a month; “Micro managing, any more than he has to, isn't Munger's style, though he never hesitates to call employees at his various enterprises and share ideas as they occur to him.” (Damn Right!, pp. 287–289)
- **REPORTED (Leeka) + AUTHOR** — *Good Sam: no tricks on billing, no FEMA claim, a loss-making service.* REPORTED (Leeka): “There are tricks a hospital can do to squeeze extra revenue from low-income and Medi-Cal billings, but Munger won't allow that,” After the Northridge earthquake other hospitals claimed every old crack; after examination found no significant damage, “Munger would not file a FEMA claim.” Leeka: “He won't do anything just for money,” “He'll go into a business (health care special services) even if he knows it will lose money, if it is the right thing to do.” AUTHOR: “Munger personally has recruited many physicians to the hospital staff, which is unconventional for a lay chairperson of a large nonprofit hospital.” AUTHOR: in Leeka's first year the hospital took a $20 million bad-debt write-off. (Damn Right!, p. 293)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Berkshire's shareholder-designated giving, 1981; stock gifts; leaving a board.* AUTHOR (p. 290): “In 1981, Munger came up with a novel corporate charity plan for Berkshire Hathaway. For each of its roughly one million shares (then trading at $470) Berkshire would contribute $2 to a charity of the shareholder's choice.” Reason, AUTHOR: “he argues that it is unfair for top officers of a company to allocate money to their favorite causes, while the real owners, the shareholders, have little or no voice in the matter.” AUTHOR (p. 289): he has given “a few hundred shares each to Good Samaritan Hospital, Planned Parenthood, Stanford University Law School, and the Harvard-Westlake School.” AUTHOR (pp. 289–290): on the National Corporation for Housing Partnerships, “Munger grew impatient with the way the work was going and eventually resigned from the board.” AUTHOR (p. 290): “It is Munger's habit to choose just two or three public causes that seem important, then concentrate on making a difference there.” (Damn Right!, pp. 289–290)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Booth)** — *The Munger Science Building, Harvard-Westlake.* AUTHOR (p. 296): he and Nancy gave more than $7 million; “Charlie himself was involved in almost every aspect of the building's design”; he thought the labs too limited. HIM at the dedication: “The problem with most buildings is that they don't build enough flexibility into them. We tried to assure that the science facility will work, and work well, for the better part of a century. I see no reason why it should become obsolete,” AUTHOR (pp. 296–297): he insisted on top-of-the-line ventilation and heating and earthquake standards far above current ones; when an architect said a sloped auditorium floor couldn't be done, “Munger pushed until the architect found a way.” REPORTED (Booth): “No, he doesn't offend people.” “He has a great ability to turn a phrase and make things amusing.” (Damn Right!, pp. 296–297)
- **HIM** — *Imitating Franklin by leaving business.* “I've tried to imitate, in a poor way, the life of Benjamin Franklin,” “When he was 42, Franklin quit business to focus more on being a writer, statesman, philanthropist, inventor, and scientist. That's why I have diverted my interest away from business.” (Damn Right!, p. 299)
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED (Katz, Buffett, Mulvey)** — *The Berkshire unit trusts: complaint to the SEC, a stinging letter, then B shares.* Situation, AUTHOR (p. 314): “Five Sigma Investment partners L.P. of Bala Cynwyd, Pennsylvania, had filed with the Securities and Exchange Commission to sell Berkshire through a vehicle called the Affordable Access Trust. The trust would require an initial deposit as small as $300.” Berkshire traded at about $35,000 a share; Nike Securities planned “a similar unit trust.” REPORTED (Sam Katz, Five Sigma): “Just because someone is not wealthy doesn't mean they don't have the aptitude or sophistication for this business.” REPORTED (an unnamed broker): “Buffett and Munger turned out to be control freaks.” / Did, AUTHOR (p. 314): Berkshire sent the SEC a 24-page memorandum in December 1995, “saying that the securities sale would mislead investors”; it “was prepared at Munger, Tolles” and also went to state regulators. “In addition to registering a complaint with the SEC and then coming up with an alternative of their own, Munger sent a stinging letter to Five Sigma.” HIM (the letter, p. 314): “Warren Buffett does not regard the current market price of Berkshire stock as a price that makes new investments in Berkshire attractive. If he were asked by a friend or family member whether he advised a new purchase of Berkshire shares at the current price, Mr. Buffett would answer, No.” REPORTED (Buffett, p. 314, naming both): “Neither Mr. Munger nor I would function as effectively if we had tens of thousands of people who were in one way or another disappointed with us. That's not Berkshire.” / The alternative, AUTHOR (p. 315): a B share worth one-thirtieth of the original (now A) share; “The deal was set up so that brokers could make very little commission money, thus discouraging them from pushing the shares onto their clients”; Schwab and Fidelity were in the underwriting syndicate. They planned 100,000 B shares “but said they would keep adding to the offering until the public demand was met”; the offering grew four times, 517,500 shares sold at $1,110, “doubling Berkshire's shareholder base to 80,000 individuals” and adding $600 million to capital. REPORTED (James K. Mulvey, Dresdner Securities): “This is a small problem that Mr. Buffett approached in a big way,” Result, AUTHOR: Nike went forward, “but the Berkshire trust never became the phenomenon that the originators had hoped it would.” (Damn Right!, pp. 313–315)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — *Owning tobacco shares, not a tobacco company (UST, April 1993).* Situation, AUTHOR (p. 315): Michael Lewis and others accused Munger and Buffett “of spouting high moral standards for the investment world, while not holding their own investments to the same requirements,” pointing to Berkshire's once having invested in “a tobacco seller.” HIM (p. 315): “We have set ourselves up, to some extent, as a moral censor of our own activities,” “but we have never had the attitude that when we buy a little piece of a company in an insurance portfolio, that we are a moral censor for the world.” / Did, AUTHOR (pp. 315–316): in April 1993 Berkshire took “approximately 5 percent” of UST, maker of Skoal and Copenhagen (and Chateau Ste. Michelle wine), at between $27 and $29 a share; “A 5 percent stake would have been worth more than $300 million.” At the same time it sold its holdings in RJR Nabisco. Reason, HIM (p. 316): “If we're buying stock in major companies that are out there, (Buffett) doesn't make his judgment based on some moral overview of their whole business. We do that in judging and controlling our own behavior.” AUTHOR: “Berkshire turned down a chance to buy a tobacco company outright.” HIM: “We don't want to be in the business of selling addictive drugs, with us being the controlling owners,” “It's not the way we do the game. We'll own shares, because if we don't own them, somebody else will. But we'll never have Berkshire in a controlling interest.” (Damn Right!, pp. 315–316)
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED (King Williams)** — *The Channel Cat: a catamaran he designed with a diver he met on a pier.* Situation, REPORTED (Williams, p. 325): two older men admired his lobster boat at the Santa Barbara pier; “They introduced themselves. Charlie Munger and a friend. I had no idea who they were.” AUTHOR: lunches talking about “fishing and boats”; REPORTED (Williams, p. 326): “Charlie once asked me, ‘King, if you could do anything in the world, what would it be?’” Williams said he would build the biggest catamaran he could; Munger “quizzed him about why he would build a catamaran”; REPORTED (Williams): “One day he said, ‘go find a catamaran.’” / Did, AUTHOR (p. 326): a yard at Green Cove Springs, Florida took the job; “Three months into the project, things went off track.” HIM, as the author gives it: “rogues, scallywags and pirates in Florida.” REPORTED (Williams): “I went down, things weren't going well,” “Charlie said, ‘Well, you can build a boat.’ So I fired the guys.” AUTHOR: Williams went into the yard “with the sheriff, a warrant, and house movers,” rented land on the St. Johns River and, because of pollution from the materials, built a hangar: REPORTED (Williams): “We made our own shipyard,” / Design, REPORTED (Williams, p. 327): “Charlie designed it, pretty much,” “Every week he'd send two to three drawings. I built it for him-with help from a marine engineer and 46 other guys.” AUTHOR: Munger “scrawled his notes, using a ruler and a black marker because they were easier for him to see” and drew a scale model of the main dining room with tables and chairs; on a visit he put a barrel in the upstairs room and, “after sitting there for awhile looking out, insisted the windows were too high”; Williams, who had fitted small windows for safety at sea, replaced them with large ones. AUTHOR: “Williams (with Munger's money) arranged for unemployment, health, and workers' compensation insurance”; REPORTED (Williams): “They all became very loyal,” and when the boat was finished Rachel Williams called the big boat builders and used the Channel Cat “as a demo to get all of the workers jobs before we left” (pp. 327–328). / Cost and reason, HIM (p. 329): “I've never in my life spent money foolishly like that. I said what the hell. It was a creative thing to do,” AUTHOR: “Although Munger will not say how much the boat cost, it is estimated by one expert that total expenditures must have run at around $6 million.” REPORTED (Williams): “Charlie wanted a dual purpose boat,” AUTHOR: Munger hopes that half the time it will be rented for corporate board meetings and the like, the other half donated to organizations for fund-raising events. Earlier, HIM (p. 324, fall 1998): “I'm building a boat,” “We're within 60 days of finishing. Call it Munger's folly. It's not an economic activity, but it's very creative. Nobody has built a boat exactly like it.” / Result, AUTHOR (p. 329): “Charlie once claimed he would never spend a night on the boat,” but when it was brought round he and Nancy flew to Cabo San Lucas and “spent three days in Baja California where they went whale watching and fishing”; the boat has no license of its own at Santa Barbara, “Partly because local tour boat owners objected, and partly because of its large size.” (Damn Right!, pp. 324–330)
- **REPORTED (Williams)** — *A house he built in Montecito.* “We were driving along one day and Charlie said, ‘I've got to show you this house I built.’” Munger directed Williams “to drive to the top of Hot Springs Road in Montecito, where he pointed out a mansion with a sweeping view of the Pacific, a lap pool, extensive gardens on so much land it took a golf cart to get around.” Williams: “I said, Charlie, your retirement home! Charlie replied, ‘Naw, I'd never live in a place like that.’” Williams had just said: “Charlie is very practical,” (Damn Right!, p. 330)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *Flying: from coach to a timeshare jet, 2000.* HIM: “Warren kids me about flying coach, which I used to do more often,” “Now, when traveling with Nancy, we usually go first class or business class.” AUTHOR: “Finally in 2000 Munger signed up for a timeshare private jet service through the Berkshire Hathaway-owned company Executive Jet.” (See TEMPERAMENT, p. 247, for his earlier chiding of Buffett over the company plane.) (Damn Right!, p. 322)
- **AUTHOR (Appendix C, time line; the .txt prints the event lines without their years)** — *Items that tie Munger to an office or a transaction.* “Munger became a trustee of Harvard School in Los Angeles, which later merged with Westlake School.” (p. 339) “In March, Buffett and Munger purchased the Illinois National Bank.” (p. 340) “Buffett and Munger acquired Scott & Fetzer, parent of World Book Encyclopedia, Kirby vacuum cleaners and other companies, for $315 million, virtually snatching it from the hands of Ivan Boesky.” (p. 341) “Berkshire Hathaway invested $1.3 billion in three companies, Gillette, USAir and Champion International. Munger negotiated Berkshire's investment in Gillette. In July, Berkshire bought $600 million in preferred stock, which later was converted into 11 percent of Gillette's common.” (p. 342) “Mutual Savings and Loan Association was liquidated.” “In January, Munger and Buffett joined the board of troubled U.S. Airways.” (p. 342) “Munger and Buffett stepped down from U.S. Airway's board.” (p. 342) No Munger words in the time line. (Damn Right!, pp. 339–342)
- **AUTHOR** — *Counting passersby instead of carrying groceries.* Situation: the Depression, boys' jobs through family connections. / Did: took “a boring job counting passersby; it paid forty cents an hour.” Reason (author's): “Charlie preferred this work to carrying heavy boxes of groceries.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 12)
- **AUTHOR** — *Why he turned to outside ventures.* Situation: his law earnings “were based on a combination of billable hours and seniority”; AUTHOR: “He wanted more than what a senior law partner would be able to earn. He sought to be like his firm's leading capitalist clients, in particular, the universally admired Harvey Mudd.” / Did: with Nancy's support, took up outside ventures and other ways to generate income. AUTHOR on the limits he kept: “he never forgot the sound principles caught by his grandfather: to concentrate on the task immediately in front of him and to control spending.” Also in: Harvey Mudd as the model client, Damn Right!, p. 87. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 17)
- **AUTHOR** — *Buying stock in a client's electronics business.* “He began investing in stocks and acquired equity in one of his client's electronics businesses-a practice common among lawyers in the mid-1950s and 1960s.” Reason (author's): “Charlie gained invaluable knowledge about business while his client enjoyed proactive attention of a lawyer who knew more than just the law.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 17)
- **AUTHOR** — *Putting the real-estate profits back in.* “In all cases, he left all of his profits in real estate ventures so that bigger and bigger projects became possible.” Author's figure: “When he stopped in 1960, he had a nest egg of $1 million from real estate projects alone.” (The same page dates the first condominium venture 1961, so the book is inconsistent on the year.) Also in: Damn Right!, pp. 95–99 (Booth ventures). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 18)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Buffett)** — *Quitting the law at the earliest point he could afford to.* AUTHOR: “Warren was unenthusiastic about Charlie's continued practice of law. He said that while law might be a good hobby for Charlie, it was a far less promising business than what Warren was doing. Warren's logic helped Charlie to decide to quit law practice at the earliest point he could afford to do so.” AUTHOR on the exit plan: the law practice “was by then a backstop rather than an ending objective for Charlie. At about the time that he was launching his new law firm, he was carefully crafting his exit plan.” (Buffett's argument is recorded here only because the text ties it to Munger's decision; his own reasons are in the entry on leaving the law, 1965.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 18, 23)
- **AUTHOR + HIM (set by the compilers)** — *The 1962–1975 partnership record and ending the fund business.* AUTHOR: “It did exceptionally well for the first eleven years, compounding at 28.3 percent gross (20.0 percent net) vs. 6.7 percent for the Dow, without a single down year. But the partnership was hit hard in the vicious bear market of 1973 and 1974 when it fell 31.9 percent and 31.5 percent in back-to-back years,” HIM: this decline was “having its major investments virtually sure of eventually being sale able at prices higher than the quoted market prices” (the .txt prints “sale able”). AUTHOR: “the partnership rebounded strongly in 1975, rising 73.2 percent, bringing the overall record over fourteen years to 19.8 percent (13.7 percent net) compounded annual returns vs. 5.0 percent for the Dow.” / Did: “After this difficult experience, Charlie followed Warren in concluding that he no longer wanted to manage funds directly for investors (Warren had closed his own partnerships in 1969). Instead, they resolved to build equity through stock ownership in a holding company.” Wheeler, Munger was liquidated and its holders received Blue Chip Stamps and Diversified Retailing shares. Also in: Damn Right!, pp. 146–150. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 25–26)
- **REPORTED (Guerin)** — *Paying two old ladies face value.* “On two occasions. I saw Charlie pay more than he needed to in business transactions. First, with two little old ladies who held notes in a business we were buying, which we could have easily redeemed at far less than face value-Charlie nevertheless paid them face value.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 87–88)
- **REPORTED (Guerin)** — *Setting the price of Guerin's half at $230,000, not $130,000.* “to raise some cash I needed for another investment, I offered to sell him my half of a venture we were in together, and he said to set a fair price-I said $130,000; he said, no, $230,000 was correct, and he paid me that.” Guerin: “he had set a more accurate price, and I did eventually come to see it that way.” Also in: Damn Right!, p. 142 (the buyout valuation). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 88)
- **AUTHOR (compilers' generalization)** — *Few, large bets.* “Charlie calls it ‘sit on your ass investing’”; AUTHOR: “when Charlie likes a business, he makes a very large bet and typically holds the position for a long period”; “In his view, a portfolio of three companies is plenty of diversification. Accordingly, Charlie is willing to commit uncommonly high percentages of his investment capital to individual ‘focused’ opportunities.” HIM, as the compilers quote it: “extreme patience combined with extreme decisiveness.” AUTHOR: he does not make “initial positions” or “small, speculative investments.” Also in: Damn Right!, p. 143 (concentration, Buffett reporting). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 66–67, 77)
- **HIM (Wesco meeting notes)** — *Ninety percent of his net worth in Berkshire.* “I'm happy having ninety percent of my net worth in Berkshire stock. We're going to try to compound it at a reasonable rate with or-rt taking unreasonable risk of using leverage. If we can't do this, then that's just too damn bad.” (the .txt prints ‘or-rt’ for the word before ‘taking’; read as ‘without’.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 98)
- **HIM (Wesco meeting notes)** — *Acquisitions: waiting for no-brainers, and looking for them.* “Two-thirds of acquisitions don't work. Ours work because we don't try to do acquisitions-we wait for no-brainers.” And, on how they found them: “we scrounge around for companies to buy. For twenty years, we didn't buy more than one or two per year. It's fair to say that we were rooting around. There were no commissioned salesmen.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 99)
- **HIM (Wesco meeting notes)** — *Choosing managers: admiration and passion.* “We've bought business after business because we admire the founders and what they've done with their lives. In almost all cases, they've stayed on, and our expectations have not been disappointed.” “I would argue passion is more important than brain power.” (the .txt runs it into a question: “What matters most passion or competence that was inborn? ... I would argue passion is more important than brain power.”) “By and large, we've chosen people we admire enormously to have the power beneath us.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 99–100)
- **HIM (Wesco meeting notes)** — *Leaving the subsidiary CEOs alone.* “we promised them they could spend one hundred percent of their time on their businesses. We place no impediments on them running their businesses.” “We have decentralized power to a point just short of total abdication.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 100)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Removing people: seldom; Paul Arnold at CORT.* “It's amazing how few times over the decades we've had to remove a person-far less than other companies. It's not that we're soft or foolish, it's that we're wiser and luckier. Most people would look back and say their worst mistake was not firing someone soon enough. [We don't say that.] Our record is fabulous. We're old fashioned.” On CORT Business Services (the compilers: “a furniture rental business that Wesco acquired”): “Warren said to me, ‘You're going to love Paul Arnold [CORT's CEO].’ And he was right. Paul's been running the business since he was in law school and loves it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 101)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Insurance: writing less when the market is unfavorable; float.* “I'm glad we have insurance, though it's not a no-brainer, I'm warning you. We have to be smart to make this work.” “Growing float at a sizeable rate at low cost is almost impossible-but we intend to do it anyway.” “Lumpy results and being willing to write less insurance business if market conditions are unfavorable...that is one of our advantages as an insurer-we don't give a damn about lumpy results. Everyone else is trying to please Wall Street. This is not a small advantage.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 102)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Berkshire repurchasing its own shares.* “In the past when Berkshire had gotten cheap we have found other even cheapest stocks to buy I would always preferred this it's more fun to have the companies so lacking in repute that we can make money for some shareholders by buying out others” (the .txt has lost its punctuation and has errors). Also on p. 105, on the stock falling after Buffett: “If the stock went down, Berkshire could buy it back.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 103, 105)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Changing his mind on See's: the criticism from Ira Marshall.* “If See's Candy, when we were buying it had asked for hundred thousand dollars more Buffett chimed in, 10000 dollars more Warren and I would have walked and that's how dumb we were.” “Ira Marshall said you guys are crazy-there are some things you should pay up for, like quality businesses and people. You are underestimating quality. We listened to the criticism and changed our mind. This is a good lesson for anyone: the ability to take criticism constructively and learn from it. If you take the indirect lessons we learned from See's, you could say Berkshire was built on constructive criticism” (the .txt prints “Ira”; Damn Right! gives “Al Marshall”). Also in: Damn Right!, pp. 174, 182. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 110)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Mistakes of omission; the Walmart miss.* “The most extreme mistakes in Berkshire's history have been mistakes of omission. We saw it, but didn't act on it. They're huge mistake-we've lost billions. And we keep doing it. We're getting better at it. We never get over it.” “After nearly making a terrible mistake not buying See's, we've made similar mistakes many times. We are apparently slow learners. These opportunity costs don't show up on financial statements but have cost us many billions.” “We rub our noses in mistakes of omission-as we just did.” AUTHOR (the compilers' bracket): they “had just discussed failing to buy Walmart stock because it moved up a bit, a $10 billion mistake.” His two types: “1) doing nothing that Warren calls "sucking my thumb" and 2) buying with an eyedropper things we should be buying a lot of.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 110–111)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *When a stock he likes goes down.* “Over many decades, our usual practice is that if [the stock of something we like goes down, we buy more and more. Sometimes something happens, you realize you're wrong, and you get out. But if you develop correct confidence in your judgment, buy more and take advantage of stock prices.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 111)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Sitting on cash, May 2004.* “Common stocks may be reasonably fairly valued, but they are not overwhelming bargains. Our cash is speaking for itself. If we had a lot of wonderful ideas, we wouldn't have so much cash. Berkshire and Wesco are full of cash that we don't know what to do with.” “It's the most extreme it's ever been. In the past, we've just been patient, and we were able to put it to work” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 117)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Stock options: not used at Berkshire; the Costco case.* “As our shareholders know our system is different from most big corporations. We think it's less capricious.” “Except where we inherit it [a stock option program], we don't use it.” (p. 126) On Costco: “For example, at Costco we issued stock options with strike prices of $30 and $60, and Black-Scholes value the $60 ones higher. This is insane.” (p. 127) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 126–127)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Gen Re's derivatives book: tried to sell, then liquidated.* “We tried to sell Gen Re's derivatives operation and couldn't, so we started liquidating it. We had to take big markdowns.” “I think we're the only big corporation in America to be running off its derivative book. It's a crazy idea for people who are already rich-like Berkshire-to be in this business. It's a crazy business for big banks to be in.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 131)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *Salomon: telling the bank not to deal with Normandy America.* Context: the compilers say Salomon had to pull Normandy's IPO in 1995 when it found “the promoter had completely manufactured his record.” “Warren, Lou Simpson, and I were all on the board [of Salomon], we were the largest shareholders, and we said, ‘Don't do business with this guy.’ But they ignored us and said that the underwriting committee had approved it.” Earlier, on Salomon and Robert Maxwell: “Solomon was at least disciplined and rational as other investment banks, but by the end Solomon was begging for investment business from Robert Maxwell whose nickname was the bouncing Czech.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 121–122)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes; the USC talk, 1994)** — *Textile mill and the new loom (Buffett's decision, Munger's account and reasoning).* “For example, when we were in the textile business, which is a terrible commodity business, we were making low-end textiles-which are a real commodity product. And one day, the people came to Warren and said, "They've invented a new loom that we think will do twice as much work as our old ones." And Warren said, "Gee, I hope this doesn't work-because if it does, I'm going to close the mill." And he meant it.” Munger on what Buffett was thinking: “It's a lousy business. We're warning substandard returns and keeping it open just to be nice to the elderly workers. But we're not going to put huge amounts of new capital into a lousy business.” (the .txt prints “[it's” and “warning” for “earning”). The quoted decision is Buffett's; the explanation is Munger's, and he joins it to his own “second step” (see RULES, p. 189). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 187–188)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994)** — *Capital Cities/ABC trade publications beaten by competitors.* “we-by which I mean Berkshire Hathaway-are the largest shareholder in Capital Cities/ABC. And have had trade publications there that got murdered-where our competitors beat us. And the way they beat us was by going to a narrower specialization.” No action is stated on the page. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 178)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — *What he says he would do as a young investor today.* “If you have only a little capital and are young today, there are fewer opportunities than when I was young.” “I'd work with very small stocks, searching for unusual mispriced opportunities, but it's such a small world.” (A statement of what he would do, not a past action.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 112)
- **HIM** — *The Washington Post, 1973–74, and the two-newspaper towns.* Situation, HIM (p. 215): “we twice bought into two-newspaper towns which have since become one-newspaper towns. So we made a bet to some extent.” / Did: bought the Post. Reason, HIM (p. 216): “we bought it at about twenty percent of the value to a private owner. So we bought it on a Ben Graham-style basis-at one-fifth of obvious value-and, in addition, we faced a situation where you had both the top hand in a game that was clearly going to end up with one winner and management with a lot of integrity and intelligence.” (the .txt prints a lower-case “washington Post”.) On the owners: “They're very high class people-the Katharine Graham family.” On the date: “That was probably a once-in-forty-years-type denouement in the markets.” Result: “That investment's up about fifty times over our cost. If I were you, I wouldn't count on getting any investment in your lifetime quite as good as the Washington Post was in '73 and'74.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 215–216)
- **HIM** — *From Graham's cheap stocks to better businesses.* Situation: Berkshire began as Graham followers (the .txt prints “Grahamires”). / Did: changed the method. Reason, HIM (p. 206): “And so having started out as Grahamires-which, by the way, worked fine-we gradually got what I would call better insights. And we realized that some company that was selling at two or three times book value could still be a hell of a bargain because of momentums implicit in its position, sometimes combined with an unusual managerial skill plainly present in some individual or other, or some system or other.” “And once we'd gotten over the hurdle of recognizing that a thing could be a bargain based on quantitative measures that would have horrified Graham, we started thinking about better businesses.” Result, HIM (p. 206): “the bulk of the billions in Berkshire Hathaway has come from the better businesses. Much of the first $200 or $300 million came from scrambling around with our Geiger counter. But the great bulk of the money has come from the great businesses.” HIM (p. 207): “even some of the early money was made by being temporarily present in great businesses. Buffett Partnership, for example, owned American Express and Disney when they got pounded down.” HIM (p. 205): “if we'd stayed with classic Graham the way Ben Graham did it, we would never have had the record we have.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 205–207)
- **HIM** — *Disney: why he talked about the stock in the Capital Cities deal.* Situation: the audience question (not his words), p. 260: “In the media, it was reported that you mentioned thinking about taking the cash.” / Did: the page records no decision beyond what the question assumes (stock for stock). Reason, HIM (p. 260): “Disney's a perfectly marvelous company, but it's also very high-priced. Part of what it does is to make ordinary movies which is not a business that attracts me at all. However, part of what Disney has is better than a great gold mine. My grandchildren-I mean. those videocassettes” (the .txt ends the answer in an ellipsis). HIM (p. 261): “Disney didn't have to invent anything or do anything except take the thing out of the can and stick it on the cassette.” HIM (p. 262): “So Disney got this enormous tail wind from life. And it was billions of dollars worth of tail wind.” “Eisner and Wells were brilliant in how they ran Disney. But the huge tail wind from videocassette sales on all of the old stuff that was there when they came in, that was just an automatic break for the new management.” HIM (p. 214), on pricing: “And Disney found that it could raise those prices a lot, and the attendance stayed right up.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 214, 260–262)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — *USAir: what the stake was meant to be.* Situation: asked about Buffett's calling the airline investment an example of what not to do. / Reason, HIM (pp. 258–259): “We were not buying stock in USAir on the theory that the common shareholders were certain to prosper-because the history of the airline business in terms of taking care of shareholders has been terrible. It was a preferred stock with a mandatory redemption. In effect, we were loaning money to USAir, and we had this equity kicker.” “We weren't guessing whether it would be a great place for the shareholders. We were guessing whether it would remain prosperous enough to pay off a credit instrument-carrying a fixed dividend and a mandatory redemption.” “And we guessed that the business would not get so bad that we'd have a credit threat for which we were not being adequately compensated by the high rate we were getting.” Outcome, HIM (p. 259): “As it happened, USAir went right to the brink of going broke. It was hanging by a thread for several months. It's since come back. And we'll probably get all our money back plus the whole coupon. But it was a mistake.” AUTHOR (editor's note, p. 259): “Berkshire did indeed come out whole on its USAir investment.” (See the USAir entry above, from Damn Right!.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 258–259)
- **HIM** — *Reversing two sets of engineers.* Situation, HIM (p. 270): “I did recently reverse [the conclusions of] two sets of engineers.” / Did: found a third engineer. Reason, HIM (pp. 270–271): “I saw huge reasons in the circumstances for bias in each set of engineers as each recommended a course of action very advantageous to itself. And what each was saying was so consonant with a natural bias that it made me distrust it. Also, perhaps I knew enough engineering to know that [what they were saving] didn't make sense.” (the .txt prints “saving” for “saying”.) Result, HIM (p. 271): “finally, I found a third engineer who recommended a solution I approved. And, thereafter, the second engineer came to me and said, ‘Charlie, why didn't I think of that?’ which is to his credit. It was a much better solution, both safer and cheaper.” Self-check, HIM (p. 270): “Well, I may be an egomaniac, but I don't think I know everything.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 270–271)
- **HIM** — *Declining to make a better policeman's helmet.* Situation, HIM (p. 281): “I was part owner of the biggest shareholder in a company that invented a better policeman's heel-rest. It was made of Kevlar or something of that sort. And they brought it to us and wanted us to [manufacture] it” (the .txt prints “heel-rest”; the next lines say “helmet”). “As a matter of ideology, we're very pro-police.” / Did: refused to make it, and told the inventors so: “We're a rich corporation. We can't afford to make a better policeman's helmet. That's just how the civilization works. All risks considered, it can't work for us. But we want the civilization to have these.” “Get somebody else to make it. Transfer the technology or whatever to somebody who can do it.
