- **HIM (Talk Seven Revisited, 2006)** — A thought experiment on investment costs. “Assume that 2006 stock prices rise by 200% while corporate earnings do not rise, at which point all the sensibly distributable earnings of all U.S. corporations combined amount to less than the total of all stockholder investment costs, because such costs rise proportionally with stock prices.” (p. 378) His verdict: “But to me it would resemble a weird and disturbing combination of (1) a gambling casino imposing an unreasonably greedy take for the house, plus (2) a form of Ponzi-like scheme, similar to the market for expensive art, in which participation would be unsuitable for pension funds, etc., plus (3) a bubble of speculation that would eventually burst, probably with unfortunate macroeconomic consequences.” “Such a state of affairs, or even a lesser version, would, I think, reduce the reputation of our country, and deservedly so.” (p. 379) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 378–379)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, a work of fiction; narrator)** — The one flaw. “The Quant Tech story is best understood as a classic sort of tragedy in which a single flaw is inexorably punished by remorseless Fate. The flaw was the country's amazingly peculiar accounting treatment for employee stock options.” (p. 381) On who chose it: “This amazingly peculiar accounting convention had been selected by the accounting profession, over the objection of some of its wisest and most ethical members, because corporate managers, by and large, preferred that their gains from exercising options covering their employers' stock not be counted as expense in determining their employers' earnings.” (pp. 385–386) “The injunction was that normally followed by insecure and powerless people: ‘Whose bread I eat, his song I sing.’” (p. 386) “Fortunately, the income tax authorities did not have the same amazingly peculiar accounting idea as the accounting profession. Elementary common sense prevailed, and the bargain element in stock option exercises was treated as an obvious compensation expense, deductible in determining income for tax purposes.” (p. 386) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 381–386)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, a work of fiction; narrator)** — Compounding and cheating both end. “However, all man's desired geometric progressions, if a high rate of growth is chosen, at last come to grief on a finite earth. And the social system for man on earth is fair enough, eventually, that almost all massive cheating ends in disgrace.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 394)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, the Great Judge speaking in the closing parable; the .txt of pp. 398–399 is partly garbled)** — Whom he would not blame hardest. “‘Smith,’ he said, ‘I can't come down hardest on low-level cognitive error, much of it subconsciously caused by the standard incentive systems of the world.’” “‘No, no,’ said the Great Judge, ‘These people operate in a virtual maelstrom of regrettable forces and can't reasonably be expected to meet the behavioral standard you seek to impose.’” (p. 398) Of the accountants, as printed (the surrounding lines are a garbled run-on): “it is inexcusable that they deliberately caused all this lying and cheating that was so obviously predictable” (p. 399) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 398–399)
- **HIM (Talk Eight Revisited, 2006)** — Bad accounting is worse than bad concrete. “I had a lot of fun composing this account in the summer of 2000. But I was serious as I tried to show how standard accounting treatment for stock options was functionally equivalent to simpler types of promotional fraud.” “To me, a profession and a nation that allow unsound accounting for management cost are leaning in the same moral direction as the group that leaves most of the steel out of the concrete in erecting high-rise apartment buildings. Moreover, the unsound accounting is more virulent than the murderous construction practice. After all, the defective constructors have a harder time rationalizing their deplorable behavior. And, therefore, the bad accounting will more easily spread than the defective construction. Which is exactly what happened, as defective accounting for stock options became ubiquitous.” “By the time stock options are exercised, the total cost charged is usually far less than total cost incurred. Moreover the part of cost that is charged to earnings is often manipulated downward by dubious techniques.” “What this accounting saga constitutes is one more sad example of evil rewarded dying hard, as a great many people conclude that something can't be evil if they are profiting from it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 400; the 1991 FASB box on p. 388 gives the editors' version, “The right way to behave is to never let improper accounting start.”)

## 3. REFUSALS

- **HIM** — Would not return to high technology. “I never went back to the high-tech mode.” (Context: the transformer and oscillograph years; see DECISIONS, p. 90.) (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **HIM** — Would not buy to liquidate. “It's not that much fun to buy a company that you hope liquidates at a profit just before it is destined to go broke.” (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **HIM** — Refused to keep customers that couldn't make money. “we finally righted it by firing all the customers who wouldn't let it make money” (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **HIM** — Would not accept a scolding without a basis. “Roy, you have no right to talk to me this way until the first time I fail you in billing and collection” (Damn Right!, p. 88)
- **HIM** — Quit a client over how he was handling things. “I didn't like the way he was doing it. I said ‘I quit’ as his attorney.” (Damn Right!, p. 99)
- **HIM** — Called skimping on landscaping malpractice. “Stinting on landscaping is building malpractice.” (Damn Right!, p. 99)
- **REPORTED (Buffett's Foreword, on Munger)** — Called envy the silliest sin. “Envy, however, strikes him as the silliest of the Seven Deadly Sins, since it produces nothing pleasant at all.” (Buffett is reporting Munger's view; no Munger sentence is quoted.) (Damn Right!, p. 8) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 140 (HIM: “Envy is a really stupid sin because it's the only one you could never possibly have any fun at.”).
- **HIM** — Disliked clients of the wrong sort. “I once complained that he [Al Munger] should have more clients like Grant McFayden and fewer like a certain other man,” (Damn Right!, p. 88)
- **REPORTED (Charles Jr., son)** — Does not go back. “My dad, if he had a bad experience in a town, in a restaurant, he would not go back. I'd try again.” (Damn Right!, p. 74)
- **AUTHOR (reporting what he said)** — Did not want to be on the Forbes list. AUTHOR: “the 76-year-old Munger said it was his goal to stay just below the wealth-level required to be named to the Forbes richest Americans list.” (Damn Right!, p. 21)
- **HIM** — Would not keep borrowing for real estate or do it part-time. “I didn't like constantly borrowing more money. Also, it was an activity with many details, each crucial, difficult to handle as a full-time activity and extremely difficult as a part-time activity.” (Damn Right!, p. 102)
- **HIM** — Would not let the zoning authorities have their way. “I developed it because I didn't want to let the zoning authorities rob me the way they wanted to.” (Sea Meadow; he adds that the result would have been better had he let them: see DECISIONS.) (Damn Right!, p. 105)
- **HIM** — Would not keep working for other people as a lawyer. “But I was entirely willing to give up working for other people as a lawyer,” “I usually thought I knew better than the client anyway, so why should I have to do it his way?” (Damn Right!, p. 130)
- **REPORTED (Marshall)** — Called the work of an impossible client a bad bargain. “Too often, if you absolutely kill yourself over an impossible client and get a ten strike, your reward is you get to do it all over again for an equally impossible client.” (Damn Right!, p. 130)
- **HIM** — Called many of Graham's precepts madness. “I thought a lot of them were just madness,” “They ignored relevant facts.” (Damn Right!, p. 114)
- **HIM** — Would not pay above intrinsic value. “I never want to pay above intrinsic value for stock-with very rare exceptions where someone like Warren Buffett is in charge,” (Damn Right!, p. 115)
- **AUTHOR (the group of Munger, Buffett and Guerin)** — No hostile moves. REPORTED (Guerin): “We were of a ‘raiders' generation,” “That of Sol Steinberg, Harold Simmons, and so forth. But we are not like them.” AUTHOR: “The group never made a tender offer without management's consent and never engaged in a proxy fight.” (Damn Right!, p. 144)
- **REPORTED (Guerin)** — Would not profit from the aunts' distress. “Charlie was not willing to benefit from their distress. He could have taken advantage of them, but did not.” (Damn Right!, p. 141)
- **REPORTED (Booth)** — No foolish risks. “In no way was there a desire to run foolish risks as many gamblers do,” “None of that. And particularly when we were younger and hungrier.” (Damn Right!, p. 143)
- **AUTHOR** — Would not manage other people's money in a limited partnership again, and would not take an override on the final distribution. “he had resolved, like Buffett, to stop managing money for others in a limited partnership format.” “And he would liquidate soon enough so that he would not take any general partner's override when the main investment positions were distributed.” (Damn Right!, p. 150)
- **HIM** — Would not wrestle with a pig. “Never wrestle with a pig because if you do you'll both get dirty, but the pig will enjoy it.” (Damn Right!, p. 169)
- **HIM** — Would not have bought See's at a slightly higher price. “If they had wanted just $100,000 more for See's, we wouldn't have bought it,” (Damn Right!, p. 182)
- **AUTHOR (what he told shareholders)** — Would not pour money into expanding a cash-throwing business. “To try to expand would be throwing money down a rat hole, he said.” (Damn Right!, p. 183)
- **HIM** — Called a newspaper's building absurd. “Why does a newspaper need a palace to publish in?” AUTHOR: he “was repelled by the design” of the building's architect and by the “artsy-craftsy” construction that caused unfixable leaks. (Damn Right!, p. 195)
- **HIM** — Called a school plan absurd. On seeing boys' and girls' bathrooms of the same size in a science building: “You're going to do this in a building where you teach biology?” (Damn Right!, p. 104)
- **HIM** — Declined the limelight. At the 1998 Berkshire meeting, after Buffett said “Put Charlie's name on the signs.” (picketers' signs about abortion funding): “I am perfectly willing to have that limelight passed.” (Damn Right!, p. 188)
- **HIM** — Would not name the surgeon. “The man who did the first operation? I won't tell you his name. A perfectly nice guy. Our family eye doctor. I made the mistake-the fault was mine.” (Damn Right!, p. 200)
- **HIM** — Would not claim the stamp business as a success. “So I presided over a failure of 99.99 percent,” “in terms of the trading stamp company, I laid an egg.” (Damn Right!, p. 165)
- **HIM** — Would not work with flannel-mouthed people. “I never had any flannel-mouthed baloney in the operation. I dealt with quality people.” (Damn Right!, p. 152)
- **HIM** — Not in favor of rewards that don't build anything. “Generally, I'm not in favor of a social system that throws huge rewards to people who don't improve the factories, don't invent better systems, and so forth.” (Damn Right!, p. 171)
- **HIM** — Would not seek action for its own sake. “not seeking action for its own sake, but instead combining extreme patience with extreme decisiveness.” (Damn Right!, p. 165)
- **HIM** — Would not do everything with “lily white hands.” “I don't believe in doing everything with lily white hands,” (Damn Right!, p. 104)
- **HIM** — Would not buy the “Jump Z tranches”. “As we select mortgage-backed securities, we will probably not be buying any complex instruments. Despite our love of comedy, we are going to avoid the newest form of ‘Jump Z tranches in REMICS.’” “We are deterred from buying such securities partly by our hatred of complexity.” “We also dread the prospect of state and federal examiners, none of whom has a PhD in physics, reviewing, one after the other, our choices for soundness and billing us on a cost-plus basis to reflect value thus added.” “Some of the wonders of modern finance go on without us as we yearn for a lost age when most reasonable people could, with effort, understand what was going on.” (The .txt prints “mortgage-hacked”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 214–215)
- **HIM** — Would not stay in the League. See DECISIONS, p. 211: “we are not willing to maintain membership.” (Damn Right!, p. 211)
- **HIM** — Would not do leveraged buy-outs. “To Wesco, which does not engage in leveraged buy-outs, making good acquisitions was always tough,” (Damn Right!, p. 216)
- **AUTHOR** — Did not approve of copying Wesco's 10-Q. “Though Munger does not approve of the practice, many investors pore over Wesco's Form 10-Q filing for insight into Buffett's investing style at Berkshire Hathaway.” (Damn Right!, p. 219)
- **HIM** — Would not write insurance to the rating agencies' capacity. At Wesco's 1993 meeting: “People are always saying to Berkshire, ‘Gee, why don't you write a lot more volume in relation to capital? Everyone else is doing it. The rating agencies say that you can write twice as much in annual volume as you have capital.’ And they look at our $10 billion in insurance capital and say, ‘That's $20 billion a year. What are you doing writing only $1 billion?’ But then ... somebody else comes in and asks, ‘Why did everybody get killed last year but you?’ Maybe the questions are related.” (Damn Right!, p. 241)
- **HIM** — Would not do another USAir. “But we're not looking for another experience like it.” (See DECISIONS, USAir.) (Damn Right!, p. 246)
- **HIM** — No option of forgiveness. On Gutfreund's failure to paint a complete picture for the board: “we had no option of forgiveness.” (Damn Right!, p. 262)
- **HIM** — Would not sell the Daily Journal. “We have no intention of selling at any price,” (to Steven Brill, 1986). AUTHOR (p. 279): Munger “maintains he will never close the paper down.” (Damn Right!, pp. 277, 279)
- **REPORTED (Leeka)** — Would not squeeze revenue at the hospital. “There are tricks a hospital can do to squeeze extra revenue from low-income and Medi-Cal billings, but Munger won't allow that,” and no FEMA claim after the Northridge earthquake. (See DECISIONS.) (Damn Right!, p. 293)
- **HIM** — Contempt for the victim mentality. “I can't imagine a more destructive frame of mind. Yet whole university departments want everyone to feel like a victim. And you pay money to send your children to these places. And this is what they teach them!” (Damn Right!, p. 298)
- **HIM** — Would not accept an undefined cost of capital. Questioning Professor Bratton on “optimal investment”: “No, I do not. At least not as these people use the term.” “Well, it's only fair, if you're going to use the cost of capital, to say what it is.” “No, I want to know what the cost of capital is in the model.” “Where? Out of the forehead of job or something?” (The .txt prints “job”.) “But if you explain things in terms of unexplainable subconcepts, what kind of an explanation is that?” And later: “It's just the cost of capital thing that always makes me go into orbit.” (Damn Right!, pp. 303–304)
- **HIM** — Called modern portfolio theory demented; would not take luck as the explanation of Buffett. “Modern portfolio theory? It's demented!” “All I can say is, if he believes that, I've got a bridge I'd like to sell him.” (See RULES.) (Damn Right!, p. 309) Also in, HIM, as printed (the .txt is garbled around it): “you should look at corporate finance portfolio theory? It's demented. It's truly amazing” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 289)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Called the emphasis on volatility nonsense; ignored Beta. “This great emphasis on volatility in corporate finance we regard as nonsense ... ” AUTHOR: “they ignore the popular financial indicator called Beta”. (Damn Right!, pp. 309–310)
- **HIM** — Called stock-option accounting corrupt. “Accounting for stock options in America is corrupt, and it's not a good idea to have corrupt accounting.” And: “it's very important not to let little corruptions start because they become big corruptions-and then you have vested interests that fight to perpetuate them.” (Damn Right!, p. 310) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 388 (the editors' box, words attributed to Charlie): “The right way to behave is to never let improper accounting start.” and p. 400 (HIM, Talk Eight Revisited); see RULES.
- **HIM** — Unmoved by the mutual-fund business. “I am not charmed with the mechanics of that business.” On endowments put into funds of funds: “Few would have predicted that, long after Cornfeld's fall into disgrace, major universities would be leading foundations into Cornfeld's system.” (Damn Right!, p. 312) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 348 (Talk Six).
- **HIM (letter, on Buffett's behalf)** — Would not tell a buyer to buy Berkshire at the then price. To Five Sigma: asked by a friend or family member whether he advised a new purchase of Berkshire shares at the current price, Mr. Buffett “would answer, No.” (See DECISIONS, pp. 313–315.) (Damn Right!, p. 314)
- **HIM** — Would not control a tobacco company. “We don't want to be in the business of selling addictive drugs, with us being the controlling owners,” “But we'll never have Berkshire in a controlling interest.” Would not be “a moral censor for the world” when buying a little piece of a company in an insurance portfolio. (See DECISIONS, pp. 315–316.) (Damn Right!, pp. 315–316)
- **REPORTED (Williams)** — Would not live in the mansion he built on Hot Springs Road. “Naw, I'd never live in a place like that.” (See DECISIONS, p. 330.) (Damn Right!, p. 330)
- **REPORTED (Molly)** — The advice he never gave. “We heard a lot of messages about how emotions can lead you to do dumb things. I'm trying to think of a time he said, ‘Go with your gut, feel the vibes. Lose yourself in the moment.’ That's not where he is.” (Damn Right!, p. 330)
- **HIM** — Would avoid the passionate ideologue in academic hiring. “professors of super strong, passionate political ideology, whether on the left or right, should usually be avoided. So also for students.” He calls the Yale law school, “then viewed by many at Harvard as trying to improve legal education by importing a particular political ideology as a dominant factor,” “a wonderful example of just such ideology-based folly.” (Damn Right!, p. 352) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 337–338 (Talk Five).
- **HIM** — Would not sell syrup to bottlers on a perpetual franchise at a frozen price (the Glotz plan). A bottler should be “a subcontractor, not a vendee of syrup, and certainly not a vendee of syrup under a perpetual franchise specifying a syrup price frozen forever at its starting level.” (Damn Right!, p. 362) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 310–311 (Talk Four).
- **HIM** — Would not change the flavor (the Glotz plan). “Even if a new flavor performs better in blind taste tests, changing to that new flavor would be a foolish thing to do.” Also: “we must avoid ever losing even half of our powerful trademarked name.” (See RULES, pp. 362–363.) (Damn Right!, p. 363) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 312–313 (Talk Four).
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — Would not accept the usual reading of the Pharisee and the Publican. “I have always refused to accept one interpretation of the Parable of the Pharisee and the Publican in the christian Bible. under this interpretation, a learned, pious man, after a long life spent doing his duty, is criticized for rejoicing that he didn't end up like another man who behaved much worse and fell into a low moral and worldly condition.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 33)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — Shared Cicero's distaste for mob rule. “His underlying philosophical view was one of deep and realistic cynicism about human nature, including a distaste for pure mob rule and demagogues.” Munger: “I later came to share Cicero's governmental views.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 31)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — Would not recommend a book to a general reader. Of Trollope's biography of Cicero: “At one time I even skimmed Trollope's biography of Cicero in the course of deciding never to recommend it to anyone not already a Cicero addict.” (the .txt prints “'Trollope's”.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 32)
- **HIM (quoted by the compilers; source not given)** — Called single-model thinking dumb. “This is a dumb way of handling problems.” (Of trying to solve all problems with one model.) And on those who cannot think in tradeoffs: “you're a horse's patoot. You clearly are a danger to the rest of the people when serious thinking is being done.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 61, 64)
- **HIM (quoted by the compilers; source not given)** — Would not be confident a policy does more good than harm. “What I'm against is being very confident and feeling that you know, for sure, that your particular action will do more good than harm.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 70)
- **HIM (as the compilers give it)** — Would not do cocaine, race trains, or “AIDS situations.” “Don't do cocaine. Don't race trains. And avoid AIDS situations.” (2004 Berkshire meeting.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 69)
- **HIM (2004 Wesco meeting)** — Would not manipulate a quarter. “we're not willing to manipulate in any way just to make some quarter look a little better.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 88)
- **HIM (2004 Wesco meeting)** — Would not go near the line of what is legal. “We believe you shouldn't go anywhere near that line.” “there should be all kinds of things you won't do even though they're perfectly legal.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 89)
- **HIM (Harvard Faculty Club, 1994)** — Would not go to closed-bid auctions. “We don't go to the closed bid auctions either, but for a different reason, one which Zeckhauser would understand.” AUTHOR (compilers' answer, p. 90): “The problem, a different one, with closed bid auctions is that they are frequently won by people making a technical mistake, as in the case of Shell Paying double for Belridge Oil. You can't pay double the losing bid at an open outcry auction.” Also: “closed bid auctions invite a possibility for big mispricing errors.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 90)
- **REPORTED (Guerin)** — Would not take advantage of partners or old ladies. “taking advantage of a cheap stock price on the stock exchange is one thing, but taking advantage of partners or old ladies is something else-something Charlie just doesn't do.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 88)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Would not use leverage as Murdoch did. “If Berkshire had used even half the leverage of, say, Rupert Murdoch, it would be five times its current size.” (Berkshire's choice not to.) And: “We're going to try to compound it at a reasonable rate with or-rt taking unreasonable risk of using leverage.” (p. 98; ‘or-rt’ for ‘without’.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 96, 98)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Would not talk the stock up. “When it runs up, we try to talk it down.” “This leads to all sorts of foolish behavior. we want to tell it like it is.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 98)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Would not try to do acquisitions. “Ours work because we don't try to do acquisitions-we wait for no-brainers.” “Anytime you sit there waiting for a deal to come by, you're in a very dangerous seat.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 99)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Would not hire consultants, or have CEOs spend time on the shareholders. “Can you imagine Carnegie hiring consultants?!” (p. 97); “we promised them they could spend one hundred percent of their time on their businesses.” (p. 100) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 97, 100)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Would not claim synergy. “The reason we avoid the word "synergy" is because people generally claim more synergistic benefits than will come. Yes, it exists, but there are so many false promises.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 101)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Would not run a fund against a benchmark or fully invested. “If I was offered the chance to go into a business where people would measure me against benchmarks, force me to be fully invested, crawl around looking over my shoulder, etc., I would hate it. I would regard it as putting me into shackles.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 108)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Beta and modern portfolio theory. “Beta and modern portfolio theory and the like none of it makes any sense to me. We're trying to buy businesses with sustainable competitive advantages at a low, or even a fair, price.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 109)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Would not be short. “Being short and seeing a promoter take the stock up is very irritating. It's not worth it to have that much irritation in your life.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 112)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Forecasters. “People have always had this craving to have someone tell them the future. Long ago, kings would hire people to read sheep guts. There's always been a market for people who pretend to know the future. Listening to today's forecasters is just as crazy as when the king hired the guy to look at the sheep guts.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 114)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — IPOs. “But the average person is going to get creamed. So if you think you're talented, good luck. IPOs are too small for us, or too high tech-we won't understand them.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 115)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Extraordinary charges. “If it happens every year like clockwork, what's so extraordinary about it?” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 119)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Called EBITDA “bullshit earnings.” “I think that, every time you see the word EBITDA [earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, you should substitute the words "bullshit earnings."” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 124)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Stock options as pay. “To give a lot of options to a CEO who built the business and is in his sixties, to incent loyalty, is demented. Would the doctors at the Mayo Clinic or the lawyers at Cravath who are in their sixties work harder if they had options?” (p. 126) “Black-Scholes is a know-nothing system.” (p. 126) “A stock option is both an expense and dilution To argue anything else is insane.” (p. 127) “I'd rather make my money playing piano in a whorehouse than account for, options as recommended by John Doerr.” (p. 127; the .txt prints the comma.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 126–127)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Leveraged financial institutions. “We are exceptionally goosey of leveraged financial institutions. If they start talking about how good risk management is, it makes us nervous.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 128)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Derivatives accounting. “It [accounting for derivatives] is just disgusting. It is a sewer, and if I'm right, there will be hell to pay in due course.” “To say accounting for derivatives in America is a sewer is an insult to sewage.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 131)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Would not be in businesses the tort system punishes. “Because of the tort system, there are whole areas for the already rich to avoid.” “It would be crazy for Berkshire to be in the business of providing security guards at airports. The system discourages the best companies from entering certain businesses.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 133)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Academia. “There's A lot wrong [with American universities]. I'd remove three-fourths of the faculty-everything but the hard sciences. But nobody's going to do that, so we will have to live with the defects.” (p. 139) “I think there is a modern name for this approach that whitehead did not like and that name is bonkers.” (p. 139; the .txt is a lower-case run-on.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 138–139)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Compensation consultants. “I would rather throw a viper down my shirt front then hire a compensation consultant” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 143)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994)** — Would not play in high tech. “However, Berkshire Hathaway, by and large, does not invest in these people that are "surfing" on complicated technology. After all, we're cranky and idiosyncratic as you may have noticed.” “And Warren and I don't feel like we have any great advantage in the high-tech sector. In fact, we feel like we're at a big disadvantage in trying to understand the nature of technical developments in software, computer chips, or what have you. So we tend to avoid that stuff, based on our personal inadequacies.” “The fact that we don't think we're very good at it and have pretty well stayed out of it doesn't mean that it's irrational for you to do it.” (p. 192) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 191–192)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994)** — Would not call the extreme efficient-market view anything but “bonkers.” “And, by the way, I have a name for people who went to the extreme efficient market theory-which is "bonkers."” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 194)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994)** — Would not discuss other markets. “I don't want to get into emerging markets, bond arbitrage, and so forth. I'm talking about nothing but plain vanilla stock picking. That, believe me, is complicated enough.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 193)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994, continued)** — Would not buy a tax shelter or anything sold on a big commission and a long prospectus. “Warren and I personally don't drill oil wells. We pay our taxes.” “Anytime somebody offers you a tax shelter from here on in life, my advice would be don't buy it.” (p. 212) “anytime anybody offers you anything with a big commission and a 200 page prospectus, don't buy it.” (p. 213) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 212–213)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994, continued)** — Knows no rich sector rotator. “I know of no really rich sector rotator.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 202)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994, continued)** — Would not call it a general system. “We have no system for having automatic good judgment on all investment decisions that can be made. Ours is a totally different system.” “We just look for no-brainer decisions. As Buffett and I say over and over again, we don't leap seven-foot fences.” (p. 254) “So, once again, we don't have any system for giving you perfect investment judgment on all subjects at all times. That would be ridiculous.” (p. 255) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 254–255)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Would not give a one-size strategy. “I don't think there's a one-size-fits-all investment strategY that I can give you.” (the .txt prints “strategY”.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 266)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Would not predict the market as a whole. “Warren and I are almost always agnostic about the market.” (p. 268) (See also RULES, same page, on Graham.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 268)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994, continued)** — Does not like technology; IBM as the example. “IBM got bounced off the wave after ‘surfing’ successfully for sixty years. And that was some collapse-an object lesson in the difficulties of technology and one of the reasons why Buffett and Munger don't like technology very much. We don't think we're any good at it, and strange things can happen.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 203)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Would not be in high tech. “The reason we're not in high-tech businesses is that we have a special lack of aptitude in that area.” (p. 258) (See RULES, same page.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 258)
- **HIM (Talk Three)** — Does not use Value Line's predictions. “I don't use Value Line's predictions because our system works better for us than theirs-in fact, a lot better.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 233)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Would not gamble against the house. “I won't bet $100 against house odds between now and the grave. I don't do that. Why should I?” “As for gambling with simple mechanical house odds against me, why in the world would I ever want to do that-particularly given how I detest the manipulative culture of legalized gambling. So I don't like legalized gambling.” (p. 263) “I don't like to be with many of the types who hang around card parlors and so forth.” “But I do not like the professional gambling milieu.” (p. 264) What he does: “I will gamble recreationally with my pals. And I'll occasionally play a much better bridge player, like Bob Hamman, who might be the best card player in the world. But I know I'm paving for the fun of playing with him. That's recreational.” (p. 263; the .txt prints “paving” for “paying”.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 263–264)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Would not write the book himself. “I don't have much interest in writing a book myself. Plus it would be a lot of work for somebody like me to try and do it in my seventies. And I have plenty else to do in life. So I'm not going to do it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 291)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Would not use psychology to manipulate beyond moral limits. “Not all of what you know how to do should you use to manipulate people.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 253)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Would give no compensation for stress. As printed: “If I were running the civilization, compensation for stress in Workers compensation would be zero” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 284)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — What he hates most. “What I personally hate most are systems that make fraud easy. Probably way more than half of all the chiropractic income in California comes from pure fraud.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 283)
- **HIM (Talk Three)** — Called Chomsky's hesitation daft. “Well, the junior professor is clearly right-and Chomsky's hesitation is a little daft.” On why: “Chomsky is passionately ideological. He is an extreme egalitarian leftist who happens to be a genius. And he's so smart that he realized that if he concedes this particular Darwinian point, the implications threaten his leftist ideology. So he naturally has his conclusion affected by his ideological bias.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 237)
- **HIM (Talk Three)** — Called two ideologues daft. “People like Dornan on the right or Nader on the left have obviously gone a little daft. They're extreme examples of what ideology will do to you particularly violently expressed ideology.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 238)
- **HIM (Talk Three)** — Called the psychology texts daft; has taken no psychology course. “In fact, I've never taken a single course in psychology. However, I've come to exactly the same conclusion-that the psychology texts, while they are wonderful in part, are also significantly daft.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 239)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Called the schools of education a disgrace. “And they're almost an intellectual disgrace.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 292)
- **HIM (Talk Three Q and A)** — Doubts the quest for money is laudable. “I'm not sure the quest for money is so laudable.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 296)
- **HIM (the “Nothing to Add” Number Four box; occasion not given)** — Would not use a computer. Question (the editors' framing, not his words): “Do you use a computer?” Answer, HIM: “I don't. I do have one in my office, but I've never turned it on. In fact, I wouldn't even know how to plug it in.” “In my life there are not many questions I can't properly deal with using my $40 adding machine and dog-eared compound interest table.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 320)
- **HIM (Talk Four Revisited, 2006)** — Avoids people who want to talk about the meaning of meaning. “I have always avoided all people who want to converse at length about the ‘meaning of meaning.’” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 321)
- **HIM (Talk Six)** — Called the orthodox view of diversification grossly mistaken. “I think the orthodox view is grossly mistaken.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 356)
- **HIM (Talk Seven)** — Never took an economics course; never tried to make money from macro forecasts. “I should concede, at the outset, that ‘wealth effects’ are part of the academic discipline of economics and that I have never taken a single course in economics, nor tried to make a single dollar, ever, from foreseeing macroeconomic changes.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 365)
- **HIM (Talk Seven)** — Called his audience's professors mistaken. “Your mistaken professors were too much influenced by ‘rational man’ models of human behavior from economics and too little by ‘foolish man’ models from psychology and real-world experience.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 375)

## 4. SIGNALS

- **HIM — trust.** The sign: a client who “treated his customers right, his suppliers right, and his employees right.” What it meant: the behaviour he wanted as the example for himself and wanted in clients. Quote: “The lesson helped me prefer McFayden types as clients and McFayden behavior as the right example for myself.” (Damn Right!, pp. 88–89) Also in: HIM, in his father's wording: “Grant McFadyen treats his employees right, his customers right, and his problems right.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 274)
- **HIM — a good business.** The sign: decisions. “good businesses throw up one easy decision after another. The bad businesses throw up painful decisions time after time.” (.txt OCR prints “had”.) (Damn Right!, p. 91)
- **HIM — suspicion.** The sign: a business bought in the hope it “liquidates at a profit just before it is destined to go broke.” What it meant: no fun, not the kind he wanted. (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **HIM — suspicion of a great asset.** The sign: an excellent, low-cost mine that someone else owns. What it meant: the owner knows. “it is already owned by someone who knows it's a very nice mine.” (Damn Right!, p. 88)
- **HIM (story he told) — suspicion.** The sign: a manufacturer of flashy lures. AUTHOR frames the story as Munger “musing on the gullibility of many investors.” Quote: “This fishing tackle manufacturer I knew had all these flashy green and purple lures. I asked, “Do fish take these?” “Charlie,” he said, “I don't sell these lures to fish.”” (Damn Right!, pp. 37–38)
- **REPORTED (Munger recalling Harvey Mudd) — trust.** The sign: a client who wanted the right thing, not the legal minimum. The words are Mudd's: “I don't want to know merely what the law is and what I can accomplish without violating the law. I welcome your help in doing rightly, all factors considered.” (Damn Right!, p. 87)
- **HIM — trust (a boss).** On senior partner Joe Peeler: “No wonder I liked him,” “Also, like me, he tended to delegate any task completely or do it all himself, and I liked his total delegation mode.” (Damn Right!, p. 87)
- **HIM — trust (a man in trouble).** On hiring Al Marshall: “I knew he was a great guy and he needed something else to do.” (Damn Right!, p. 100)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (clients).** “The problem with law, Munger felt, is that the people he most enjoyed working with didn't get in much legal trouble, and the people who needed him most sometimes were defective characters.” (Damn Right!, p. 89)
- **AUTHOR — market signal.** On the first condominium project: ground-floor units “sold out quickly, but the upstairs units seemed to sell molasses slow.” Response: next projects single-level. (Damn Right!, p. 98)
- **AUTHOR — the other party's choice.** The shopping-center owner took cash up front instead of half the profit: “which turned out to be a mistake.” (Damn Right!, p. 99)
- **AUTHOR — industry.** “While Charlie was at first fascinated with industrial companies, he found that making money in manufacturing was fraught with danger.” (Damn Right!, p. 95)
- **HIM — a place.** On finding lunch: “Usually a shopping center has some place,” (Damn Right!, p. 93)
- **AUTHOR — a fellow director's remark.** Garrett: “Charlie said that deep down, he knew Garrett didn't like him very well.” (Damn Right!, p. 88)
- **REPORTED (Booth)** — someone he trusted on instinct. “I, at least, found we thought along parallel lines. If he started to say something, I knew what he was going to say.” (Booth is describing Munger, not Munger's view of Booth.) (Damn Right!, p. 96)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (a partner's habit).** Jack Wheeler, REPORTED (Marshall): “he also would suffer big reverses once in a while, which did not appeal to Charlie.” What it meant: Munger took the operation away from him (see DECISIONS, p. 139). (Damn Right!, p. 139)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (a manager).** Roy Garrett brought in a man “who Charlie described as a ‘control freak.' Munger was among the seasoned attorneys who finally got fed up.” (Damn Right!, p. 121)
- **HIM — suspicion (a seller of funds).** The sign: a fund that raised $60 million and kept $54 million for investment after commissions. What it meant: “It was as if,” “the customers had asked their brokers ‘What shall I do with my money?' and the brokers had responded: ‘First, give 10 percent of it to me.' ” (Damn Right!, p. 145)
- **AUTHOR — a bargain.** Closed-end funds trading well below net asset value: the Fund of Letters (“traded well below its net asset value”), Source Capital ($18 per share asset value, trading at $9). Wesco: shares “in the low teens, less than half its book value.” Source Capital's managers: “considerable talent and a mind-set similar to that of Munger and Buffett.” (Damn Right!, pp. 145, 159)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (a deal's terms).** Wesco's merger: Wesco shareholders “to swap their undervalued shares for those of overvalued Financial Corp.” REPORTED (Buffett): “I read these terms, and I didn't believe them. And I told Munger the terms and he couldn't believe it either.” (Damn Right!, pp. 159–160)
- **AUTHOR — trust (straight talk).** Vincenti, Wesco's president, told Munger Blue Chip could vote against the merger, but “the outcome would be determined by shareholders, not Vincenti. This was the kind of straight talk Munger liked. He immediately developed an admiration for Vincenti.” HIM later: “He was brilliant, principled, decisive, and parsimonious,” (Damn Right!, pp. 160, 168)
- **HIM — suspicion (complexity).** What the SEC read in Blue Chip's ownership structure, and Munger's own statement of the rule: “People assume if what you're doing is enormously complicated, you're probably doing something wrong.” (Damn Right!, pp. 161–162)
- **REPORTED (Hal Borthwick) — suspicion of the early deals.** “Those guys were still cigar butt investors. They were, you know, looking for assets on the cheap.” (Damn Right!, p. 165)
- **REPORTED (Carol Loomis) — a good business versus a bad one.** Loomis: when he met Buffett, Munger “had already formed strong opinions about the chasms between good businesses and bad. He served as a director of an International Harvester dealership in Bakersfield and saw how difficult it was to fix up an intrinsically mediocre business; as an Angeleno, he observed the splendid prosperity of the Los Angeles Times; in his head he did not carry a creed about ‘bargains' that had to be unlearned.” (The .txt prints “lie” for “He”.) (Damn Right!, p. 115)
- **REPORTED (Buffett, tying Munger) — the shift.** “Charlie shoved me in the direction of not just buying bargains, as Ben Graham had taught me.” (Buffett on what Munger did to his thinking.) (Damn Right!, p. 116)
- **HIM — trust (a business culture).** See's: “The ordinary candy company puts in too many stores,” “But See's has always had the discipline of knowing their own business.” “And of course the fanaticism about the quality of the product and service is the heart and soul of the business.” (Damn Right!, p. 173)
- **HIM — trust (suppliers and customers).** “You get suppliers who are good and who are trusted because they deserve trust, and you behave the same way toward your own customer, then you are a little part of a civilization that is a seamless web of deserved trust.” (Damn Right!, p. 173)
- **HIM — a good business.** Businesses that “just drown in money every year.” (Damn Right!, p. 183)
- **AUTHOR — industry change.** Buffett and Munger say newspapers “are no longer the bulletproof franchises they used to be, since technology such as television and the Internet has changed the way people get their information and has dimmed newspaper prospects.” AUTHOR: “In fact, says Munger, the Internet will increase competition and make it hard for all companies to show a profit.” (Damn Right!, p. 199)
- **HIM — suspicion of a building.** On the Buffalo plant: “Why does a newspaper need a palace to publish in?” AUTHOR: balconies unusable in windy Buffalo, “artsy-craftsy” construction that caused unfixable leaks, “all at great cost.” (Damn Right!, p. 195)
- **REPORTED (Nancy) — trust (his judgment of people).** “He judges people pretty well-which ones to join with and which ones not to join,” “He has avoided attaching himself to people who are problems-that's helpful.” (Damn Right!, p. 121)
- **REPORTED (Booth) — trust.** On putting money into Wheeler, Munger: “I knew Charlie well enough.” (Damn Right!, p. 142)
- **REPORTED (Marshall) — trust.** “If he trusts you, he trusts you completely,” (Damn Right!, p. 139)
- **AUTHOR — trust (no paper).** “There never had been a written contract covering the terms of their work together. Rather, Munger and Buffett simply went forward on trust.” (Damn Right!, p. 168)
- **AUTHOR + HIM — what he admires (a membership rule).** AUTHOR: Charlie admires the Hillcrest Country Club's practice “of demanding a generous charitable giving history as a condition of membership.” HIM: “I have heard that, long ago, a big-time theater owner tried to join, delivering a yellowing newspaper clipping reporting on a World War II savings bond rally held in one of his theaters. Back came the reply of the membership committee: ‘This is a very useful piece of paper. For instance, you could wipe your ass with it. But it won't get you into this Club.' ” (Damn Right!, pp. 138–139)
- **HIM — suspicion (a trade association).** The sign: League lobbying that resisted reform. What it meant to him: the League had “clearly misled its government for a long time, to the taxpayers' great detriment”. Quote: “the League's recent lobbying efforts regularly resist minimal reform.” (Damn Right!, pp. 212–213)
- **HIM — suspicion (what ruined the S&Ls, Wesco 1990 report).** The signs, in his list of five causes: “(1) competitive pressure on the ‘spread’ between interest paid and interest received put on associations and banks when federal deposit insurance is provided to entities free to pay any interest rates they wish in order to attract deposits, (2) loose asset deployment rules for associations, (3) admission and retention of crooks and fools as managers of associations without regulatory objection, (4) general real estate calamities in certain big regions, and (5) continuous irresponsible protection and enhancement of unsoundness by the savings and loan lobby and certain members of Congress beholden to the most despicable savings and loan operators.” (Damn Right!, pp. 213–214)
- **HIM — suspicion (financial engineering).** The sign: subdividing mortgage pools into ever finer contractual fractions. Quote: “At this rate, subdivision will soon get down to quarks.” (Damn Right!, p. 214)
- **HIM — suspicion (a stock price).** The sign: a stock price above liquidation value. Quote: “An orangutan could figure out that the stock is selling miles above the value of the company if it were liquidated.” (Damn Right!, p. 220)
- **HIM + AUTHOR — suspicion (a business).** The sign: a small New England textile enterprise. Quote: Munger described Berkshire as “a small, doomed New England textile enterprise”; AUTHOR: “and he was correct.” (Damn Right!, p. 225)
- **REPORTED (Buffett) — suspicion (earning power).** The sign: earning power that is “hypothetical” (“Texas Instruments or Polaroid”). What it meant: Buffett credits Munger with making him look for earning power “only when you can be sure of it”. (Damn Right!, p. 238)
- **HIM + AUTHOR — suspicion (an industry).** The sign: an airline. Quote: “marginal cost with wings.” AUTHOR: Munger said that once an airline files for bankruptcy, “it is then operating debt free, thus it can be more competitive than a solvent one.” HIM on the unions and competitors: “all those unions that could only think about reality from their own point of view. And the idiot competition-including bankrupt competitors who stiff-armed their creditors while they lost money ruining our business.” (Damn Right!, pp. 244–246)
- **HIM — trust (a franchise).** The sign: customers hooked on a store. Quote: “If you get hooked on going to Costco with your family, you'll go for the rest of your life,” AUTHOR: “One of the reasons Munger likes Costco is because of Jim Sinegal, the company's president, whose office has no walls separating him from passing employees.” AUTHOR: “Charlie absolutely crows at the story of Costco's paper towels, a story that to Munger represents an admirable example of business ethics”; when the towels didn't match the guarantee, “they were withdrawn until a suitable towel could be sold.” (Damn Right!, pp. 249–250)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (Salomon's talking points).** The sign: passive voice in a press statement (“one part of the problem has been known since late April”). What it meant: “Munger objected to the use of the passive voice, and demanded to be told who exactly knew.” REPORTED (Carol Loomis, Fortune, quoted by the author): “Munger didn't like it, finding this behavior neither candid nor smart. But not considering himself an expert on ‘funding,’ he subsided.” HIM: “Warren and I didn't see John's downfall” that first evening. (Damn Right!, p. 255)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (a board's excuse).** The sign: a press release saying management had waited nearly four months because of the “press of other business.” What it meant: “That lame excuse, the board felt, would not fly. The wording was changed”. (The text says “the board”, not Munger by name.) (Damn Right!, p. 256)
- **AUTHOR — trust, then distrust (Gutfreund).** Trust: “Gutfruend impressed Warren and Charlie when in 1987 Salomon took a large trading loss, and then in a restructuring laid off 800 employees. That year Gutfreund declined a bonus worth about $2 million.” AUTHOR (Lowenstein, quoted): “To Charlie Munger, Gutfreund evoked all that was noble in Salomon's tribal culture, particularly its willingness to lay its capital on the line.” Distrust: “they were disturbed that at board meetings, directors were given out-of-date balance sheets.” (Damn Right!, pp. 259–260)
- **HIM — suspicion (a manager who hands the problem up).** The sign: Meriwether reported Mozer's trading to Gutfreund and added “I hope you can figure out a way to save this fine young man”; Gutfreund was “indecisive-‘How can I save my profit center, this employee, etc., etc.’ He procrastinated and didn't level with the board until the Fed threatened him-and then he still didn't fully disclose.” What he said the general counsel should have told him: “Tell the truth, tell it fully, tell it fast.” “He would have had to cashier the trader, but he got cashiered anyway.” AUTHOR: Munger called Gutfreund's reaction a “thumb-sucking response.” (Damn Right!, pp. 259–260)
- **HIM — suspicion (information withheld from directors).** The sign: a letter from the New York Fed's president went to outside lawyers and not to directors. Quote: “He gave it to outside lawyers but didn't send a copy to the directors,” “Warren and I went into that crisis without knowing all the facts. We got mildly nebulous responses. If we'd known, we would have worked it differently.” AUTHOR: “his credibility with the board of directors was destroyed.” (Damn Right!, pp. 260–261)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (pay against results).** The sign: bonuses unrelated to the unit's results, 106 people at $1 million or more in a below-average year (Buffett's figure, in the company's quarterly report); “some issues, such as the granting of excessive bonuses, were never resolved to Munger's and Buffett's full satisfaction.” (Damn Right!, pp. 265–266)
- **REPORTED (Simpson) — what Munger looked for on the audit committee.** “He seized on tough issues and came back to them.” (The .txt prints “hack”.) “He spent the whole time reading and re-reading audit material. He added a lot of value in just questioning practices and the way to think about these practices.” (Damn Right!, p. 264)
- **HIM — suspicion (overconfidence).** The sign: high IQs, high leverage, and self-appraisals of superior methods (LTCM). Quote: “relying on their self-appraisals that they have superior talents and methods.” (Damn Right!, p. 269) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 352 (Talk Six).
- **REPORTED (Marshall) + AUTHOR — suspicion (newspapers).** REPORTED (Marshall): “Marshall pointed out that neither Munger nor Buffett like investing in newspapers as much as they once did.” AUTHOR (p. 282): “newspapers aren't as lucrative as they once were”; legal advertising “has always been cyclical” and is “a slowly shrinking revenue source”. HIM on the Daily Journal's litigation (p. 273): “Berkshire has practically no litigation,” “But if you take our legal newspapers, I don't think a year passes without litigation. Discrimination, sex, old age, race. It is very litigious. The Metropolitan News is suing us now regarding the foreclosure business. It gets to be a bit of a sewer.” (Damn Right!, pp. 273, 277, 282)
- **HIM + AUTHOR — trust (a manager).** The sign: Salzman, “a known, tested, and trusted person”; HIM: “talented and so honest”. (See DECISIONS, p. 280.) (Damn Right!, p. 280)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (a hospital).** The sign: a board that always backed the medical staff, whose decisions “frequently protected the economic interests of certain doctors”; a cardiovascular department “rife with political intrigue, turf protection, and not enough attention to medical issues.” (Damn Right!, pp. 291–292)
- **AUTHOR — suspicion (inflated claims).** The sign: other hospitals claiming earthquake damage for “every old crack in their cement”; Good Sam found no significant damage and Munger would not file. (Damn Right!, p. 293)
- **HIM — trust (a company run by a fanatic).** The sign: an early annual report in which “an idiot could tell that here was a talented fanatic-very favorably located.” (National Cash Register under Patterson.) What it meant: “Therefore, the investment decision was easy.” (Damn Right!, p. 311)
- **HIM — suspicion (an undefined number in a model).** The sign: a model that uses the cost of capital without saying what it is. Bratton: “In the model? It will just be stated.” Munger: “Where? Out of the forehead of job or something?” and “if you explain things in terms of unexplainable subconcepts, what kind of an explanation is that?” (The .txt prints “job”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 303–304)
- **HIM — what to ask of a business with fabulous results.** The sign: results that look fabulous. What it meant: ask how long they can last. “that's to think about why the results are occurring now-and then to figure out the forces that could cause those results to stop occurring.” (Damn Right!, p. 307)
- **HIM — a franchise, and its price.** The sign: a brand people reach for without thinking (Wrigley's). What it meant: “It's not worth it for you to think about buying an alternative gum.” The caution: when “everybody can see that they're wonderful businesses,” it trades at “eight times book value or something. And everything else is at three times book value.” (Damn Right!, p. 308)
- **AUTHOR (what he said he remembers) — suspicion (a business that looks invincible).** The sign: downtown department stores that “seemed invincible”: “They offered enormous selections, had large purchasing power, and owned the highest priced real estate in town, the corners where the streetcar lines crossed.” What it meant: “as time passed, private cars became the prevalent mode of transportation. The streetcar lines were taken out, customers moved to the suburbs and shopping centers became the dominant shopping venues.” AUTHOR: “Some simple changes in the way we live can completely alter the long-term value of a business.” (Damn Right!, p. 308)
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED (Bogle) — suspicion (layers of fees).** The sign: consultants hired to hire money managers who pick funds run by other money managers; AUTHOR: “At each step, there is a commission to be paid, which skims off the money that can be used in the work of the charity.” HIM: “And they get these fees just for maintaining shares in place”. REPORTED (Bogle, on Munger's point): “Market returns-5 percent; total cost, 3 percent; net return, 2 percent.” (Damn Right!, p. 312)
- **HIM — suspicion (the adviser's incentives).** The sign: a narrow professional adviser outside your own territory. What it meant: “It is not usually the conscious malfeasance of your narrow professional adviser that does you in. Instead, your troubles come from his subconscious bias. His cognition will often be impaired, for your purposes, by financial incentives different from yours.” (The .txt prints “cone” and “he impaired”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 356–357) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 301 (Talk Four) and p. 325 (Talk Five).
- **HIM — suspicion (little corruptions).** The sign: accounting that hides a cost (stock options). What it meant: “it's very important not to let little corruptions start because they become big corruptions-and then you have vested interests that fight to perpetuate them.” (Damn Right!, p. 310) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 388 (the editors' box, words attributed to Charlie): “The right way to behave is to never let improper accounting start.”
- **HIM — suspicion (passion in a thinker).** The sign: a professor or student of “super strong, passionate political ideology”. What it meant: “Best-form multidisciplinarity requires an objectivity such passionate people have lost, and a difficult synthesis is not likely to be achieved by minds in ideological fetters.” (Damn Right!, p. 352) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 337–338 (Talk Five).
- **REPORTED (Molly) — trust.** “After something is checked out and you can feel safe, he gets very devoted to people,” (The opening quotation mark is lost in the .txt, so the attribution rests on the closing “said Molly”.) (Damn Right!, pp. 330–331)
- **HIM (as the author gives it) — suspicion (a shipyard).** The sign: trouble three months into the build at the original Florida yard. What he called it: “rogues, scallywags and pirates in Florida.” REPORTED (Williams): “I went down, things weren't going well,” (See DECISIONS, p. 326.) (Damn Right!, p. 326)
- **HIM — suspicion (a company's own choices, Coca-Cola).** The sign: the real company “did lose half its trademark and did grant perpetual bottling franchises at fixed syrup prices.” What it meant: it “did lose much pricing control that would have improved results, had it been retained.” (Damn Right!, p. 364) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 313 (Talk Four).
- **HIM — suspicion (educated people who cannot explain a success).** The sign: educators “who can't satisfactorily explain Coca-Cola, even in retrospect, and even after watching it closely all their lives,” and executives who “did not understand elementary psychology well enough to predict and avoid the ‘New Coke’ fiasco”. (Damn Right!, p. 364) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 314–315 (Talk Four).
- **AUTHOR — trust (a handshake).** Of Munger and Buffett: “There was no formal partnership or contractual relationship-the bond was created by a handshake and backed by two Midwesterners who understood and respected the value of one's word.” No quote from him. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 23–24)
- **AUTHOR — trust (a likeness to Munger).** Dr. Davis, Munger's father's best friend, met Buffett and “agreed to place a large part of their life savings-$100,000-with him. Why? The doctor explained that Warren reminded him of Charlie Munger.” (The signal is Davis's, as the author tells it; no quote from Munger.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 19)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (passion in a manager).** The sign: “Berkshire is full of people who have a peculiar passion for their own business. I would argue passion is more important than brain power.” (The .txt runs the first sentence into the question “What matters most passion or competence that was inborn?”.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 99)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (a manager who admits earlier error).** The sign: people he admires with power under him, “And they create the culture for whatever invention and reality recognition is going on in their businesses. And included in that reality recognition is the recognition that previous conclusions were incorrect.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 100)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (old-fashioned businesses).** “A lot of the businesses we buy are kind of cranky and old-fashioned like us.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 97)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a CEO's duty is to make the stock go up).** The sign: “Today, it seems to be regarded as the duty of CEOs to make the stock go up This leads to all sorts of foolish behavior.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 98)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a seat waiting for deals).** The sign: sitting and waiting for the acquisition to come by. “Anytime you sit there waiting for a deal to come by, you're in a very dangerous seat.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 99)
- **AUTHOR (compilers' description of his screens) — suspicion (promoted deals).** “many investor favourites such as pharmaceuticals and Technology for example go straight to the too tough to understand basket”; “heavily promoted Deals And IPOs earn an immediate no.” (The .txt for this page is run together in places; quoted as printed. The compilers' wording, not his.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 71–72)
- **AUTHOR (compilers' description of his screens) — what he checks in management.** “He especially assesses a company's management well beyond conventional number crunching-in particular, the degree to which they are ‘able, trustworthy, and owner-oriented.’ For example, how do they deploy cash? Do they allocate it intelligently on behalf of the owners, or do they overcompensate themselves, or pursue ego-oriented growth for growth's sake?” (The compilers' words; the inner quote is as printed.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 74)
- **AUTHOR — what he asks of a cheap stock.** “Okay, it's a good company. But is the price low enough? Is the management made up of people Munger and Buffett Are comfortable with? If it is cheap enough to buy, is it cheap for the wrong reason or the right reason? What's the flip side? What can go wrong that I haven't seen?” (The compilers' words.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 78)
- **REPORTED (Gates) — suspicion (stock options, cost-plus contracts).** Gates on Munger's phrases: “When discussing venture capitalists who defend stock options, he deems them ‘no better than the piano player in a whorehouse.’ When discussing the deleterious effects on efficiency of cost-plus contracts, he likes to say even the mule knew to slow down.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 59)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a claim of synergy).** The sign: a promise of synergy. What it meant: “people generally claim more synergistic benefits than will come.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 101)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (a manager).** The sign: Paul Arnold, CORT's CEO, of whom he says: “Paul's been running the business since he was in law school and loves it.” Munger had Buffett's word first: “You're going to love Paul Arnold [CORT's CEO].” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 101)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a CEO's or an activist's behavior).** The sign: an activist who says “having Warren on the board of Coke is contrary to the interests of Coke.” What it meant: “The cause of reform is hurt not helped when an activist makes an idiotic suggestion like saying that having Warren on the board of Coke is contrary to the interests of Coke. Nutty behavior undermines their cause.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 119)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (an adviser's incentive: brokers).** The sign: brokers who switch clients between funds. What it meant: “costing another three to four percentage points.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 119)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (managers who are crooks; the hard-to-see ones).** “Bernie Ebbers and Ken Lay were caricatures-they were easy to spot. They were almost psychopaths. But it's much harder to spot problems at companies like Royal Dutch [Shell].” “But we don't learn because I'd still expect that Exxon's figures are fair.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 118)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a business whose numbers keep surprising).** The sign: the same “extraordinary” charge every year. Quote: “If it happens every year like clockwork, what's so extraordinary about it?” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 119)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (an investment bank).** The sign: a bank screening its clients less over time. The contrast he draws: “We did a $6 million deal years ago for Diversified Retailing, and we were rigorously and intelligently screened. The bankers cared and wanted to protect their clients.” Now: “The culture now is that anything that can be sold for a profit will be.” “An investment banker earning would be increased in a significant way if he wrote a few more tickets to Maxwell you have to control this if guys can make money by bringing dubious things in the door” (p. 122; the .txt is garbled and unpunctuated). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 121–122)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (an auditing firm).** The sign: selling tax shelters. What it meant: “tax shelters, with their contingency fees and secrecy, are a total abomination.” “You'll better understand the evil of top audit firms starting to sell fraudulent tax shelters when I tell you that one told me that they're better [than the others] because they only sold the schemes] to their top twenty clients, so no one would notice.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 123)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (pension return assumptions).** The sign: expected pension returns of nine or ten percent. What it meant: “[Company leaders] may believe it-they're honest people-but subconsciously they believe it because they want to believe it. It makes earnings good so they can promote the stock.” Why accountants say nothing: “Whose bread I eAt, his song I sing.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 125)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (EBITDA).** The sign: the term EBITDA. What it meant: “bullshit earnings.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 124)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a financial institution).** The sign: talk of how good its risk management is. Quote: “If they start talking about how good risk management is, it makes us nervous.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 128)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (a derivatives book).** The test: “I think a good litmus test of the mental and moral quality at any large institution [with significant derivatives exposure] would be to ask them, "Do you really understand your derivatives book?" Anyone who says yes is either crazy or lying.” And: “You can't believe how big the incentives are to have wishful thinking about values and wishful thinking about ability to clear.” (p. 128) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 128–129)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — suspicion (earnings “blessed by accountants”).** “all kinds of earnings, blessed by accountants, are not really being earned.” (pp. 130–131) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 130–131)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes) — trust (a capital allocator).** The sign: Henry Singleton's record. AUTHOR (the compilers): “Charlie wonders, "Given the many talent and record, have we learned enough from him?"” HIM: “Henry Singleton has the best operating and capital deployment record in American business...if one took the 100 top business school graduates and made a composite of their triumphs, their record would not be as good as Singleton.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 138)
- **HIM (on Lou Simpson) — trust (an investor).** The sign: Simpson's record and what he did under pressure. “It's not unheard of to beat the market averages for a couple of years, maybe even five or ten years. But, imagine beating the S&P 500 by an average annual gain of 6.8 percent over twenty-five years!” “You can't believe the pressure that he was under, year after year, as the world seemed to be reaping enormous gains while he, correctly, avoided the bubble altogether, staying true to fundamentals. Lou was a wonderful example in that period-intelligent, honorable, and true to his fundamentals.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 147)
- **HIM (on Jim Sinegal) — trust (an operator).** “You just wouldn't believe how efficient and sophisticated Costco's warehouse operation is. Jim Sinegal is a fabulous business operator-like a Carnegie, Rockefeller, or James J. Hill.” “He works eighty hours a week and sets a terrific example for his entire organization in terms of work ethic, integrity, loyalty, and selflessness. He's a moral leader as well as a practical leader. These are not minor virtues.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 148)
- **HIM (Harvard School commencement, 1986) — trust (a person).** The sign: reliability. A college roommate, severely dyslexic: “perhaps the most reliable man I have ever known.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 154)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — trust (an early-stage business).** The sign: John Patterson of National Cash Register. “Patterson was a small retail merchant who didn't make any money. One day, somebody sold him a crude cash register, which he put into his retail operation. And it instantly changed from losing money to earning a profit because it made it so much harder for the employees to steal.” He “got the best distribution system, the biggest collection of patents, and the best of everything. He was a fanatic about everything important as the technology developed.” “a well-educated orangutan could see that buying into partnership with Patterson in those early days, given his notions about the cash register business, was a total one hundred percent cinch.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 190–191)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (a manager who does not want bad news).** The sign: Bill Paley at CBS. “But he didn't like to hear what he didn't like to hear, and people soon learned that. So they told Paley only what he liked to hear. Therefore, he was soon living in a little cocoon of unreality and everything else was corrupt although it was a great business.” “If people tell you what you really don't want to hear-what's unpleasant-there is an almost automatic reaction of antipathy. You have to train yourself out of it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 181)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (a big organization).** The signs: layers and “established way of thinking.” On Sears: “Sears had layers and layers of people it didn't need. It was very bureaucratic. It was slow, to think. And there was an established way of thinking. If you poked your head up with a new thought, the system kind of turned against you.” On departments sharing power: “If you won't bother me, I won't bother you, and we're both happy.” (pp. 179–180; the quote is on p. 180) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 180, 184)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (a capital-spending projection).** The sign: a salesman's or internal bureaucrat's projection of savings. What it meant: “In all cases, the people who sell the machinery-and, by and large, even the internal bureaucrats urging you to buy the equipment-show you projections with the amount you'll save at current prices with the new technology. However, they don't do the second step of the analysis-which is to determine how much is going to stay home and how much is just going to flow through to the customer.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 189)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (an industry).** The sign: a commodity with a rival like “a demented Kellogg.” What it meant: “In some businesses, the participants behave like a demented Kellogg. In other businesses, they don't. Unfortunately, I do not have a perfect model for predicting how that's going to happen.” “If it's a pure commodity like airline seats, you can understand why no one makes any money.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 185–186)
- **HIM (USC talk, 1994) — suspicion (investment advisers).** The sign: an adviser's incentive. What it meant: “So what makes sense for the investor is different from what makes sense for the manager. And, as usual in human affairs, what determines the behavior are incentives for the decision maker.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 200)
- **HIM — suspicion.** The sign: anything sold with a tax shelter, or “a big commission and a 200 page prospectus.” What it meant: don't buy it. Quote: “Anytime somebody offers you a tax shelter from here on in life, my advice would be don't buy it.” (p. 212) “anytime anybody offers you anything with a big commission and a 200 page prospectus, don't buy it.” (p. 213) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 212–213)
- **HIM — suspicion (experts whose advice pays them).** The sign: two sets of engineers, each recommending a course “very advantageous to itself.” What it meant: distrust the recommendation; look for a third opinion. Quote: “I saw huge reasons in the circumstances for bias in each set of engineers as each recommended a course of action very advantageous to itself. And what each was saying was so consonant with a natural bias that it made me distrust it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 270–271)
- **HIM — suspicion (the confident non-expert).** The sign: people who always answer questions confidently without knowing. What it meant: he gets rid of them. Quote: “I try to get rid of people who always confidently answer questions about which they don't have any real knowledge. To me, they're like the bee dancing its incoherent dance. They're just screwing up the hive.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 272)
- **HIM — suspicion (a system that rewards cheating).** The sign: easy-to-claim injuries and referral schemes in workers' compensation and auto accidents. What it meant: the cheating spreads and is very hard to stop. Quote: “And he got two chiropractors cards and one lawyer's card before he'd even left the intersection. They're in the business of manufacturing claims that necks hurt.” (p. 283) The plant, as printed (p. 251): “massive fraud in the workers compensation system to the point that is premium reached double digit percentages of payroll” The result of moving: “So my friend closed his plant and moved the work to Utah among a community of believing Mormons.” “It's two percent of payroll” (p. 252) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 251–252, 283)
- **HIM — suspicion (a flood of imitators).** The sign: other institutions rushing to copy Harvard and Yale, “driven by envy and salesmen,” and the earlier venture-capital rush. What it meant: “a lot of future adversity.” Quote: “foreseeing a lot of future adversity for other worthy institutions, driven by envy and salesmen into enthusiastic imitation of Harvard and Yale” (p. 222) “caused about $90 billion to rush into low-quality, imitative early stage ventures that by now may have created as much as $45 billion in net losses for late-coming investors.” (p. 222) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 222)
- **HIM — suspicion (an executive who postpones the unpleasant disclosure).** The sign: Gutfreund, told by his general counsel that he should tell the federal authorities about Salomon's trading improprieties. What it meant: it did not get done. Quote: “The task was easy to put off-because it was unpleasant. So that's what Gutfreund did-he put it off.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 278)
- **HIM — suspicion (success that makes a company think it knows everything).** The sign: GEICO “making a lot of money.” What it meant: foolish things and huge losses; the sound business underneath could be saved by cutting the folly. Quote: “They got to thinking that, because they were making a lot of money, they knew everything. And they suffered huge losses.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 217)
- **HIM — suspicion (an industry's record with shareholders).** The sign: the airline business. What it meant: he did not buy the common; he lent against a preferred. Quote: “the history of the airline business in terms of taking care of shareholders has been terrible.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 258–259)
- **HIM — suspicion (being the only deep pocket).** The sign: a product that can injure a customer, from a rich corporation. What it meant: “bad business.” Quote: “There are businesses-given the way the civilization has developed-where being the only deep pocket around is bad business.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 282)
- **HIM — suspicion (heavy ideology in a thinker).** The sign: a mind “absolutely sure.” What it meant: a distorted cognition, even in a genius. Quote: “If ideology can screw up the head of Chomsky, imagine what it does to people like you and me.” (p. 237) “But a heavy ideology that makes you absolutely sure that the minimum wage should be raised or that it shouldn't-and it's kind of a holy construct where you know you're right-makes you a bit nuts.” (p. 238) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 237–238)
- **HIM — suspicion (advisers' interest in the problem).** The sign: law firms that cluck at litigation while it pays them. What it meant: they are “somewhat schizophrenic on the subject.” Quote: “But, really, they're somewhat schizophrenic on the subject because it's been very good for [them].” “Part of the defense bar lobbied quietly against certain propositions and, effectively, against their clients because they didn't want their clients to catch 'em in the process.” “And the reason that they did so was because it became harder for plaintiffs to bring cases.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 280)
- **HIM — what he noticed (an adviser whose career rides on one person).** The sign: Salomon's general counsel, with “very little constituency within Salomon except for the CEO.” What it meant: his career depended on the CEO. Quote: “And the general counsel had very little constituency within Salomon except for the CEO. If the CEO went down, the general counsel was going down with him.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 278)
- **HIM — trust (untapped pricing power).** The sign: a business where “any manager could raise the return enormously just by raising prices-and here they haven't done it.” What it meant: “the ultimate no-brainer”; Disney, See's Candy, Coca-Cola. Quote: “So they have huge untapped pricing power that they're not using. That is the ultimate no-brainer.” (p. 214) “we invested in Coca-Cola-which had some untapped pricing power. And it also had brilliant management.” (p. 215) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 214–215)
- **HIM — trust (owners of integrity, one winner left).** The sign: the Washington Post: top hand in a game with one winner, and “management with a lot of integrity and intelligence.” What it meant: “That one was a real dream.” Quote: “They're very high class people-the Katharine Graham family. That's why it was a dream-an absolute. damn dream.” (the .txt prints a period after “absolute”.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 216)
- **HIM — trust (a stable, hard-to-copy product).** The sign: Hershey, which “knew enough when they wanted to expand into Canada to know they shouldn't change their winning flavor”; International Flavors and Fragrances, which gets royalties on what can't be patented.
