What it meant: “One of my favorite business stories comes from Hershey.” (p. 230) Quote: “Well, it took them five years to duplicate their own flavor.” (p. 231) “It's the only company I know that does something on which you can't get a copyright or a patent, but which nevertheless receives a permanent royalty.” (p. 231) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 230–231)
- **HIM — trust (managers he admires).** The sign: managers at Berkshire he and Buffett “love and admire.” What it meant: the culture is theirs, and Berkshire does little. Quote: “By and large, we've chosen people we admire enormously to have the power beneath us. It's easy for us to get along with them on average because we love and admire them. And they create the culture for whatever invention and reality recognition is going on in their businesses.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 256–257)
- **HIM — trust (a tail wind he did not have to create).** The sign: Disney's old movies already “in the can,” with the copyright, when the videocassette arrived. What it meant: “that's a marvelous model if you can find it.” (p. 262) Quote: “They had all those movies in the can. They owned the copyright.” “All you have to do is to sit there while the world carries you forward.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 261–262)
- **HIM — suspicion (layers of consultants and analysts).** The sign: foundations with “many investment counselors, chosen by an additional layer of consultants,” who rely in turn on investment-bank analysts. What it meant: a cost that “can easily reach three percent of foundation net worth per annum.” Quote: “Now, in some foundations, there are not few but many investment counselors, chosen by an additional layer of consultants who are hired to decide which investment counselors are best, help in allocating funds to various categories, make sure that foreign securities are not neglected in favor of domestic securities, check validity of claimed investment records, ensure that claimed investment styles are scrupulously followed, and help augment an already large diversification in a way that conforms to the latest notions of corporate finance professors about volatility and ‘beta.’” (p. 348) “These security analysts receive enormous salaries, sometimes set in seven figures after bidding wars. The hiring investment banks recoup these salaries from two sources: (1) commissions and trading spreads borne by security buyers (some of which are rebated as ‘soft dollars’ to money managers) plus (2) investment banking charges paid by corporations that appreciate the enthusiastic way their securities are being recommended by the security analysts.” (p. 349) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 348–349)
- **HIM — suspicion (an expert who rates himself above average).** The sign: a seller of investment advice judging his own prospects. What it meant: “people who are successfully selling something, as investment counselors do, make Swedish drivers sound like depressives.” Quote: “Virtually every investment expert's public assessment is that he is above average, no matter what is the evidence to the contrary.” (p. 351) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 351)
- **HIM — suspicion (an excess of professionalism: General Motors' truck).** The sign: a company that trusted “fancy consumer surveys” over watching people use the product. What it meant: “Using fancy consumer surveys, its excess of professionalism, it concluded not to put a fourth floor in a truck designed to serve also as the equivalent of a comfortable five-passenger car.” (pp. 351–352; the .txt prints “fourth floor”) Its competitors “had actually seen five people enter and exit cars. Moreover, they had noticed that people were used to four doors in a comfortable five-passenger car and that biological creatures ordinarily prefer effort minimization in routine activities and don't like removals of long-enjoyed benefits.” (p. 352) Quote: “There are only two words that come instantly to mind in reviewing General Motors' horrible decision, which has blown many hundreds of millions of dollars. And one of those words is ‘oops.’” (p. 352) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 351–352)
- **HIM — suspicion (the financial scene that reminds you of Sodom and Gomorrah).** The sign: widespread corrupt accounting. What it meant: it “will eventually create bad long-term consequences.” Quote: “I suggest that when the financial scene starts reminding you of Sodom and Gomorrah, you should fear practical consequences even if you like to participate in what is going on.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 376)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction; the editors say the founder is loosely based on Carl Braun, whom Charlie greatly admired) — trust (a founder's compensation and balance-sheet habits).** The sign, as he writes it: no stock options, pay tied to precise performance, no dividends, a fortress of cash. What it meant: “a golden age of sound values.” (p. 381) Quote: “There were no stock options because the old man had considered the accounting treatment required for stock options to be ‘weak, corrupt, and contemptible,’ and he no more wanted bad accounting in his business than he wanted bad engineering.” “Moreover, the old man believed in tailoring his huge incentive bonuses to precise performance standards established for individuals or small groups, instead of allowing what he considered undesirable compensation outcomes, both high and low, such as he believed occurred under other companies' stock option plans.” (p. 382) “Moreover, he loved his business and his coworkers and always wanted to have on hand large amounts of cash equivalents so as to be able to maximize workout or work-up chances if an unexpected adversity or opportunity came along.” (p. 383) “The old man's dividend policy for Quant Tech was simplicity itself He never paid a dividend. Instead, all earnings simply piled up in cash equivalents.” (p. 384; the .txt has no period after “itself”) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 381–384)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction) — suspicion (a board that wants a higher stock price, and the officers it hires).** The sign: new officers recruited from a company with a conventional option plan and a higher multiple. What it meant: “modern financial engineering.” Quote: “Incident to the recruitment of the new executives, it was made plain that Quant Tech's directors wanted a higher market capitalization as soon as feasible.” (p. 385) “Therefore, the new officers were attracted to employing what they called ‘modern financial engineering,’ which required prompt use of any and all arguably lawful methods for driving up reported earnings, with big, simple changes to be made first” (p. 385; the .txt has no period) The alternative he offers, as printed: “A wiser board could then have bought in Quant Tech's stock very aggressively” (p. 384; the .txt continues in garbled form). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 384–385)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction) — suspicion (earnings that grow by the same percentage every year; cash that cannot square with the profit).** The sign: smoothness, a falling tax rate, a pile of cash. What it meant: manufactured earnings. Quote: the officers wanted “Quant Tech's reported earnings to go up by roughly the same percentage each year throughout the whole twenty years because they believed that financial analysts, representing institutional investors, would value Quant Tech's stock higher if reported annual earnings growth never significantly varied.” (p. 390) “They saw that as Quant tech's reported earnings, containing an increasing phony element, kept rising at twenty-eight percent, Quant Tech's income taxes as a percentage of reported pretax earnings kept going lower and lower. This plainly increased chances for causing undesired questions and criticism.” (p. 392) “Cash equivalents on hand now amounted to an astounding $85 billion, and somehow it didn't seem impossible to most investors that a company virtually drowning in so much cash could be earning the $16 billion it was reporting.” (p. 392) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 390–392)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction) — suspicion (accountants who have already signed; critics dismissed as cranks).** The sign: auditors who bless a few statements. What it meant: they cannot turn back. Quote: “Third, the outside accountants, once they had blessed a few financial statements containing earnings increases, only a minority share of which were phony, would probably find it unendurably embarrassing not to bless new financial statements containing only the same phony proportion of reported earnings increase.” (p. 389) “No one criticized Quant techs financial reporting except a few people widely regarded as impractical, overly theoretical, misanthropic cranks.” (p. 391; the .txt prints “techs”) “The new CEO privately called the desirable, cautious approach ‘wisely restrained falsehood.’” (p. 387) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 387–391)

## 5. TEMPERAMENT IN ACTION

- **HIM** — On being a footnote. Epigraph, when first named to the Forbes list in 1993: “I've been associated with Warren (Buffett) so long, I thought I'd be just a footnote.” (Damn Right!, p. 17) Also in: AUTHOR, “While he doesn't mind the wealth, he regrets having his name on any such list. Despite his healthy self-image, Charlie would prefer to be anonymous.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 26)
- **REPORTED (Katharine Graham)** — Letters over about ten years. “He was reassuring me that I was doing better than I thought.” (Damn Right!, p. 18)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — First meeting Buffett, 1959. HIM: “I knew everyone in the Buffett family except Warren,” and “I would have to say that I recognized almost instantly what a remarkable person Warren is.” AUTHOR: “he did not have particularly high expectations about meeting him”; “Charlie was so wrapped up in what he was saying that when he raised his glass to sip his drink, he held his other hand up to stop anyone else from interrupting the conversation.” (Damn Right!, p. 19)
- **HIM** — What he wanted from money. “Not because I wanted Ferraris-I wanted the independence. I desperately wanted it. I thought it was undignified to have to send invoices to other people.” (Damn Right!, p. 20)
- **REPORTED (Buffett's Foreword)** — “In 41 years, I have never seen Charlie try to take advantage of anyone, nor have I seen him claim the least bit of credit for anything that he didn't do.” “He has knowingly let me and others have the better end of a deal, and he has also always shouldered more than his share of the blame when things go wrong and accepted less than his share of credit when the reverse has been true.” p. 8: “Charlie judges himself entirely by an inner scorecard-and he is a tough grader.” “we have never in our entire friendship had an argument nor found disagreement a reason to be disagreeable.” (Buffett on Munger's conduct; no Buffett reasoning recorded.) (Damn Right!, pp. 7–8)
- **AUTHOR** — At the Berkshire meeting: “Charlie sits there looking as if he'd already been chiseled into Mount Rushmore, and gives a brusque reply. “Nothing to add.”” (Damn Right!, p. 10) Also in: AUTHOR (Tilson): “Warren generally takes the first stab at answering a question, but then usually turns and says, ‘Charlie?’” and “Charlie typically replies, ‘I have nothing to add.’” “at the special meeting for the Gen Re acquisition in September 1998, Warren actually showed up with a cardboard cutout of Charlie and a recording of Charlie saying, ‘I have nothing to add.’” REPORTED (Emilie Ogden, p. 51): “His hands are crossed in front of him, as he shakes his head saying, ‘I have nothing to add.’” HIM (as Tilson gives it): “cranky, old fashioned”. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 51, 92–93)
- **AUTHOR** — The biography. Munger “didn't say much except that he didn't think the book would sell many copies.” Then, in a loud voice at the Wesco meeting: “Is Janet Lowe here?” and “Follow me,” then in an office: the family “didn't want a biography of him.” And: “All right then,” Munger barked. “You can start by reading these books.” (Damn Right!, pp. 10–11)
- **REPORTED (Hal Borthwick)** — On the Berkshire stage persona. “It's true, probably, he doesn't have a lot to add and he wants to get on with it.” and “that isn't the real Charlie Munger that's sitting up there.” (Damn Right!, p. 22)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED** — Bridge, boats. AUTHOR: “His partners sometimes have a little trouble understanding why he makes certain bids or plays certain cards, though usually he is following some simple logic of his own.” REPORTED (Molly): “My dad is a pushover for anyone with a wild idea for a boat,” (Damn Right!, p. 23)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Buffett)** — Associated Cotton Shops, 1967. “Suddenly Buffett looked around and discovered he was talking to himself. Charlie was gone. Later Buffett learned that Munger remembered he had to catch a plane, and just walked off.” REPORTED (Buffett): “He is a sensational friend. The niceties are not there. None of the superficial acts, but all of the real ones.” “If you talk about ideas, he's not going to get emotional about it. But if he has superior facts or reasoning, he won't back off.” (Damn Right!, p. 25)
- **AUTHOR** — Ordering from television. Munger, “a certified billionaire,” had “newfangled lures” ordered “from a television fishing show.” (Damn Right!, p. 28)
- **HIM** — The rat and the hamsters. “I'd already sensed her aversion to rodents, so I picked it up and said, ‘Mother, what's this?’ and waved it in front of her.” “It was the only time she took out after me with a coat hanger,” “The idea was getting a bigger buck, or a hamster with unusual coloration, or something like that,” AUTHOR: “when one of them died, he wanted to keep it in the refrigerator.” (Damn Right!, p. 32) Also in: AUTHOR, “At home, Charlie developed a fondness for raising hamsters and periodically traded them with other children. Even at an early age, Charlie showed sagacious negotiating ability and usually gained a bigger specimen or one with unusual coloring.” and “When his brood grew to thirty five animals, his mother ordered an end to his hobby because of pungent odor from his basement hamster farm.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 10–11)
- **HIM** — Fishing. Tribute to J.D. Ramsey: “I have seen a lot of peculiar fishermen in my life who, like me, are willing to suffer to fish in promising water,” (Damn Right!, p. 35)
- **REPORTED (Barry, son)** — “He tries to find the best technique day in and day out and will stick with that lure, or whatever, even if others on the boat are having better success with something else.” (Damn Right!, p. 36)
- **REPORTED (Guerin) + HIM** — The sunk boat. REPORTED: Munger insisted on driving; put it in reverse; Guerin yelled and Charlie replied, “I'll take care of that.” and put on full power, still in reverse; the boat sank. HIM: “The borrowed boat, explained Charlie, was not designed so as to keep the water from rushing in when the boat was going backward.” and “Even when I took him fishing in Minnesota and upset the boat and we had to swim to shore, he didn't scream at me,” (Damn Right!, p. 39) Also in: REPORTED (Buffett): “Charlie managed to sink a boat with no outside help whatsoever.” REPORTED (Guerin): “the time to negotiate a reward for rescuing someone is before, not after you save them” and “I call Charlies catamaran ‘Munger's Folly.’ It's the only completely irrational thing Charlie has ever done.” (the .txt prints “Charlies”); HIM (as Broggie gives it): “The boat was fun to design and build. What's irrational about that?” REPORTED (Gates, repeating Buffett's warning): “managed to sink a boat in a totally calm lake with no other traffic by going full speed in reverse with a low transom on the stern.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 26–27, 59)
- **HIM** — After that: “After dunking him in the lake, we tried to cheer him up by making him watch a bunch of high school students perform Moliere in a Bemidji tent.” (Damn Right!, p. 40)
- **AUTHOR** — Home. “They can take the boy out of Omaha but not Omaha out of the boy,” (Munger “paraphrases an old saying”). (Damn Right!, p. 42)
- **HIM** — His grandfather, the judge. “He wanted not to be poor,” “Self-sufficiency and hard work would be his salvation. My grandparents thought Robinson Crusoe was a great moral work.” “That generation admired the conquering of nature through discipline.” (Damn Right!, p. 44)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED** — Aunt Oofie. AUTHOR: “Her nephew Charlie adored Oofie, partly because her standards were so extreme that she amused him.” REPORTED (Oofie, to Charlie, on her father's death): “It was God's grace to take judge, knowing he wouldn't have wanted to stay on and make errors.” AUTHOR: “even Charlie was floored” by it. (Damn Right!, p. 46)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — His father. AUTHOR: “For years after his father died, Charlie carried Al Munger's briefcase to work.” HIM: “was one of the happiest men who ever lived and achieved exactly what he wished to achieve, no more or less. He faced all troubles with less fuss than either his father or his son, each of whom spent considerable time foreseeing troubles that never happened.” and “I don't see my father as less successful in the sense that really matters.” (Damn Right!, p. 48)
- **HIM** — The rifle team. “I hoped to impress the girls with my sports letter, prominently worn on my sweater,” “And I did turn heads, but the reason was the girls wondered how a spindly little guy like me could have won a sports letter.” (Damn Right!, pp. 48–49) Also in: AUTHOR, “Too small to compete in regular high school sports, he joined the rifle team, earned a varsity letter, and eventually became team captain.” and HIM (Munger's memory, as Broggie gives it): “a large letter on a very small chest” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 11)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Father and food. HIM: “He loved everything about the out-of-doors,” “To him, heaven was finding a farmstand.” AUTHOR: “The little obsessions,” Charlie called them. (Damn Right!, p. 49)
- **HIM** — Omaha school and movies. “There were better behavior standards in school and everywhere else.” “I loved comedies, loved to laugh.” “Once, in the Orpheum we laughed so hard that the rest of the people in the theater started laughing just because we were.” (Damn Right!, p. 50)
- **HIM** — The Berkshire meeting in Omaha, 1977. “I used to come here as a boy for the circus. Now we have a circus of our own.” (Damn Right!, p. 51)
- **HIM + REPORTED** — School. HIM (p. 51): “Teachers were very well-behaved people,” “good moral exemplars in the old-fashioned sense. There was discipline. The moral teaching was good.” REPORTED (sister, p. 52): “He was too independent minded to bow down to meet certain teachers' expectations.” REPORTED (p. 53): “Charlie is both smart and smartie,” (Damn Right!, pp. 51–53)
- **HIM** — Books. “I met the towering intellectuals in books, not in the classroom, which is natural.” “I had Thomas Jefferson over my bed at seven or eight.” “My family was into all that stuff, getting ahead through discipline, knowledge, and self-control.” (Damn Right!, p. 53)
- **HIM** — Dr. Ed Davis. “I became a friend of my father's friend.” “I watched movies of his main operations and familiarized myself with surgical outcome statistics in his field.” (Damn Right!, p. 54) Also in: AUTHOR, “Charlie often read the medical journals belonging to Dr. Ed Davis, who was both his father's best friend and a family physician.” and “he watched motion Pictures of Dr. Davis, a urologist, performing surgery and became fascinated with the statistical outcomes of similar procedures in the field.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 10)
- **HIM** — The Depression. “It was amazing how poor people were in the 1930s,” “One summer it took family pull to get me a summertime job at 40 cents per hour.” “I had the example in early life of family members who behaved well under stress.” HIM on his grandfather's bank rescue: “Uncle Tom needed about $35,000 worth of good assets to replace $35,000 of crap. Grampa Munger had $35,000 in good mortgages and put them into his son-in-law's bank in exchange for the crap. It was a big risk.” “At the end of the bank holiday, Uncle Tom's bank re-opened, and, eventually, over many years, much of the judge's investment was recovered as bad assets became merely mediocre assets.” (Damn Right!, p. 55)
- **HIM** — His father in 1936; the grocery. “My father was never again so rich in real income as he was in 1936.” “The hours were long, the pay low, opinions cast in iron, and foolishness zero.” (Damn Right!, p. 56)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — The Buffett store. HIM: “You were just goddam busy from the first hour of morning until night,” AUTHOR: “At the end of the day, Charlie handed Ernest his two pennies and in return received two dollar bills, plus a lecture on the evils of socialism.” (Damn Right!, p. 57) Also in: AUTHOR (Broggie, “According to Charlie”): the boss “scheduled his young workers for twelve-hour shifts with no meals or breaks” and “his boss's staunch anti socialist attitude was manifested in his rule requiring the boys to present two pennies at the end of their shifts to cover the cost of the new Social Security Act, In return, they received a $2 daily wage along with a considerable lecture about the evils of socialism.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 8–9)
- **REPORTED (Molly)** — Chapter epigraph. “Even when we were children, he'd say, ‘We'll see. We'll see,’ more than any other comment.” (Damn Right!, p. 59)
- **AUTHOR** — A grandson falls down the stairs. “Charlie continues to read. “I didn't think so,” he mutters.” (Damn Right!, p. 60)
- **HIM** — The Army. “It was very expensive bacon,” (his father's wartime pigs) “I think my father did it mostly because he liked raising pigs.” “I remember talking to someone. I said I wanted a lot of children, a house with lots of books, enough money to have freedom.” AUTHOR: “Charlie did much better than that, scoring 149.” (Damn Right!, p. 61)
- **HIM** — The war and California. “I don't think I knew well 15 people who died in World War II.” “I never got near military action. I was stationed in Nome. I couldn't have gotten farther from action.” “Southern California was quite different. It looked like a bigger, more interesting place than Omaha, a city that I love,” (Damn Right!, p. 62)
- **REPORTED (Molly) + AUTHOR** — First marriage. REPORTED: “And they utterly rushed into marriage-he was 21, she 19-no idea of what they were doing, both people of high spirits. Young people in the middle of a war. They made severe mistakes.” AUTHOR: “It took several years before it would become apparent that Munger's marriage was a misjudgment.” (Damn Right!, p. 63)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Harvard. HIM: “I came to Harvard Law School very poorly educated, with desultory work habits and no college degree.” AUTHOR: “Charles Munger once described himself as having a black belt in chutzpah”; to a professor, “I haven't read the case, but if you give me the facts, I'll give you the law.” AUTHOR: “Munger later came to realize that conversational gambits of that type were foolish and impeded his progress in life.” and he “apparently was behind the door when humility was handed out.” (Damn Right!, p. 65)
- **REPORTED (Gross; Sinegal) + AUTHOR** — REPORTED (classmate Henry Gross, answering the charge that prosperity made him pompous): “Nonsense,” “I knew him when he was young and poor; he was always pompous.” REPORTED (Costco's Sinegal): “have an agenda. If you don't buy off on his viewpoint, he doesn't pout. He's prepared to move on with the conversation.” AUTHOR: “what saves him is that his opinions aren't set in stone.” (Damn Right!, pp. 65–66)
- **HIM** — Harvard and a baby. “I used to move her crib into the bathtub each night.” Law Review story: “But after a while, I imitated the tramp.” (The tramp: “Fuck this shit. I didn't kill anybody.”) (Damn Right!, p. 66)
- **REPORTED (Molly)** — The divorce, 1953. “He lost everything in the divorce,” “Every Saturday he was there. Every Saturday he was cheerful.” On the yellow Pontiac: “To discourage gold diggers,” (Damn Right!, p. 70)
- **HIM + REPORTED (Guerin)** — Teddy's leukemia. HIM: “In those days there was no medical insurance,” “I just paid all the expenses.” “You'd have a bonding experience in the leukemia ward. Parents and grandparents were having the same experience. They were all going to lose.” “I've often wondered how professionals are able to steel themselves when dealing time after time with children facing mortality rates so high.” “I can't imagine any experience in life worse than losing a child inch by inch,” “By the time he died my weight was down 10 to 15 pounds from normal.” REPORTED (Guerin): “he'd go in and hold him for a while, then go out walking the streets of Pasadena crying.” (Damn Right!, p. 71)
- **REPORTED (Charles Jr., son)** — “He just walks away from that (emotionally). Dad says to himself, that doesn't work. Don't revisit it.” (Damn Right!, pp. 73–74)
- **REPORTED (Molly)** — Money in the 1950s. “He was always interested in money,” “He talked about business in a way that was animated and interesting though now I see he was almost broke.” “I knew he drove an awful car.” (Damn Right!, p. 74)
- **REPORTED (Molly)** — “He's not a very good manager,” “He's the utter absent-minded professor. He buys on impulse. If he had expensive tastes, he'd be in real trouble.” (Damn Right!, p. 76)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Hal)** — After the blind date: “Charlie assured him that everything went very well, but then scolded Tolles for not telling Charlie the most important thing about Nancy-that she had been a Phi Beta Kappa student.” Hal on both: “neither of them suffer fools particularly gladly or wants to waste time,” (Damn Right!, p. 79)
- **REPORTED (Hal Borthwick)** — Discipline of the stepson. “Charlie made short work of all that.” “not afraid to spank, though Charlie had to be pushed before he would do so, and the spankings were not severe.” p. 81: “I'm sure Charlie didn't particularly enjoy it, but at the end of the day it got the job done.” (Damn Right!, pp. 80–81)
- **HIM** — Children. “I didn't see life as a breeder's derby,” “I'm very glad to have had children.” (Damn Right!, p. 81)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Wendy)** — At home. AUTHOR: “Charlie habitually did several things at once. He would sit in a chair in the evening and read a book, at the same time following and interjecting comments into the family conversation.” REPORTED (Wendy): “He doesn't say children and stepchildren, he simply says we have eight children,” (Damn Right!, p. 83)
- **REPORTED (Emilie)** — “I think he teaches through telling stories about people who are admirable in his eyes and those who are not.” (Damn Right!, p. 84)
- **REPORTED (Nancy; Molly)** — “He was not much of a helpmate around the house. I always say, he lives in a lovely hotel that others maintain. He's no potterer.” Molly: “He probably feels that if he ever began, he would be overwhelmed by his emotions,” (Damn Right!, p. 85)
- **AUTHOR** — Young lawyer. “But he also sometimes found himself punished for his outspoken brashness and tendency to show off his brains.” REPORTED (Rickershauser, relaying a senior partner's advice to him, told to Charlie): “You must always remember that your duty is to conduct yourself so that everyone appraises you no higher than the third smartest person in the room. The client must be made to appear smartest, with me the next smartest, and only after this should any wisdom seem to reside in you.” (Damn Right!, p. 87)
- **HIM + REPORTED (Buffett)** — Garrett and reality. HIM: “Roy Garrett was a dominant personality, and he and I naturally clashed,” REPORTED (Buffett): “He was always seeing reality. He is unable to be around a problem without thinking about it.” AUTHOR on his early principles: “Charlie lived by principles he'd learned at his grandfather's knee-first, the surest way of building a business is by concentrating on the work already on his desk, and second, by underspending his income and amassing a pile of cash that could be invested to build future wealth.” (Damn Right!, p. 88)
- **HIM** — Ed Hoskins. “Ed worked 90 hours a week” “He designed every transformer in the early days. I can't tell you how close we were and what a wonderful man he proved to be. We had troubles that made his hair fall out. Terrible struggles.” “It was a lot of struggle, a lot of nerve pain.” (Damn Right!, p. 90)
- **AUTHOR** — Driving to lunch in Pasadena. AUTHOR: “Munger is blind in his left eye and has little peripheral vision in the other. He has adapted, developing tricks to figure out where traffic is, and when he has a clear shot to enter or exit the flow.” (Damn Right!, p. 93)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Booth)** — The waiter. HIM: “I'm not old enough for that,” (senior discount) “Are you our waiter?” “Well, keep the change then,” AUTHOR: “shoving two $10 bills across the counter for a bill that couldn't have been more than $15.” HIM: “I don't look at waiter's faces, so I never recognize them later,” “It's a terrible habit to live with. Very embarrassing.” “I'm always thinking about other things. I forget to look around.” Booth: “Charlie has enormous powers of concentration,” “When he concentrates, everything else goes away.” (Damn Right!, p. 94)
- **REPORTED (Booth)** — “Charlie was the same then as he is now,” “Hungrier, more aggressive.” (Damn Right!, p. 96)
- **REPORTED (Booth)** — The pregnancy lunch. “Charlie grinned and said, ‘I think there's something you should know. Nancy's pregnant.’” (Damn Right!, p. 97)
- **REPORTED (Booth)** — “Charlie isn't secretive,” “but simply very compartmentalized in his communication. He follows the ‘need-to-know’ rule.” (Damn Right!, p. 98)
- **REPORTED (Charles Jr.)** — Construction sites. “The kids would spend the day with dad at the construction site. We liked to pick up the metal plugs from the electrical boxes, like little coins,” (Damn Right!, p. 99)
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (Marshall)** — The golf course. AUTHOR: on the second hole Charlie asked, “What do you do?”, on the third how he was bidding; “Charlie replied, “You're doing it all wrong.”” REPORTED (Marshall): “I said okay, if you're so smart, why don't you do the legal and financing work and I'll do the rest,” (Damn Right!, p. 100)
- **REPORTED (Marshall)** — Who was boss. “One of the good things about working with Charlie,” “was that there was no doubt about who should be boss.” (Damn Right!, p. 101)
- **REPORTED (Booth) + HIM** — Getting his way with architects. REPORTED (Booth): “No, he doesn't offend people,” “He has a great ability to turn a phrase and make things amusing.” HIM, on the bathrooms: “You're going to do this in a building where you teach biology?” REPORTED (Booth), on the gym/arts building: Charlie told the board, “Look, man has only one instrument that serves two functions and it always gets him into trouble.” (Damn Right!, p. 104)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Giving way to his daughter. AUTHOR: Molly chose, over his objection, a high school where none of her friends went; “As Charlie gave in to his willful daughter, he said,” HIM: “Molly, you are insisting on raising yourself. Make sure you do a good job.” (Damn Right!, pp. 110–111)
- **HIM** — Why he took to Buffett. “With my background, how could I fail to take to a man who preferred reading and thinking to delivering groceries and who had learned something from everything he ever read, including the manuscript his grandfather left behind entitled, ‘How to Run a Grocery Store and a Few Things I Have Learned About Fishing.' ” (Damn Right!, p. 109)
- **AUTHOR (unattributed quotation)** — “Charlie can analyze and evaluate any kind of deal faster and more accurately than any man alive. He sees any valid weakness in 60 seconds. He's a perfect partner.” (The book gives no speaker.) (Damn Right!, p. 112)
- **REPORTED (Guerin)** — “He's always willing to listen. Always had an open mind. If you said ‘Charlie, stop talking and listen to me, to what I'm trying to say,' he would listen.” (Damn Right!, p. 113)
- **REPORTED (a friend; Lou Simpson; Buffett)** — Friend: “One of Warren's strengths is that he's very good at saying no, but Charlie is better. Warren uses Charlie as one last litmus test.” (The .txt prints “lie's” for “he's”.) AUTHOR: Buffett has called Munger “the abominable no man,” and Lou Simpson says it is “more of a joke than anything.” REPORTED (Simpson): “Charlie thinks outside of the box.” REPORTED (Buffett): “He's just the best pal a guy could have.” AUTHOR: Munger “can be self-willed, preoccupied, and abrupt, says Buffett.” (Damn Right!, p. 113) Also in: Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 106–107 (REPORTED, Buffett and Rickershauser; entries in TEMPERAMENT in this block).
- **AUTHOR + REPORTED (a member)** — The Einstein lecture at the Buffett Group. AUTHOR: “Once Munger gave a lecture on Einstein's Theory of Relativity. Few people were interested, but most felt obligated to go.” REPORTED (one member): “If Buffett was there, he probably understood it. I don't think anyone else did.” (Damn Right!, pp. 116–117)
- **REPORTED (Molly)** — Gifts. “The idea of him out shopping is extremely funny. He bought us all Brooks Brothers gift certificates, He loves Brooks Brothers.” On the watch: “He looked up from his paper and said ‘Oh yes, that's very appropriate. Go out and pick yourself out a watch and send me the bill.' ” and “and have it engraved, from your loving father.” Later, in her first year of law school, a framed set of head shots of her great-grandfather, grandfather, father and herself, with “Some little note from him.” (Damn Right!, p. 120)
- **REPORTED (Olson)** — “He can talk over the best conversationalist I know. His opinions are never hidden.” “Charlie, from the first day I met him, was the most wise, most mature, most sensible man I ever met.” (Damn Right!, p. 125)
- **REPORTED (Denham)** — As a client. “He is an unusually smart client. He understood legal work quite well and the business issues. Working for him was hard, demanding, but it's the best work you can do, because you learn a lot from it.” (Damn Right!, p. 126)
- **AUTHOR** — Confidential calls. In the cramped Wheeler, Munger office, “if Charlie got a sensitive call from a client, he asked Al and Vivian, their secretary, to step outside so that he could maintain confidentiality. Al and Vivian would stand around in the hall, on one foot and then the other, and wait.” (Damn Right!, p. 129)
- **REPORTED (Marshall) + HIM** — Not seeing people; needling. Marshall: golf partners said hello in the elevator; “Charlie would just stare straight ahead.” “What guy?” “Oh. I didn't see him,” (Marshall: “This was even before he had his eye operation,”). REPORTED (Marshall), to a West Point officer just before he addressed the ball: “I don't know, but I think a little communism is good for the military.” (“The West Pointer's face reddened and he missed his putt.”) (Damn Right!, pp. 134–135)
- **REPORTED (Marshall)** — Names. “Charlie just kept calling all of them Vivian.” “I've always said it's a good thing his second wife was named Nancy, too. Otherwise he'd never have remembered her name.” (Damn Right!, p. 139)
- **REPORTED (Marshall)** — At home. “One kid was climbing on his shoulder, another was pulling his arm. Another was yelling. It was bedlam, but he didn't send them out or correct them. It didn't bother him a bit.” (Damn Right!, p. 140)
- **AUTHOR** — Lunch with the executor. “Munger, who was at best a little awkward when dealing with women, telephoned the mistress/executor and invited her to lunch at the California Club”; when she arrived in a nurse's uniform, “Charlie was flustered, but there was no way of getting out of accompanying the woman to lunch.” (Damn Right!, p. 141)
- **REPORTED (Guerin)** — Valuing the buyout. “It was an opportunity for him to show me how stupid I was,” “Charlie has a saying, ‘Think about it a little more and you will agree with me because you're smart and I'm right.' ” (Damn Right!, p. 142)
- **REPORTED (Molly)** — Taxes. “He made a lot of money in this or that deal and didn't have to pay as much in taxes. He said, ‘If I'm a lawyer I have to pay more. The capital gains tax is less, and in my case it doesn't seem fair.' ” (Damn Right!, p. 144)
- **AUTHOR (quoting Lemann)** — Why independence. “He wanted to be rich so he could be completely independent, like Crusoe on his island, and not have to do what anybody else said.” (Lemann's words as the author quotes them: Defoe, not Franklin, “behind Munger's lust to be his own man”; pp. 144–145.) (Damn Right!, p. 145)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — Under loss. AUTHOR: “When dealing only with his own money, investment losses never bothered Munger much.” But “he now found that reported, temporary quotational losses in the Wheeler, Munger limited partnership accounts gave him tremendous pain.” (see DECISIONS, pp. 149–150.) (Damn Right!, pp. 149–150)
- **REPORTED (a host, via Marshall) + HIM** — Talking and joking. Host: “Nobody can get a word in edgewise. He's lecturing them on difficulties religions have in describing heaven, something he called a thousand-year orgasm.” Munger, at a grocery's meat counter, after Al Marshall grabbed a stranger's behind, shouting from the other end: “You know, he does that to all the women.” (AUTHOR: “Charlie's comment only made the woman angrier.”) (Damn Right!, p. 152)
- **REPORTED (Fred Ballard, via Molly)** — Explaining himself. “I have no idea who [Munger] is from what he said of himself. He could be working for the CIA.” Molly: “Daddy made no coherent explanation of himself.” (Damn Right!, p. 153)
- **REPORTED (Molly)** — “I've accused Father of being negative. He has a buoyant, cheerful upside, but also a negative side. He said, ‘No, no, I'm not negative. I jump like a little trout if it's a good idea.' His hand went up in the air.” (Damn Right!, p. 155)
- **AUTHOR vs. REPORTED (Hills)** — The SEC chairmanship. AUTHOR: “According to some reports, Munger called Hills several times and berated him for not standing by his friends, but Hills said that Munger never called him about the matter. They never discussed it at the time or later.” (Damn Right!, pp. 162–163)
- **HIM** — A joke. “My wife told the guy who owns the automobile repair shop where she takes her car to hold onto it. Well, the other day when she stepped out of her car he hugged and kissed her. So maybe we should buy into another dying business.” (Damn Right!, p. 166)
- **AUTHOR** — At the table. Molly thanks him for the great hall; “Charlie stares straight ahead, not responding to her comment, not blinking.” “Did you hear me, Daddy?” “Yes,” he murmurs. Nancy: “Charlie! He never listens to me,” (Damn Right!, p. 172)
- **HIM** — At See's anniversary, climbing out of the sidecar. “It gave me the chance to look a lot spryer than I really am.” (Damn Right!, p. 173)
- **REPORTED (Huggins)** — Meeting Munger. “Charlie would periodically interrupt and put in his comments.” “Charlie would volunteer whether asked or not.” “Charlie's a college professor or a chief justice of the Supreme Court,” “I was a little guarded with Charlie. He was very absolute. Warren left some room for your opinion.” “If you've ever tried to get Charlie off a position, you're wasting your time. He sort of huffs and tosses his head, and you can forget anything from there on.” (Damn Right!, pp. 177–179)
- **REPORTED (Huggins)** — “The Ben Franklin thing is appropriate. Charlie's as corny as hell, but what more do you need?” (AUTHOR: Huggins “came to the opinion that Munger was a very practical person.”) (Damn Right!, p. 181)
- **REPORTED (Buffett) + HIM** — Franklin. Buffett: “Charlie overdosed on Ben Franklin early in his life, and he believes that a penny saved is a penny lost.” Charlie: “I can tell you a lot about Warren, he reminds me of Ben Franklin, I can tell you a lot about Ben Franklin.” (Damn Right!, p. 187)
- **REPORTED (Ron Olson)** — “Charlie gives more than time,” “He transforms the charities.” (Damn Right!, p. 191)
- **REPORTED (Barry Munger) + HIM** — The toast. At a party for the obstetrician Keith Russell, after a patient toasted him for the babies he'd delivered, Charlie raised his glass and declared: “I want to toast Dr. Russell for the thousands of babies he didn't deliver.” (Damn Right!, p. 192)
- **AUTHOR** — The children and the pay phone. After the Buffalo News purchase: “The youngsters scurried around their lakeside Minnesota cabin collecting coins, then went with Charlie to the telephone booth at the marina across the lake to help him feed money into the pay phone as he and Buffett plotted their strategy for the paper.” (Damn Right!, p. 194)
- **AUTHOR** — To a tired colleague. When the lawyer Ernest Zack complained of the Buffalo litigation, “Munger admonished him, ‘Oh, it's good for you.' ” (Damn Right!, pp. 196–197)
- **REPORTED (Denham) + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Molly)** — Losing his sight. Denham: “He was pretty stoic. I think he found it quite frustrating. He didn't take it out on other people.” AUTHOR: “Finally Munger had to admit he was not able to read paperwork the way he once did, and warned his colleagues not to count on him to discover errors the way he used to. He told Denham that the responsibility for carefully reviewing documents was now his.” AUTHOR: “Buffett was amazed that Charlie didn't complain about his problems.” Molly: “He practically ran the boat into the dock, he couldn't see. He was afraid of being blind.” (Damn Right!, p. 197)
- **REPORTED (Olson)** — In the Buffalo litigation. “Charlie was confident and ready to stand by the lawyers as it played out.” (Damn Right!, p. 198)
- **REPORTED (Stanford Lipsey)** — “I've never seen Charlie get angry. If Warren and Charlie believe in the principle of something, they don't deviate from it, even if it's not popular with the individuals around them.” (Damn Right!, p. 199)
- **REPORTED (Wendy)** — “I had this movie star father-I just want people to know he didn't always wear thick glasses. That was only after his surgery.” (Damn Right!, p. 200)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Marshall)** — Driving with one eye. AUTHOR: he “has learned how to change into lanes on his blind side by counting the cars in the rearview mirror, and knowing after which one there will be a gap.” He drives a Lexus with a powerful engine and “tends to make what out-of-state drivers call California stops”. REPORTED (Marshall): “He used to carry a gallon of gas in the trunk, which wasn't safe at all,” “because he could never remember to put gas in the thing.” In Hawaii Marshall shouted “Stop” at a washed-out bridge; Charlie, talking and gesturing, asked “Why?” and stopped just short of the edge. (Damn Right!, pp. 201–202)
- **REPORTED (Booth, Borthwick)** — Losing the eye. Booth: “When he lost his eyesight, he handled it in a pragmatic fashion,” “He got some books on braille to see if that would work for him.” AUTHOR: when he had enough vision to read well, “Charlie gave up all thoughts of braille.” Borthwick: “In every one of our houses there will ibe three, four, five books stacked by his chair and three, four, or five more stacked by the bed.” (The .txt prints “ibe”.) “He's not a fiction reader so it's either a business book or a biography or a history or science book. It's always based on facts.” (The .txt prints “hook”.) (Damn Right!, p. 202)
- **REPORTED (Booth) + HIM** — The Department of Motor Vehicles. Booth: “He told the person at the desk that he was blind in one eye,” the examiner wanted a doctor's note. HIM (as Booth reports it): “Hell, I can prove it to you right here. It's a false eye. I'll take it out and lay it on the counter if you like.” Booth: “It took about a half hour to sort out.” (Damn Right!, p. 202)
- **AUTHOR** — At his mother's funeral. Toody Munger died; “He remembered his father and his grandparents, the Russells, and he realized that his mother's had been a ‘blessed life.’” (Damn Right!, p. 203)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Wesco's meetings. HIM, 1997, of the crowd: “Typical group of gluttons (for punishment) and masochists-when you attract that crowd it gets bigger every year because nobody ever leaves,” And, in the larger room: “But I know that many of you are disappointed to see our annual meeting held in such an elaborate room-even if your heavy attendance has established a new record for occupancy per square foot,” AUTHOR: at the 1999 meeting shareholders stayed three hours “plying the 75-year-old capitalist with questions”; he “protests at the silliness of it all, but he is willing to sit for hours answering questions”. (Damn Right!, pp. 206–207, 221)
- **HIM** — The muskies story. About how hard good acquisitions are, Munger told of Ed Hoskins and his guide at Leech Lake: “Are any muskies caught in this lake,” / “More muskies are caught in this lake than in any other lake in Minnesota. This lake is famous for muskies.” / “How long have you been fishing here?” / “Nineteen years.” / “How many muskies have you caught?” / “None.” (Damn Right!, p. 216)
- **REPORTED (Lipsey)** — Adulators and the rental car. “I heard Charlie say for the first time he was getting worried about adulators-movie star, rock star-type adulators.” “I had rented a normal-sized car, then I noticed Charlie had rented a smaller one.” (Damn Right!, p. 233)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — The Berkshire weekend dinner. AUTHOR: “Munger can be as smug about the success of Berkshire as its investors are, he can't resist telling everyone why they're there.” HIM, at his own dinner party: “It's wonderful that we all come here each year,” “But why do we really do it? Yes, it's fun. But it's also a way of subtly saying, I'm very rich. It's also a way of subtly saying, I'm very smart.” AUTHOR: he then suggested everyone bring unmarried children and grandchildren and hold mixer dances so Berkshire heirs would marry one another and keep the shares within fewer families: “Just another one of Charlie's semi-bewildering jokes.” (Damn Right!, p. 234)
- **HIM** — Plugging World Book at the 1994 meeting. “I give away more of that product than any other product that Berkshire Hathaway makes.... It's a perfectly fabulous human achievement. To edit something that is user-friendly with that much wisdom encapsulated is a fabulous thing.” AUTHOR: he drinks Coca Cola “though not as much of it as Buffett does” and “doesn't mind substituting his Coke with an occasional beer or glass of wine.” (Damn Right!, p. 234)
- **REPORTED (Emilie, Molly)** — The family and the money. Emilie: “My dad was a creature of habit so everything was exactly the same. We never had a feeling we were growing up in some rich household.” Molly: “If you just keep pressing on and don't let anything that happens get to you, your life is so much better.” (Damn Right!, pp. 235–236)
- **HIM + REPORTED (Buffett)** — Clarifying a succession remark. AUTHOR: Munger says Buffett “did not mean to imply that Simpson would replace him”. HIM: “We could have done the job of naming a replacement directly and not in some crazy, indirect way,” (The .txt prints “clone”.) (Damn Right!, p. 243)
- **HIM + AUTHOR + REPORTED (Buffett)** — The company plane. AUTHOR: when Buffett bought a private jet, “Munger continued to take commercial flights and chided Buffett for his extravagance”; Buffett dubbed it the “Indefensible”. After Berkshire bought FlightSafety, HIM: “Prompted by Al Ueltschi (the company's founder), we are changing the name of the company plane from ‘the Indefensible’ to ‘the Indispensable,’” AUTHOR (p. 265): when Buffett took the helm at Salomon, “Munger said that if any CEO is entitled travel by private airplane, it is Buffett. ‘It’s the most deserved jet in corporate America.’” (Damn Right!, pp. 247, 265)
- **REPORTED (Buffett) + HIM** — Talking less, still in the game. Buffett: “We talked about more prospective ideas 25 years ago. There was a time when we averaged well over once a day.” “Charlie doesn't have his ego wrapped up in Berkshire the way I do, but he understands it perfectly.” HIM: “Warren likes the game,” “I like the game. And even in periods that are thought of as a tough times for other people, it's a lot of fun.” (Damn Right!, p. 252)
- **REPORTED (Wendy)** — Under pressure at Salomon. “He doesn't show that he's under pressure to his kids,” “he was totally absorbed by it and engaged, and it was the first time we ever saw him in a suit on Star Island.” (Damn Right!, p. 254)
- **REPORTED (Simpson, Olson)** — The audit committee and the lawyers. Simpson: “Charlie, to a lot of people in management, was a pain in the rear.” “I suspect people thought Charlie was a crank.” Olson: “I felt I had just about wrung myself out in the case,” “Then I would run into Charlie. He'd say, You can take it.” (Damn Right!, p. 264)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — In the witness chair, summer 1999. AUTHOR (p. 271): Olson had warned him to limit his testimony to answering the questions; “gradually he slipped into the persona he occasionally displays at the Berkshire Hathaway annual meeting”. REPORTED (Girardi, plaintiff's attorney): “He's yet to answer one question directly out of the 42 questions that have been asked so far.” AUTHOR: “the judge seemed perplexed at what to do, since it was becoming clear that Charlie's way was Charlie's way”. Then HIM: “Ow, Ow, ouch, ouch, ouch.” “I've got a cramp in my leg,” “It's the beauty of getting old.” and “When you get as old as I am, it will happen to you,” (Damn Right!, pp. 271–273)
- **REPORTED (Grace) + HIM** — The retrial. Grace, the other publisher, said Munger's “arrogant” and “dismissive” behavior on the stand was a boon to his case. HIM: “We won't lose,” “The Daily Journal did nothing that violated any laws.” AUTHOR: for the retrial the paper “beefed up its defense team”. (Damn Right!, p. 281)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — How much time he gives the Daily Journal. AUTHOR (p. 284): he spends “5 percent or less of his time at the newspaper”; “his main job is to stay out of Salzman's way and let him run the company.” HIM: “I'm plenty active though,” “I don't have a good half speed.” “I'm quite active in the newspaper but not on the editorial side.” (Damn Right!, p. 284)
- **REPORTED (Guerin)** — What Guerin says of the Daily Journal. “Money is not everything to Charlie. We do hope we've advanced civilization an inch.” (Damn Right!, pp. 283–284)
- **REPORTED (Leeka) + AUTHOR** — The first meeting at Good Sam. Munger “strode in and seated himself at the head of the table”; Leeka: “I don't think he really looked up to see me,” and he “simply ignored” Leeka's outstretched hand. A board member explained: “He couldn't see your hand,” “He's blind in that eye.” AUTHOR (p. 288): “Despite Munger's idiosyncratic behavior, Leeka knew right away that he liked Munger.” Leeka (p. 293): “I see people who just don't understand him,” “They think he's zoning out. He's not. He's sitting there looking at cash flow, return on investment three years out, then back filling to see how he can make it work.” (Damn Right!, pp. 287–289, 293)
- **REPORTED (Borthwick)** — Why he does it. “And if you ask Charlie why he does it, one of the things he'll say is that he doesn't want all the hands that he plays in life to be easy ones.” (Damn Right!, p. 294)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — The microphone that was off. At the annual employee dinner Charlie “went to the wrong microphone, one that was not turned on.” Nancy shouted “Charlie, the mike's not on,” several times; “Charlie paid no attention.” When the sound came on, he was wrapping up: “Thank you for working at Good Samaritan,” he said, and left the stage. (Damn Right!, pp. 294–295)
- **REPORTED (Emilie, Booth)** — What they see in him. Emilie: “Don't just take for your own family and hoard it. He gives a lot to institutions, especially educational institutions.” Booth: “It is not readily apparent, but he has an immense, high level of compassion and understanding. It's way back inside of him.” “He does not wear his heart on his sleeve, but he nonetheless has a large heart.” (Damn Right!, pp. 299–300)
- **REPORTED (Ronald Olson)** — Chapter epigraph. “The values that guide his personal life guide his public life. You live life simply. You live it in the middle of the field, and you don't cut corners.” (Damn Right!, p. 302)
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED (Bratton)** — Stepping in as moderator, Cardozo School of Law, 1996. AUTHOR: “Pity the poor professor who gets caught up in a debate with Munger on the academic treatment of investment policy.” The scheduled moderator could not come “due to the death of a close friend”; HIM: “The accidents of mortality have given you a Baptist bumpkin suddenly put in charge of a bunch of Catholic archbishops who are going to debate revisions of the Catholic mass, in Latin. But I figure I could moderate such a convention.” On Professor William Bratton's research on dividends, HIM: “What is the break point where a business becomes suboptimal in an ordinary corporation or when an investment becomes suboptimal?” Bratton: “When the return on the investment is lower than the cost of capital.” HIM: “And what is the cost of capital?” Later: “Why? Why do you do this?” Bratton: “It's in my job description.” HIM: “Because other people are teaching it, is what you're telling me.” AUTHOR: “The bantering was done in a good-natured tone, but the point of the exchange was quite serious.” Then HIM, to make amends: “I don't want my remark about the cost of capital to be interpreted as meaning that I think the great bulk of Professor Bratton's paper is wrong. I think it's profoundly right. When he talks about agency costs in corporations and the discipline caused by levels of debt and the discipline caused by dividend conventions, I think he is profoundly right. And to the extent that those are the conventional academic explanations, I think it's wisdom he's giving. It's just the cost of capital thing that always makes me go into orbit.” (AUTHOR then gives Buffett's definition of cost of capital, pp. 304–305; no Munger words attached.) (Damn Right!, pp. 302–305)
- **HIM** — Captain Glotz. A shareholder asked Buffett how he spent his days; Buffett said he mostly read and talked on the telephone, “That's what I do. Charlie, what do you do?” HIM: “That [question] reminds me very much of a friend of mine in World War II in a group that had nothing to do,” “A general once went up to my friend's boss, we'll call him Captain Glotz. He said, ‘Captain Glotz, what do you do?’ His boss said, ‘Not a damn thing.’” “The General got madder and madder and turned to my friend and said, ‘What do you do?’” “My friend said, ‘I help Captain Glotz.’ That's the best way to describe what I do at Berkshire.” (Damn Right!, p. 317)
- **REPORTED (Nancy)** — Chapter epigraph. “Charlie talks as much as he always did, the only difference is, now people listen.” (Damn Right!, p. 319)
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED** — The boat in the rice, Little Rice Lake, August 1999. Nancy, Molly and David Borthwick (driving) aboard. HIM: “Let's throw out a line and fish,” instructing David to pull the boat near the mouth of a water course. “‘Aren't we too close to the edge,’ a worried Nancy asks repeatedly. ‘Naw,’ says Charlie.” AUTHOR: he had David let the boat drift across the inlet and motor back near the shallow edge; a gust drove it into the rice and the propeller clogged; “Charlie, David, and a houseguest bend over the back of the boat, stripping long green shreds from the propellor”. Nancy, “Rather than show her frustration, and perhaps to resist saying, ‘I told you so,’” scanned the sky for birds. AUTHOR: Molly “cheerfully announces that, based on past experiences of boating with her father, she has worn her swimsuit underneath her slacks and is prepared to jump in the lake and push the boat out into the channel if necessary.” AUTHOR: “Getting caught in the weeds or stuck on a sandbar when boating is only one of the Munger family traditions.” (Damn Right!, pp. 319–320)
- **AUTHOR** — His Santa Barbara study. “The Mungers drive from Los Angeles to their Santa Barbara home on many weekends. ‘We love this house,’ said Charlie.” The study has “a model sailing ship, carved wooden ducks, and stacks of books. Books by Somerset Maugham; biographies of Margaret Thatcher, Mark Twain, and Albert Einstein; The Moral Animal by Robert Wright; Tales from the Drone's Club by P.G. Wodehouse. And stacked on a side table, The Ultimate Rose Book and The French Interior.” (Damn Right!, p. 320)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Aiming low; Johnson. HIM: “Believing that the secret of human felicity is to aim low, I promptly did that,” adding that he wanted to be able to say what Samuel Johnson said of his dictionary: “I knew very well what I was trying to do. And very well how to do it. And I have done it very well.” AUTHOR: “Of course Munger means that as a bit of a joke, and he admits he overshot his mark somewhat.” (Damn Right!, pp. 320–321)
- **AUTHOR** — How the days go. “Charlie Munger cannot be described as a lavish person, but he lives fully and even colorfully.” He comes “running when Berkshire has a crisis and needs him,” tends his duties at the Daily Journal, Wesco, Good Samaritan and Harvard-Westlake, and with Nancy “visits friends everywhere from Maine to Idaho, plays golf in Hawaii, and fishes on various continents and bodies of water for trout, bonefish, Atlantic Salmon, or whatever may be present and biting.” He traveled “to the Australian rain forest” with Ira Marshall and Otis Booth; “He reads voraciously about everything from dinosaurs, to black holes to psychology.” AUTHOR: “As he grows older and wealthier, Munger still avoids a showy life, but he is willing to accept a little ease.” (See DECISIONS, p. 322, for the flying.) (Damn Right!, p. 322)
- **HIM + REPORTED (Molly) + AUTHOR** — On his children. HIM: “I am quite pleased with all my children in terms of morality, behavior, and such,” but he is less certain how to make them “hunger to work hard and become even richer than he has made them” (AUTHOR's phrase); HIM: “I've had kids in both moderate and immoderate circumstances,” “and to be honest, my children that were raised when we had less money have worked harder.” REPORTED (Molly), of his gruffness: “It's very much an act, it's self-parody, it's a joke on himself,” “You know people who are stiff, ponderous. He's not. He doesn't expect you to believe it. He's utilizing that particular characteristic. He has a huge range. This was the one that suited him.” AUTHOR (p. 324): “Munger swells with pride when telling of Charles, Jr.'s work in science education or his wife Mandy's election to her local school board.” REPORTED (Molly): “He thinks I'm an ultra liberal, but part of that is for effect,” “He likes to play the curmudgeon, but I don't think he thinks I'm a crazy person.” AUTHOR: Charlie “is famous for carrying a book and reading, no matter how wonderful the surrounding scenery might be”; so is Emilie, who did not notice food burning in the oven and, reading on the floor of an airport shop, missed her plane and was locked in. (Damn Right!, pp. 323–324)
- **HIM + AUTHOR** — Not wanting to be remembered for paper. AUTHOR: “Munger has said that he and Buffett don't want to go down in history as shrewd, miserable accumulators.” HIM: “We didn't want to be remembered by friends and family for nothing but pieces of paper.” AUTHOR: “And so, Charlie has decided it's all right to be whimsical once in a while.” (See DECISIONS, the Channel Cat.) (Damn Right!, p. 324)
- **REPORTED (Williams) + AUTHOR** — Working with him. Williams: “Charlie used to ask me a question, and I'd shoot back an answer,” “A couple of days later I'd think, ‘why did I say that? That's not what he wanted to know.’ Now I think carefully before answering him. I like to say that now I'm up to just one day behind him.” AUTHOR (p. 327): “Munger often called Williams on the telephone and the minute he had said what he needed to say, Munger would abruptly hang up. It has never been his habit to indulge in small talk. Williams learned not to be surprised or to take offense.” Williams on the drawings: “I cherish those drawings,” AUTHOR (p. 325): Munger found “an unlikely ally, King Williams III, a deep sea diver whose hobby is hang gliding off the Santa Barbara cliffs”; “Munger does not intimidate him either”. (Damn Right!, pp. 325–327)
- **AUTHOR + HIM + REPORTED (Williams)** — The car in the marina lot. After the boat was finished, a car backed out and shot toward them; Williams yelled and the driver stopped before hitting Charlie. HIM: “You probably saved my life.” Williams: “Charlie, I'd never let anything happen to you.” HIM: “Then you're walking on my wrong side,” AUTHOR: “referring to his blind left eye.” (Damn Right!, p. 330)
- **REPORTED (Molly) + AUTHOR** — Emotions. Molly: “He is very distrustful of emotions.” “A good argument can be made that he's a very emotional person-that it is an achievement that he brought a large psyche under control. A unique mix of an (emotional) personality under wraps.” AUTHOR: “Yet Molly says there always were signs that her father could be frivolous.” (Damn Right!, pp. 330–331)
- **AUTHOR + HIM** — Succession and death, the Postscript. AUTHOR: “Munger deflected questions of corporate succession in the standard midwestern way, with humor.” HIM: “They asked George Burns when he was 95, ‘What does your doctor say about you smoking these big, black cigars?’ And he said, ‘My doctor's dead.’” HIM: “In due course this corporation will have a change in management,” “There is not a way to fix that.” AUTHOR: “Both men say, however, that there is a successor (or possibly two, one for operations and one for investments) who will be revealed at the appropriate time.” HIM: “The one place a death will hurt is we're not likely to get as good an allocator of capital as Warren in the next CEO, whoever that is. But it still will be one hell of a business.” (The .txt ends that quotation with garbled punctuation.) AUTHOR: Munger said he and Buffett aren't “obsessing” about their successors yet. HIM: “Fortunately, Warren plans to live almost indefinitely.” Asked who is the next Charlie Munger: “There's not much demand,” AUTHOR: “Unable to resist a ghoulish joke, Munger said that when he dies people will ask, ‘How much did he leave?’ The answer will be, ‘He left it all.’” (Damn Right!, p. 331)
- **HIM** — How he talks to an audience of his own classmates (Appendix D). “(Yes, I am suggesting today that mighty Harvard would do better if it thought more about pilot training.)” (p. 347) Of his own education: “sometimes making me like the only one without a blindfold in a high-stake game of ‘pin the donkey.’” (p. 354) “What is most interesting to me in my answers is that, while everything I have said is non-original and has long been obvious to the point of banality to many sound and well educated minds, all the evils I decry remain grossly overpresent in the best of our soft-science educational domains” (pp. 354–355). “If I could somewhat fix my many gross deficiencies, so can they.” (p. 355) “It is, of course, normal for self-appraisal to be more positive than external appraisal. Indeed, a problem of this sort may have given you your speaker today.” (p. 365) On Chicago abolishing its psychology department: “perhaps with an undisclosed hope of later creating a better version.” (p. 365) And to the Coca-Cola audience: “Of course, those of you with 50 percent of net worth in Coca-Cola stock, occurring because you tried to so invest 10 percent after thinking like I did in making my pitch to Glotz, can ignore my message about psychology as too elementary for useful transmission to you. But I am not so sure that this reaction is wise for the rest of you.” (p. 367) And of the Harvard Business School test: “Ah, my fellow members of the Harvard Law Class of 1948: if only we had been much more often tested like that, just think of what more we might have accomplished!” (p. 352) (Damn Right!, pp. 347, 352, 354–355, 365, 367) Also in: HIM, Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 323, 328, 336–337 (Talk Five). The Almanack also gives the opening: “Today i am going to engage in a game reminding us of our old professors: Socratic solitaire.” (p. 323; the .txt prints “i”).
- **HIM (Rebuttal, on credit)** — On his role with Buffett. “I think there's some mythology in the idea that I've been this great enlightener of Warren. He hasn't needed much enlightenment. I frankly think I got more credit than I deserve. It is true that Warren had a touch of brain block from working under Ben Graham and making it ton of money. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 3)
- **HIM (Rebuttal, on Buffett's successor)** — “I think the top guy won't be as smart as Warren. - **AUTHOR** — The Depression. “Charlie's observations of the plight of those less fortunate made lasting impressions. He saw hobos roaming the streets looking for handouts and others who were willing to sweep a driveway or porch in exchange for a sandwich.” And the family: “Charlie learned that, by supporting each other, the Mungers weathered the worst economic collapse in the nation's history.” (Grandfather the judge exchanged “$35,000 in sound first mortgages for the bank's weak loans” to keep Uncle Tom's bank open after the bank holiday: “The judge eventually recovered most of his investment, but not until a great many years later.”) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 12–13)
- **AUTHOR** — At Harvard. “Charlie had a tendency to be abrupt, which was often interpreted as rudeness. Actually, Charlie was just in a hurry and customary pleasantries of the classroom were of little concern to him.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 15)
- **REPORTED (a friend, as the author gives it)** — Teddy's death. “A friend remembers that Charlie would visit his dying son in the hospital and then walk the streets of Pasadena crying.” (Also in: Damn Right!, p. 73 for his own words after Teddy's death.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 16)
- **REPORTED (Buffett)** — First meeting, 1959. “Sometime during the evening, when Charlie started rolling on the floor laughing at his own jokes, I knew I had met a kindred spirit.” “I've been known to roll on the floor laughing at my own stuff, too, and to try to dominate the conversation. Charlie was a little more successful at that than I've ever been, but he's been great to study under.” Also in: Damn Right!, p. 19. (Buffett's account of his own reaction is recorded here only as a report of what Munger did.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 19–20)
- **REPORTED (Buffett)** — The one word. “one time, some attractive woman sat next to Charlie and asked him what he owed his success to, and, unfortunately, she insisted on a one-word answer. He had a speech prepared that would have gone on for several hours. But, when forced to boil it down to one word, he said that he was ‘rational.’” Buffett: “He doesn't always apply it elsewhere, but he applies it in business.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 20)
- **REPORTED (Buffett)** — “there is honesty and integrity, and always doing more than his share and not complaining about what the other person does. We've been associated for forty years, and he's never second-guessed anything I've done. We've never had an argument. We've disagreed on things, but he's a perfect partner.” (Buffett on Munger's conduct.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 21)
- **REPORTED (Susie Buffett)** — The first night. “It was amazing to me to see Warren get quieter and let Charlie take the lead. I had never seen that before. Warren always took that role, and I'd never seen anybody take that away from him, and he relinquished it to Charlie that night.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 22)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — Enthusiasm for his own subject. “I had long believed that I had a social defect in my tendency to gain enthusiasm in the course of telling others what I thought they should know. After all, I often caused antipathy” and “I could partially counteract only through being generous to an extent that was sometimes irksome.” (the .txt breaks the sentence after “antipathy”.) On Cicero's view: “Happily, the great Cicero described this trait as a helpful virtue, easing the process of instructing the world.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 32–33)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — Ambition at Omaha Central High. “Cicero's talk would have done me no good at Omaha Central High, where my main ambition was to make a better impression on Shirley Smails.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 32)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — Lost in thought. “As the years have passed, I have encountered more and more criticism from being lost in my own thoughts when others were talking to me. This behavior, too, is deemed a virtue by Cicero.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 33)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — The gift of the book. “Notwithstanding the McBains' questionable social tact in giving an old man a book on aging, their whole judgment was perfect.” On finding no Cicero in school: “I practically went into orbit upon seeing a work by Cicero that I could not recall ever having heard of, full of praise for old age.” (age 82.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 29–32)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — The Glotz joke on old age. “an old friend of mine, here called ‘Glotz.’ Just before he died at age 75, he said to me: ‘I used to be troubled by unwelcome, lustful thoughts about my friends' wives, but, by God, I finally licked it.’” And on Cicero preferring pleading to pleasure: “Surely the rest of us, when still in our early sixties, will not very often prefer litigation as Cicero does.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 38)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — On Cicero and the Senate. “Ah, those were the days! Given such Roman power, would not any wise person now approach the U.S. Senate with delight? And perhaps also publish a list of Senators under review?” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 34)
- **HIM (‘Praising Old Age’)** — On Buffett in old age. “Warren Buffett, as a sort of modern baton carrier for a Ciceronian point of view, is now doing a good job in intimating Cicero and Franklin in old age. Not only does Buffett, much like Cato, stay in the arena with no plan to ever leave. He does so joyously and while providing good results for those who will trust him patiently.” (the .txt prints ‘intimating’; read as ‘imitating’.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 40–41)
- **REPORTED (Charles Munger Jr.)** — The tank of gas. “Charlie, when you borrow a man's car, you always return it with a full tank of gas.” Said on a ski trip, after a ten-minute detour to fill a half-full tank, in a jeep the son later learned belonged to Rick Guerin, who would not have noticed. “My dad still didn't skip a point of fairness and consideration. So I was taught that day not only how to get a good friend, but also how to keep one.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 43)
- **REPORTED (Wendy Munger)** — Dinner-table tales. “His favorite educational tools were the Morality Tale, in which someone faced an ethical problem and chose the correct path, and the Downward Spiral Tale, in which someone made the wrong choice and suffered an inevitable series of catastrophic personal and professional losses.” “His specialty was the Downward Spiral Tale.” The story he told: a financial officer lost hundreds of thousands of dollars, told the president at once, and the president answered: “This was a terrible mistake, and we don't want you ever to make another one like it. But people make mistakes, and we can forgive that. You did the right thing, which was to admit your mistake. If you had tried to hide the mistake, or cover it up for even a short time, you would be out of this company. As it is, we'd like you to stay.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 43–44)
- **REPORTED (Hal Borthwick)** — The paper and the keys. The newspaper forgotten in a storm: “Go back and get the paper and never forget it again!” Charlie's mother's car keys dropped in the lake: “Go out with your friends and keep diving till you get those keys, and don't come home without them.” (Both after “a one-second or so discussion”.) Of Charlie in those years: “in those days when Charlie worked so hard and so long, that was the only meaningful time we spent with him.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 45–46)
- **REPORTED (David Borthwick)** — The tennis machine. He wanted a ball machine for the Minnesota court, “For it was Father who was out on court more than anyone, with the machine positioned so he could endlessly practice-volley close by the net.” “By working on the tennis version of golf's short game, which few others could be bothered to practice, Father, as he's done throughout his life, gave himself a fair if maddening competitive advantage.” “Father's ability to Chinese wall off the most intrusive distractions from whatever mental task he was engaged in-a practice alternately amusing and irritating if you were trying to get his attention -accounts as much as anything else for his success.” (David's words.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 46–47)
- **REPORTED (Molly Munger)** — Raising skeptics. “Daddy raised us to be skeptical, even contrarian.” The adjectives: “‘Crazy,’ ‘maladjusted,’ ‘pompous,’ ‘self-satisfied’-we knew from his adjectives what he thought we should avoid.” The aquaplane behind the boat: “He would make sharp turns to see if we could hold on, and the only way to avoid the disgrace of a fall was to keep shifting weight to compensate for the extreme angles.” In 1966 she was sent “the allowance of a much richer father,” keeping her “in professionally ironed shirts.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 47–48)
- **REPORTED (Emilie Ogden)** — Books. “if you play Password and give the clue ‘Charlie Munger hands,’ anyone will first answer, ‘books.’ No matter where he is, his hands are always holding open a volume, typically a Ben Franklin biography, or the latest treatise on genetics.” Also “graph paper, for the buildings he's been designing.” On stage: “His hands are crossed in front of him, as he shakes his head saying, ‘I have nothing to add.’” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 50–51)
- **REPORTED (Barry Munger)** — Messages. At bridge, “If he doesn't like the way his bridge partner plays out a hand, for example, he might say, ‘You played that like a plumber,’” but to a child “he is more likely to couch the message in an anecdote, preferably delivered in a group setting so that no one is singled out.” Note on a shareholder's letter, 1996: “I hope you find this amusing,” “If only I had the influence with my wife and children that I have in some other quarters!” When Barry sent him a book, it came back; “he had simply instructed his secretary to send copies to the whole family.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 52–54)
- **REPORTED (Philip Munger)** — Clothes. “He once said that he was nonconformist enough in his behavior and opinions that it made sense to chart a very straight course in attire. His going along with normal social customs and his sense of humor, he said. were what allowed his otherwise sometimes prickly temperament to harmonize with other people.” Of pleated trousers bought with the Christmas gift card: “Do you want to look like a jazz drummer?” Of a Marks and Spencer coat: “This will always keep its crease.” “He admired both stores because they were durable institutions and because their merchandise was, too, and fairly priced.” Wing-tip shoes for the summer job at the Daily Journal: “a coming of age ceremony required by Father for each boy.” (The .txt prints “Marla and Spencer” and “review” for “view”; read as Marks and Spencer.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 54–55)
- **HIM (epigraph)** — On himself. “We all have our little quirks-my family and friends tell me I'm sometimes absent minded and opinionated. Maybe they're right.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 56)
- **REPORTED (Roy Tolles; Glen Mitchell)** — Tolles: “Charlie has a desire to understand exactly what makes things happen. He wants to get to the bottom of everything, whether it's something of serious interest to him or not.” Mitchell: “People will come into the room and pat him on the back or offer him another cup of coffee or something, and he won't even acknowledge their presence because he is using one hundred percent of his huge intellect.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 56)
- **REPORTED (Bob Bird)** — The elevator. After a satisfactory meeting with CenFed's CEO Tad Lowrey on the Wesco savings and loan: “Just as we got there, the elevator door opened, and Charlie walked directly inside. He never said goodbye, never shook hands, nothing. Tad and I were left standing there, smiling and speechless.” Bird: “Charlie can put on the charm if he puts his mind to it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 56–57)
- **REPORTED (Buffett)** — The taxi and the lights. After a Salomon board meeting: “I had been talking to myself for some time. I looked around for Charlie, only to see him climbing into the back of a taxicab, headed off to the airport. No goodbye, no nothing.” And: “People think it's Charlie's eyes that cause him to miss seeing things ... BUT IT'S NOT HIS EYES, IT'S HIS HEAD! I once sat through three sets of traffic lights, and plenty of honking behind us, as Charlie discussed some complex problem at an intersection.” (The second passage follows Buffett's heading in the layout; the .txt prints it as a new quoted paragraph.) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 57)
- **REPORTED (Dick Esbenshade)** — The eminent dead at the law firm. - **REPORTED (Howard Buffett)** — “I remember not saying much, being scared to ask a question and appearing stupid. He is so darned smart, like my father, in the stratosphere.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 58)
- **REPORTED (Gates)** — Talking with him. Buffett warned Gates “not to expect to get a word in edgewise when talking to Charlie because even at a cocktail party Charlie would hold his hand up to prevent others from starting to speak while he took a drink.” Gates on the correspondence: “Our most memorable correspondence was about stock options and their power to distort business results. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 58–59)

(Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 100)
- **HIM** — Declining to add on moats. At the annual meeting Buffett described the moat and then asked “Charlie?”; Munger: “How could you say it better?” Buffett: “Sure. Have some peanut brittle on that one” (Buffett's words recorded only as the cue). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 73)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — On the myth that he enlightened Buffett. “I think those authors give me more credit than I deserve.
