- **REPORTED (Buffett, in the Tilson notes) and HIM** — The abominable no-man. Buffett: “You just have to learn how to calibrate his answers. If you ask Charlie something and he says "no," then we put all of our money in it. [f he says "that's the stupidest thing I've ever heard," then we make a more moderate investment.” HIM, at a shareholders' meeting, after Buffett asked if he had additional comment: “No. I think you said ho' perfectly.” (the .txt prints “ho'”). Also in: Damn Right!, p. 113. (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 106)
- **REPORTED (Chuck Rickershauser)** — Asking for a percentage. “Warren told me a story once that, back in the early days of their relationship, when they had no financial connection and each had an investment partnership, Warren would frequently call up Charlie and say, 'I'm thinking of doing something' and describe it, and Charlie would say, 'my God, are you kidding? There's this risk and that risk.' They'd go right through all these risks that Charlie saw and Warren would usually say, 'I think you're right.' But once in a while, he would say, 'Charlie, I've heard everything you said, but I think I'm going to go ahead.' Warren said that it wasn't until that instant that he'd learn what Charlie really thought because occasionally Charlie would respond, 'Warren, if you do it, could I have a percentage of it?' People often think of Charlie as identifying risks and saying, 'No,' but it was his ability to identify the times to ask for a percentage that was most valuable.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 106–107)
- **REPORTED (Otis Booth)** — Saying no ninety percent of the time. “Charlie realizes that it is difficult to find something is really good. So, if you say 'No' ninety percent of the time, you're not missing much in the World.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 107)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — On his own error pattern. “We rub our noses in mistakes of omission-as we just did.” “We are apparently slow learners.” “We fret way earlier than other people. We've left a lot of money on the table through early fretting. It's the way we are-you'll just have to live with it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 111, 128)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Apology and credit. “I want to make an apology. This wasn't my witticism; it was used long ago] to describe the robber barons.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 118)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — On the mutual fund scandal. “It was as if a man came up and said, "Why don't we kill your mother, and we'll split the insurance money?" - **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Worn out on stock options. “I'm so tired of this subject. I've been on this topic for so long. It's such a rotten way to run a civilization to make the accounting wrong. It's like getting the engineering wrong when making a bridge. When perfectly reputable people say options shouldn't be expensed, it's outrageous.” “These people [the eighty-eight senators who voted to maintain the status quo of not expensing stock options] are stupid and dishonorable. They know, it was wrong and did it anyway.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 127)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — On Frank Partnoy's book. “You would be disgusted if you had a fair mind and spent a month really delving into a big derivative operation. They make the worst economics professors look like gods. Moreover, there is depravity augmenting the folly.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 132)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Not envying others' tech gains. “It doesn't bother us at all [that others are making money in the tech sector].” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 140)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — On giving and being asked. “I would hate to have people ask me for money all day long. Warren couldn't stand it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 142)
- **HIM (Tilson's notes)** — Humor (pp. 143–145). On a cocktail-party question whether he played the piano: “I don't know I have never tried” (the .txt prints it as a reported-speech sentence). “I always like it when someone attractive to me agrees with me so I have fond memories of Philfisher” (the .txt run-on). “In the corporate world if you have analysts, due diligence and no horse sense you have just described hell” (p. 144). “It's been so long since we've bought anything that [asking us about our market impact when we're trading] is like asking Rip Van Winkle about the past twenty years.” (p. 144) “I think the people who are attracted to be prison guards are not nature's noblemen to begin with.” (p. 145) “You don't want to be like the motion picture executive who had many people at his funeral, but they were there just to make sure he was dead.” (p. 145) “It was predictable that the year 2000 would come.” (p. 145; to a 1999 Berkshire meeting question on the Y2K issue). - **REPORTED (Lou Simpson)** — Munger under stress. “I can attest that Charlie has a combination of characteristics that I have never seen in any other single individual. - **REPORTED (Jim Sinegal)** — Lunch at the California Club. “despite our being interrupted continuously by the other 500-plus people dining there that day, who all felt compelled to say hello to Charlie and whom Charlie identified by name in almost every instance, we managed to have a great conversation.” “I liked him from the moment we met when he told me how much he enjoyed shopping at Costco;” “Later when he called to accept the position on the board, he referred to himself as a septuagenarian.” (pp. 147–148) On Munger as a director: “Since then he has provided everything that can be expected from a director. - **HIM (Harvard School commencement, 1986)** — Opening jokes and a confession. “It behooves the speaker to address two questions in every mind: 1) Why was such a selection made? 2) How long is the speech going to last?” “While not having significant public-speaking experience, I do hold a black belt in chutzpah,” “I am much older than Carson was when he spoke and have failed and been miserable more often and in more ways than was possible for a charming humorist speaking at a younger age.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 150–151)
- **HIM (Harvard School commencement, 1986)** — Alcohol and his friends. “The four closest friends of my youth were highly intelligent, ethical, humorous types, favored in person and background. Two are long dead, with alcohol a contributing factor, and a third is a living alcoholic-if you call that living.” “I have yet to meet anyone, in over six decades of life, whose life was worsened by fear and avoidance of such a deceptive pathway to destruction.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 152)
- **HIM (Harvard School commencement, 1986)** — Resentment, and Disraeli's drawer. “Resentment has always worked for me exactly as it worked for Carson. I cannot recommend it highly enough to you if you desire misery.” Disraeli, who “learned to give up vengeance as a motivation for action, but he did retain some outlet for resentment by putting the names of people who wronged him on pieces of paper in a drawer. Then, from time to time, he reviewed these names and took pleasure in noting the way the world had taken his enemies down without his assistance.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 153)
- **HIM (Harvard School commencement, 1986)** — McDonald's and elite campuses. “Indeed, I have often made myself unpopular on elite college campuses pushing this reliability theme. What I say is that McDonald's is one of our most admirable institutions. - **HIM (USC talk, 1994)** — Late to psychology; still makes errors. “And nobody taught it to me, by the way. I had to learn it later in life, one piece at a time. And it was fairly laborious. - **HIM (USC talk, 1994)** — On what he does not know. “Here's a model that we've had trouble with. - **HIM** — Taking losses; admitting the mistake. “I'm good at taking losses. I can take 'em psychologically. And, besides, I have very few.” (p. 266) On USAir: “But it was a mistake.” (p. 259) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 259, 266)
- **HIM** — Growing old for the first time. “I sometimes tell my friends, I'm doing the best I can. But i have never grown old before. I'm doing it for the first time. - **HIM** — Learning modern Darwinism in his seventies. “I was miseducated horribly. And I hadn't bothered to pick up what's called modern Darwinism. I do a lot of miscellaneous reading, too. And with the aid of Dawkins-Oxford's great biologist-and others, I picked it up” (p. 287) “Well, it was an absolute circus for me in my seventies to get the modern Darwinian synthesis in my head. - **HIM** — Pressed by a student on how to know which models are right. “No, no. You overestimate the difficulty. - **HIM** — What he finds funny: the fish man, the undertaker, the doctors. “Now when Carnation tried to make a deal for its trademark, there was this one guy who sold Carnation Fish. And they trusted their wives.” (p. 283, of two doctors who divorced and put property in their wives' names when malpractice premiums rose) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 233–234, 280, 283)
- **HIM** — Leisure and gambling among friends. “I've always taken a fair amount of time to do what I really wanted to do some of which was merely to fish or play bridge or play golf.” (p. 294) “I do like the manly art of wagering, so to speak. And I like light social gambling among friends.” (p. 264) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 264, 294)
- **HIM** — What he is passionate about, and his own talk. “Yeah, I'm passionate about wisdom. I'm passionate about accuracy and some kinds of curiosity. Perhaps I have some streak of generosity in my nature and a desire to serve values that transcend my brief life. But maybe I'm just here to show off. Who knows?” (p. 296) “Now this has been a very peculiar talk for some businessman to come in and give at a law school-some guy who's never taken a course in psychology telling you that all of the psychology textbooks are wrong. This is very eccentric. But all I can tell you is that I'm sincere.” (p. 295) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 295–296)
- **HIM** — His father and McFadyen: still trying. “I've been trying to imitate Grant McFadyen ever since-for all my life. I may have had a few lapses. But at least I've been trying.” (p. 276) “I owe a lot to these long-dead predecessors” (p. 298) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 276, 298)
- **HIM** — Archimedes and Mark Twain. “The grain of my emotional nature is to respond as Archimedes might respond if he complained now to God: ‘How could you put in those dark ages after I published my formulas?’ Or as Mark Twain once complained: ‘These are sad days in literature. Homer is dead. Shakespeare is dead. And I myself am not feeling at all well.’” “Fortunately, I am still able to refrain from complaint in the mode of Mark Twain.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 297)
- **HIM** — Whom he admires in the talk. On Graham: “great though he was as a man” (p. 268). On Jack Welch: “He's a star businessman. He's a marvelous guy.” (p. 271) On the University of Chicago president who abolished the psychology department: “And I must admit that I admire a college president who will do something like that.” (p. 293) On Salomon's ex-general counsel: “brilliant and generous” (p. 279). (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 268, 271, 279, 293)
- **REPORTED (the editors, “as suggested by Charlie”)** — Reception of Talk Three. “Most people don't understand this talk. Charlie says it was an extreme communication failure when made, and people have since found it difficult to understand even when read slowly, twice.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 299)
- **HIM (Talk Four Revisited, 2006)** — Admitting he failed as a teacher. “As matters worked out, my 1996 talk failed to get through to almost all people hearing it. Then later, between 7996 and 2006, even when the talk's written version was slowly read twice by very intelligent people who admired me, its message likewise failed.” (pp. 320–321; the .txt prints “7996”) “On the other hand, no one said to me that the talk was wrong. Instead, people were puzzled briefly, then moved on.” (p. 321) “Thus my failure as a communicator was even more extreme than the cognitive failure I was trying to explain. Why?” “The best explanation, I now think, is that I displayed gross folly as an amateur teacher. I attempted too much.” “Yet I chose as my title, ‘Practical Thought about Practical Thought?’ This was a start into tough territory.” “Thus, for most hearers, I did the rough equivalent of trying to explain some hard-to-comprehend ideas by simply defining those ideas as equivalent to themselves.” (p. 321) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 320–321)
- **HIM (Talk Five Revisited, 2006)** — Not changing a word. “As I review Talk Five in 2006, I would not change a word. And I continue to believe that my ideas are important. In my attitude I may be displaying too much similarity to my long-dead relative, Reverend Theodore Munger, former chaplain of Yale.” His account of the relative: “Theodore published a collection of his sermons, laying out proper conduct with a strong, excathedra tone.” “Then, later in life,he published a final edition,reporting in his foreword that he had made no changes at all and was now producing the new edition only because the extreme popularity of his sermons had caused excessive wear in the original printing plates.” (the .txt prints “life,he” and “edition,reporting”) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 344)
- **REPORTED (Dick Esbenshade, friend and business associate since 1956; the editors print it on p. 345)** — How he decides. “Charlie draws his confidence from the unusually rigorous process he follows to research, analyze, evaluate and decide. He knows he may not always be right, but that the odds are in his favor because his process is so disciplined and realistic. For this reason, he is never reluctant to make a decision and to act decisively upon it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 345)
- **AUTHOR (the editors), quoting him, source not given** — On being disliked. The editors say the 14 October 1998 foundation speech “helps account for Charlie's line: ‘It's sad, but true: Not everybody loves me.’” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 346)
- **HIM (Talk Six)** — How he opens an unwelcome talk. “I am speaking here today because my friend, John Argue, asked me.” “And John well knew that I, u,ho, unlike many other speakers on your agenda, having nothing to sell any of you, would be irreverent about much current investment practice in large institutions, including charitable foundations.” (the .txt prints “u,ho”) “Therefore any hostility my talk will cause should be directed at John Argue, who comes from the legal profession and may even enjoy it.” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 347)
- **REPORTED (Jody Curtis of Foundation & Commentary, as the editors give it)** — How he came across at the Philanthropy Roundtable, November 2000. The editors write that, “Startling Charlie's family and friends,” Curtis “characterized Charlie as ‘a friendly old uncle, one with a jolly sense of humor at that.’” (Poor Charlie's Almanack, p. 364)
- **HIM (Talk Seven, and Talk Seven Revisited)** — Amateur, with a sense of how it will land. “Let me, the ultimate amateur, boldly try to do a little better, or at least a little differently.” (p. 368) At the end of the passage on the mutual fund study, as printed (the paragraph is a garbled run-on): “this may be my last talk to charitable foundations” (p. 373) “Well, this is enough uncredentialed musing for one breakfast meeting.” (p. 375) And six years later: “But no one trained in economics has ever tried to engage with me on this subject.” “Undeterred by this apathy, I am now going to push my reasoning one notch further by laying out a ‘thought experiment’ extrapolating the combined reasoning of Talks Six and Seven to an assumed higher level of investment costs.” (p. 378) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 368–378)
- **HIM (Talk Eight, fiction)** — What he finds funny in the scandal. “And now nearly everyone said this stood for ‘Financial Accounts Still Bogus.’” (p. 396) “So great was the disappointment with conventional economists that Harvard's John Kenneth Galbraith received the Nobel Prize in economics.” (p. 397) “The butcher says, ‘The reputation of lawyers has fallen dramatically,’ and the checkout clerk replies, ‘How do you fall dramatically off a pancake?’” (p. 397) (Poor Charlie's Almanack, pp. 396–397)

Covered: Damn Right!, pages 1–100, 101–200, 201–300, 301–400, 401–415; Poor Charlie's Almanack, pages 1–100, 101–200, 201–300, 301–400
